Lie Algebras
Kevin McGerty

*Background

From group actions to group representations

In mathematics, group actions give a way of encoding the symmetries of a space or physical system. Formally these are defined as follows: an action of a group \(G\) on a space \(X\) is a map \(a \colon G \times X \to X\), written \((g, x) \mapsto a(g,x)\) or more commonly \((g, x) \mapsto g.x\) which satisfies the properties

  1. \(e.x = x\), for all \(x \in X\), where \(e \in G\) is the identity;
  2. \((g_1 g_2).x = g_1.(g_2.x)\) for all \(g_1, g_2 \in G\) and \(x \in X\).

Natural examples of group actions are that of the general linear group \(\GL_n(\RR)\) on \(\RR^n\), or the action of the group of rigid motions \(\SO_3\) on \(S^2\), the unit sphere \(\{x \in \RR^3 : \|x\| = 1\}\) in \(\RR^3\).

Whenever a group acts on a space \(X\), there is a resulting linear action (a representation) on the vector space of functions on \(X\). Indeed if \(\operatorname{Fun}(X)\) denotes the vector space of real-valued functions on \(X\), then the formula \[g(f)(x) = f(g^{-1}.x), \qquad \forall g \in G,\; f \in \operatorname{Fun}(X),\; x \in X,\] defines a representation of \(G\) on \(\operatorname{Fun}(X)\). If \(X\) and \(G\) have more structure, e.g. that of a topological space or smooth manifold, then this action may also preserve the subspaces of say continuous, or differentiable functions.

Remark.

If one defines the action of \(G\) on \(\operatorname{Fun}(X)\) by the more natural-looking formula \(g(f)(x) = f(g.x)\) then one finds that \[g_1(g_2(f))(x) = g_2(f)(g_1.x) = f((g_2 g_1).x) = ((g_2 g_1).f)(x), \qquad \forall x \in X.\] In other words, the resulting action on \(\operatorname{Fun}(X)\) is a right-action. Since inversion \(\iota(g) = g^{-1}\) gives an isomorphism of groups \(\iota \colon G \to G^{\mathrm{op}}\) (where \(G^{\mathrm{op}}\) is the group obtained by using the binary operation \(g_1 \star g_2 = g_2 g_1\)), by precomposing with \(\iota\) we may turn a right action into a left action, and in this course our convention will be to do this where necessary to ensure all actions are left actions unless the contrary is explicitly stated.

Infinitesimal symmetries

In this section I use some material, like multivariable analysis, which is not necessary for the main body of the course, but if you know it, or are happy to rely on notions from Prelims multivariable calculus for which you have not been given a rigorous definition, it will help to put the material of this course in a broader context. For those worried about such things, fear not, it is non-examinable.

Lie algebras arise as the "infinitesimal version" of group actions, which loosely speaking means they are what we get by trying to differentiate group actions. When working in \(\RR^n\) we will denote the partial derivative of \(f\) in the direction of the \(i\)-th standard basis vector by \(\partial_i f\) (in preference to the notation \(\partial f / \partial x_i\) you may have seen more often).

In order for a group \(G\) to have a Lie algebra associated to it, it must have additional structure -- for example when a group is also a metric space in a natural way, and the group operations are continuous functions, then it is an example of a topological group. To possess a Lie algebra, it must make sense to talk about differentiable functions on \(G\), not just continuous functions. In general this requires developing the theory of manifolds, but some important examples already arose in the Prelims Geometry course. The orthogonal group \(\mathrm{O}_n\) of linear isometries of an \(n\)-dimensional real inner product space, is naturally identified with the subset \(\{A \in \Mat_n(\RR) : A \cdot A^\top = I_n\}\) of \(n \times n\) matrices whose columns form an orthonormal basis of \(\RR^n\). As such, a smooth function on \(\mathrm{O}_n\) is just the restriction of a smooth function on \(\Mat_n(\RR)\) (an \(n^2\)-dimensional real vector space). Equally, a smooth curve on \(\mathrm{O}_n\) is just a smooth curve in \(\Mat_n(\RR)\) whose image is contained in \(\mathrm{O}_n\).

The action of \(\mathrm{O}_n\) on \(\RR^n\) is linear and hence certainly smooth. To obtain a "derivative" of the group action, we can consider smooth curves \(\gamma \colon (-1,1) \to G\) which pass through the identity \(I_n\) at \(t = 0\), and then given a function on \(\RR^n\) we can consider the action of the curve \(\gamma\) on a function \(f \colon \RR^n \to \RR\): \[f(x) \mapsto f(\gamma(t)^{-1}.x),\] and, since our curve is smooth, it makes sense to take the derivative at \(t = 0\), that is, we set \[\nu_\gamma(f) = \frac{d}{dt}\bigl(f(\gamma(t)^{-1}.x)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}.\] In this way, we obtain a family of operators on the space of functions on \(\RR^n\). While it appears that the family is indexed by smooth curves through \(I_n \in \mathrm{O}_n\), since we take the derivative at \(t = 0\), the operator \(\nu_\gamma\) only depends on the derivative of \(\gamma\) at \(t = 0\), that is, the velocity of the curve as it passes through the identity element in \(\mathrm{O}_n\).

Note that we only consider curves on \(\mathrm{O}_n\) as they pass through the identity element, rather than at all points on the group. This is because if we measure the infinitesimal action on \(\RR^n\) near a group element \(A \in \mathrm{O}_n\) using a path \(A(t)\) with \(A(0) = A\), then setting \(\gamma(t) = A^{-1}A(t)\) we have, for \(x \in \RR^n\), \[A(t)^{-1}.x = \gamma(t).A^{-1}.x,\] and thus we will simply get the infinitesimal action of the curve \(\gamma\) at the point \(y = A^{-1}x \in \RR^n\). Knowing the infinitesimal action by curves through the identity at all points in \(\RR^n\) thus already captures that given by the velocity of curves as they pass through other elements of the group.

As \(\det(A) = \det(A^\top)\), if \(A \in \mathrm{O}_n\) then \(\det(A)^2 = 1\) and hence \(\det \colon \mathrm{O}_n \to \{\pm 1\}\) is a group homomorphism which is easily seen to be surjective. Its kernel \(\SO_n\), the group of orientation preserving linear isometries of \(\RR^n\), is thus an index 2 subgroup which is the connected component containing the identity element. The infinitesimal action associated to an action of \(\mathrm{O}_n\) will thus only depend on the action of \(\SO_n\).

Example.

When \(n = 2\) the group \(\SO_2\) is just the group of rotations about the origin. The map \[t \mapsto g(t) = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(t) & -\sin(t) \\ \sin(t) & \cos(t) \end{pmatrix}\] is smooth (because each of its components is) and a group homomorphism, that is, \(g(t_1 + t_2) = g(t_1)g(t_2)\). Indeed since \(g(t + 2\pi) = g(t)\) the parametrization given by \(g\) gives us another description of the smooth functions on \(\SO_2\) -- they are simply the smooth functions on \(\RR\) which are periodic with period \(2\pi\).

The action of \(\SO_2\) on \(\RR^2\) induces an infinitesimal action on the smooth functions on \(\RR^2\). Since \(\SO_2\) is 1-dimensional, we need only consider the curve through \(I_2\) given by \(g(t)\). Since \(g(t)\) is a group homomorphism, the operator \(\nu = \nu_g\) defined above becomes in this case \[\nu(f)(x) = \frac{d}{dt}\bigl(g(t)(f)(x)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0} = \frac{d}{dt}\bigl(f(g(-t).x)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}.\] Now \[g(-t).x = \begin{pmatrix} \cos(t) & \sin(t) \\ -\sin(t) & \cos(t) \end{pmatrix} \begin{pmatrix} x_1 \\ x_2 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} x_1 \cos(t) + x_2 \sin(t) \\ -x_1 \sin(t) + x_2 \cos(t) \end{pmatrix}\] hence \[\frac{d}{dt}(g(-t).x) = \begin{pmatrix} -x_1 \sin(t) + x_2 \cos(t) \\ -x_1 \cos(t) - x_2 \sin(t) \end{pmatrix} \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{d}{dt}(g(-t).x)\Big|_{t=0} = \begin{pmatrix} x_2 \\ -x_1 \end{pmatrix}.\] Thus by the chain rule, since \(g(0).x = x\) we have \[\nu(f) = df_x \begin{pmatrix} x_2 \\ -x_1 \end{pmatrix} = \bigl(\partial_1 f \quad \partial_2 f\bigr) \begin{pmatrix} x_2 \\ -x_1 \end{pmatrix} = x_2 \partial_1 f - x_1 \partial_2 f.\]

Remark 0.0.1.

If we instead write \(g(t) = \exp(it)\) and view \(\RR^2\) as \(\CC\) with \(z = x_1 + ix_2\), then we may also compute \(\nu\) as follows: \[\nu(f) = \frac{d}{dt}\bigl(f(\exp(-it)z)\bigr)\Big|_{t=0} = df_x(-iz) = df_x(x_2 - ix_1) = x_2 \partial_1 f(x) - x_1 \partial_2 f(x).\]

The operator we obtained in this example, \(\nu = x_2 \partial_1 - x_1 \partial_2\), is a \(\mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^2)\)-linear combination of \(\partial_x\) and \(\partial_y\). Operators of this form encode "infinitesimal symmetries". The next definition formalises its key properties.

Definition.

For any positive integer \(n\), an \(\RR\)-linear operator \(\nu \colon \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n) \to \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) is said to be a derivation if, for any \(f_1, f_2 \in \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) it satisfies \[\nu(f_1 \cdot f_2) = \nu(f_1) \cdot f_2 + f_1 \cdot \nu(f_2). \tag{\dagger}\]

The next Lemma (which follows readily from a version of Taylor's theorem for functions on \(\RR^n\) for example) shows that the previous, somewhat formal, definition, actually results in a class of objects with a very concrete description.

Lemma.

If \(\nu \colon \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n) \to \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) is a derivation, then there exist unique smooth functions \(a_1, \ldots, a_n \in \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) such that \(\nu = \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_j \partial_j\), that is, for all \(f \in \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) we have \[\nu(f) = \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_j \partial_j(f).\]

Proof.

(Sketch.) Let \(\nu\) be a derivation. Since \(1^2 = 1 \in \RR\), we must have \(\nu(1) = \nu(1) \cdot 1 + 1 \cdot \nu(1)\), that is, \(2\nu(1) = \nu(1)\) so that \(\nu(1) = 0\), and hence by linearity \(\nu(\lambda) = 0\) for all \(\lambda \in \RR\), i.e. any derivation vanishes on the subspace of constant functions \(\RR \subseteq \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\).

The characterisation of derivations then follows from the fact that for any \(c \in \RR^n\), the maximal ideal \(\mathfrak{m}_c := \{f \in \mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n) : f(c) = 0\}\) of the ring \(\mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\) is generated by \(\{(x_i - c_i) : 1 \leq i \leq n\}\).

□

Thus to give a derivation is the same as to give an \(n\)-tuple of functions \((a_1, \ldots, a_n)\), or in other words a smooth function \(a \colon \RR^n \to \RR^n\).

Definition.

A vector field on \(X = \RR^n\) is a (smooth) function \(\nu \colon \RR^n \to \RR^n\). Let \(\Theta_{\RR^n}\) denote the vector space of all vector fields on \(\RR^n\). To any vector field \(\eta = (a_i)_{i=1}^{n}\) one can associate the derivation \(\theta_\eta = \sum_{j=1}^{n} a_j \partial_j\) which one can think of as giving the infinitesimal direction of a flow (e.g. of a fluid, or an electric field say) and the previous Lemma shows that this gives an equivalence between the space of vector fields and the space of derivations of \(\mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^n)\).

Note that if we compose two derivations \(\nu_1 \circ \nu_2\) we again get an operator on functions, but it is not given by a vector field, since the presence of second-order derivatives prevents it from satisfying \((\dagger)\). However, it is easy to check that if \(\nu_1, \nu_2\) are derivations, then the commutator \([\nu_1, \nu_2] = \nu_1 \circ \nu_2 - \nu_2 \circ \nu_1\) does obey the identity \((\dagger)\) and hence is again a derivation. Thus the space of vector fields \(\Theta_{\RR^n}\) inherits a nonassociative product given by the commutator of the corresponding derivations. Explicitly, if \(\eta_1 = (a_i)_{i=1}^{n}\), \(\eta_2 = (b_i)_{i=1}^{n} \in \Theta_{\RR^n}\) and \([\theta(\eta_1), \theta(\eta_2)] = \sum_i \bigl(\sum_j (a_j \partial_j b_i - b_j \partial_j a_i)\bigr) \partial_i\), so that \[[\eta_1, \eta_2] = \Bigl(\sum_j (a_j \partial_j b_i - b_j \partial_j a_i)\Bigr)_{i=1}^{n}.\]

The space of derivations equipped with the commutator bracket \([\cdot, \cdot]\) (or equivalently the space of vector fields with the corresponding operation) is an (infinite dimensional) example of a Lie algebra. The derivatives of a group action give subalgebras of the algebra \(\Theta_X\): the fact that the commutator product preserves them is a sort of infinitesimal remnant of the group multiplication.

Heuristically, we think of the infinitesimal version of a group action as the collection of derivations on smooth functions obtained by "differentiating the group action at the identity element". (For the circle the collection of vector fields we get are just the scalar multiples of the vector field \(\nu\), but for actions of larger groups this will yield a larger space of derivations).

Example.

Consider the action of \(\SO_3(\RR)\) on \(\RR^3\). This is the group of orientation-preserving linear isometries of \(\RR^3\). It is well-known that any element \(g \in \SO_3(\RR)\) is a rotation by some angle, say \(\theta\), about an axis \(L\) through the origin. Then there is a smooth path \(\gamma\) in \(\SO_3(\RR)\) from the identity to \(g\) which, for \(t \in [0,1]\), is the rotation by \(t \cdot \theta\) about that axis.

Since \(\gamma\) is smooth and its definition extends to \(t\) in an open interval containing \(t = 0\), it makes sense to associate to it the operator \(\nu_\gamma\) on \(\mathcal{C}^\infty(\RR^3)\) given, just as for the circle, by \(f \mapsto \frac{d}{dt}(f(\gamma(-t)(x))\), so that \(\nu_\gamma(f) = df(-\gamma'(0)x)\). If we pick an orthonormal basis \(\mathcal{F} = (v_1, v_2, v_3)\) of \(\RR^3\) with \(v_1\) spanning the axis of the rotation, then \(\gamma(t)\) has matrix \[A = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & \cos(t) & -\sin(t) \\ 0 & \sin(t) & \cos(t) \end{pmatrix},\] and a calculation almost identical to the case of the circle discussed above shows that, in the coordinates \((z_1, z_2, z_3)\) given by \(\mathcal{F}\), \(\nu_\gamma(f) = z_3 \partial_2 - z_2 \partial_3\). Now \(\partial_i\) is just the directional derivative \(\partial_{v_i}\) and \(z_i(x) = v_i \cdot x\), thus \(\nu_\gamma\) corresponds to the vector field \((v_3 \cdot x)v_2 - (v_2 \cdot x)v_3\), that is, \(-v_1 \wedge x\), where \(\wedge\) denotes the vector product. Thus it follows that the space of all vector fields obtained in this way is the 3-dimensional subspace of \(\Theta_{\RR^3}\) which is spanned by \(\{e_1 \wedge x,\; e_2 \wedge x,\; e_3 \wedge x\}\) or in terms of derivations it is the span of \[\{x_3 \partial_2 - x_2 \partial_3,\quad x_1 \partial_3 - x_3 \partial_1,\quad x_2 \partial_1 - x_1 \partial_2\}.\]

Chapter 1. Lie algebras: Definition and basic notions

1.1 Definitions and Examples

The definition of a Lie algebra is an abstraction of the example of the product on vector fields given. It is purely algebraic, so it makes sense over any field \(\mathbb{k}\). We begin, however, with an even more basic definition:

Definition 1.1.1.

Let \(R\) be a commutative ring. An \(R\)-algebra is a pair \((A, *)\) consisting of an \(R\)-module \(A\) and an \(R\)-bilinear map \(* \colon A \times A \to A\), that is, for all \(a_1, a_2, b_1, b_2 \in A\) and \(r \in R\), the operation \(*\) satisfies: \[(r \cdot a_1 + a_2) * b_1 = r \cdot (a_1 * b_1) + (a_2 * b_1),\] \[a_1 * (r \cdot b_1 + b_2) = r \cdot (a_1 * b_1) + (a_1 * b_2).\] We say that \((A, *)\) is unital (or has a unit) if there is an element \(1_A \in A\) such that \(1_A * a = a * 1_A = a\) for all \(a \in A\). Note that if it exists, the multiplicative unit is unique. We say that \((A, *)\) is associative if \(a * (b * c) = (a * b) * c\) for all \(a, b, c \in A\). When \(A\) is associative, we will normally suppress the operation \(*\) and so, for any \(a, b \in A\), write \(ab\) rather than \(a * b\) for the value of the bilinear map on the pair \((a, b)\).

Note that an associative \(\ZZ\)-algebra (i.e. letting \(R = \ZZ\) the integers) is just a ring. In this course we will usually assume that \(R\) is a field, which we will denote by \(\mathbb{k}\).

Definition 1.1.2.

A Lie algebra over a field \(\mathbb{k}\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra \((\gg, [\cdot, \cdot]_\gg)\) which satisfies the following axioms:

  1. \([\cdot, \cdot]_\gg\) is alternating, i.e. \([x, x]_\gg = 0\) for all \(x \in \gg\).
  2. The Lie bracket satisfies the Jacobi Identity: that is, for all \(x, y, z \in \gg\) we have: \[[x, [y, z]_\gg]_\gg + [y, [z, x]_\gg]_\gg + [z, [x, y]_\gg]_\gg = 0.\]
Remark 1.1.3.
  1. Note that by considering the bracket \([x + y, x + y]_\gg\) it is easy to see that the alternating condition implies that for all \(x, y \in L\) we have \([x, y]_\gg = -[y, x]_\gg\), that is \([\cdot, \cdot]_\gg\) is skew-symmetric. If \(\operatorname{char}(\mathbb{k}) \neq 2\), the alternating condition is equivalent to skew-symmetry.
  2. If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra, then the alternating property implies that \(\gg\) cannot have a unit. It is also almost never the case that an associative product will satisfy the conditions to be a Lie bracket. Thus, viewed as such, a Lie algebra is a non-commutative, non-unital and (usually) non-associative algebra.
  3. We will normally write \([\cdot, \cdot]\) for the Lie bracket on any Lie algebra and decorate it only for emphasis or where there is the potential for confusion.
Definition 1.1.4.

Let \((\gg_1, [\cdot, \cdot]_1)\) and \((\gg_2, [\cdot, \cdot]_2)\) be Lie algebras. A \(\mathbb{k}\)-linear map \(\phi \colon \gg_1 \to \gg_2\) is said to be a homomorphism of Lie algebras if it respects the Lie brackets. That is: \[\phi([a, b]_1) = [\phi(a), \phi(b)]_2 \qquad \forall a, b \in \gg_1.\] An isomorphism of Lie algebras is a bijective homomorphism, since, just as for group homomorphisms and linear maps, the (set-theoretic) inverse of a Lie algebra homomorphism is automatically itself a Lie algebra homomorphism.

Example 1.1.5.
  1. If \(\dim_\mathbb{k}(\gg) = 1\), then the alternating condition forces the Lie bracket to vanish. Thus, up to isomorphism, there is a unique 1-dimensional Lie algebra over \(\mathbb{k}\), that is, any 1-dimensional Lie algebra \(\gg\) is isomorphic to \(\mathbb{k}\) equipped with the zero Lie bracket.
  2. If \(\mathfrak{a}\) is any vector space then setting the Lie bracket \([\cdot, \cdot]\) to be zero, i.e. setting \([a, b] = 0\) for all \(a, b \in \mathfrak{a}\), we get a (not very interesting) Lie algebra. Such Lie algebras are called abelian Lie algebras.
  3. If \(A\) is an (associative) \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra, then \(A\) can be given the structure of a \(\mathbb{k}\)-Lie algebra, where if \(a, b \in A\) then we set \([a, b] = a \cdot b - b \cdot a\), the commutator of \(a\) and \(b\). The commutator bracket is clearly alternating, and checking the Jacobi identity is a fundamental calculation. Indeed we have \[[x, [y, z]] = x(yz - zy) - (yz - zy)x = xyz - xzy - yzx + zyx\] \[= (xyz - yzx) + (zyx - xzy)\] where, in the final expression, we have paired terms which can be obtained from each other by cycling \(x, y\) and \(z\). Since the terms in these pairs have opposite signs, it is then clear that adding the three expressions obtained by cycling \(x, y\) and \(z\) gives zero. We will write \(\gg_A\) for the Lie algebra \((A, [\cdot, \cdot])\) obtained from an associative algebra in this way.
  4. An important special case of the previous example is that where \(A\) is the space of \(n\)-by-\(n\) matrices \(\Mat_n(\mathbb{k})\) with entries in \(\mathbb{k}\). The Lie algebra one obtains by equipping this space with the commutator Lie bracket \[[X, Y] = X \cdot Y - Y \cdot X, \qquad \forall X, Y \in \Mat_n(\mathbb{k}),\] is denoted \(\gl_n(\mathbb{k})\), or, if the field \(\mathbb{k}\) is clear from context, simply \(\gl_n\). Slightly more abstractly, if \(V\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-vector space, then we will write \(\gl_V\) for the Lie algebra obtained from the associative algebra \(\End_\mathbb{k}(V)\) by equipping it with the commutator bracket.
  5. If \(\dim(V) = 1\) then \(\gl_V = \gl_1 = \mathbb{k}\): the action of scalars gives an injective map \(\mathbb{k} \to \End(V)\) for any nonzero vector space \(V\) which is an isomorphism if \(\dim(V) = 1\). We will therefore write \(\gl_1\) for \(\mathbb{k}\) viewed as a Lie algebra with zero Lie bracket.
  6. If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \(\mathfrak{s} \leq \gg\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-subspace of \(\gg\) on which the restriction of the Lie bracket takes values in \(\mathfrak{s}\), so that it induces a bilinear operation \([\cdot, \cdot]_\mathfrak{s} \colon \mathfrak{s} \times \mathfrak{s} \to \mathfrak{s}\), then \((\mathfrak{s}, [\cdot, \cdot]_\mathfrak{s})\) is clearly a Lie algebra, and we say \(\mathfrak{s}\) is a (Lie) subalgebra of \(\gg\). If \(\mathfrak{s}\) is a Lie subalgebra of \(\gg\) then the inclusion map \(i \colon \mathfrak{s} \to \gg\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras.

Let \(\tr \colon \gl_n \to \mathbb{k}\) denote the trace map, so that if \(X = (x_{ij}) \in \gl_n\) then \(\tr(X) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} x_{ii}\). Then \(\tr\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras: indeed \[\tr([X, Y]) = \tr(XY) - \tr(YX) = 0 = [\tr(X), \tr(Y)].\] It follows that if \(\spl_n := \{X \in \gl_n : \tr(X) = 0\}\) then \(\spl_n\) is a Lie subalgebra of \(\gl_n\) (even though it is not a subalgebra of the associative algebra \(\Mat_n(\mathbb{k})\) provided \(n > 1\)). Similarly we define \(\spl_V\) to be the Lie subalgebra of \(\gl_V\) consisting of endomorphisms of trace 0. These are called special linear Lie algebras. More generally we say any Lie subalgebra of \(\gl_V\) for a vector space \(V\) is a linear Lie algebra.

  1. If \(\gg\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-Lie algebra and \(x \in \gg\), then the map \(\phi_x \colon \gl_1(\mathbb{k}) \to \gg\) given by \(\phi_x(t) = t \cdot x\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism, because the alternating property means that a Lie bracket vanishes on any 1-dimensional subspace of a Lie algebra. This gives a bijection between Lie algebra homomorphisms \(\phi \colon \gl_1(\mathbb{k}) \to \gg\) and the elements of \(\gg\) where if \(x \in \gg\) we let \(x \mapsto \phi_x \colon \gl_1(\mathbb{k}) \to \gg\) as above, while given \(\phi \colon \gl_1(\mathbb{k}) \to \gg\) we associate to it \(\phi(1) \in \gg\).
  2. If \(\gg_1, \gg_2\) are Lie algebras, then we may form their direct sum \(\gg_1 \oplus \gg_2\), which is the direct sum of \(\gg_1\) and \(\gg_2\) as a vector space, with Lie bracket given by \([(x_1, x_2), (y_1, y_2)] = ([x_1, y_1], [x_2, y_2])\) for all \(x_1, y_1 \in \gg_1\), \(x_2, y_2 \in \gg_2\). We may define the direct sum of \(k \geq 2\) Lie algebras in the same way.
  3. If \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an abelian Lie algebra then if we choose a basis \(\{e_1, \ldots, e_k\}\) of \(\mathfrak{a}\), then we obtain an isomorphism \(\theta \colon \gl_1(\mathbb{k})^{\oplus k} \to \mathfrak{a}\) where \(\theta(t_1, \ldots, t_k) = \sum_{i=1}^{k} t_i e_i\). Indeed the Lie bracket on both \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \(\gl_1(\mathbb{k})^{\oplus k}\) is zero, hence we need only check that \(\theta\) is an isomorphism of vector spaces, which is clear by construction.

The following definition should be understood as the infinitesimal analogue of an automorphism of a \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra.

Definition 1.1.6.

Generalising the example of vector fields in the previous chapter, if \(A\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra and \(\delta \colon A \to A\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-linear map, then we say \(\delta\) is a \(\mathbb{k}\)-derivation if it satisfies the Leibniz rule, that is, if: \[\delta(a \cdot b) = \delta(a) \cdot b + a \cdot \delta(b), \qquad \forall a, b \in A.\] It is easy to see by a direct calculation that if \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) denotes the \(\mathbb{k}\)-vector space of \(\mathbb{k}\)-derivations on \(A\), then \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) is stable under taking commutators, that is, if \[[\delta_1, \delta_2] = \delta_1 \circ \delta_2 - \delta_2 \circ \delta_1,\] then \([\delta_1, \delta_2] \in \Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\). Indeed \[(\delta_1 \circ \delta_2 - \delta_2 \circ \delta_1)(a \cdot b) = \delta_1(\delta_2(a) \cdot b + a \cdot \delta_2(b)) - \delta_2(\delta_1(a) \cdot b + a \cdot \delta_1(b))\] \[= \delta_1\delta_2(a) \cdot b + \delta_2(a) \cdot \delta_1(b) + \delta_1(a) \cdot \delta_2(b) + a \cdot \delta_1\delta_2(b)\] \[\quad - \delta_2\delta_1(a) \cdot b - \delta_1(a) \cdot \delta_2(b) - \delta_2(a) \cdot \delta_1(b) - a \cdot \delta_2\delta_1(b)\] \[= [\delta_1, \delta_2](a) \cdot b + a \cdot [\delta_1, \delta_2](b).\]

Example 1.1.7.
  1. If \(A\) is an associative \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra, then if \(a \in A\) the operation of taking commutator with \(a\) is a derivation. That is, if \(\delta_a \colon A \to A\) is given by \(\delta_a(b) = [a, b]\) for any \(b \in A\), then \(\delta_a \in \Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\). Indeed \[\delta_a(b) \cdot c + b \cdot \delta_a(c) = (ab - ba)c + b(ac - ca) = a \cdot (bc) - (bc) \cdot a = \delta_a(b \cdot c).\] The map \(\Delta \colon \gg_A \to \Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) given by \(\Delta(a) = \delta_a\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, that is, \(\Delta([a, b]) = [\delta_a, \delta_b]\). In fact slightly more is true: if \(\partial \in \Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) and \(b \in A\) then \([\partial, \delta_b] = \delta_{\partial(b)}\). (Applying this to \(\partial = \delta_a\) gives the compatibility with commutators). Indeed for all \(c \in \gg\) we have \[[\partial, \delta_b](c) = \partial(bc - cb) - (b\partial(c) - \partial(c) \cdot b) = \partial(b) \cdot c - c \cdot \partial(b) = \delta_{\partial(b)}(c).\]
  2. Given a Lie algebra \(\gg\) we let \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(\gg) = \{\phi \in \gl_\gg : \phi([x, y]) = [\phi(x), y] + [x, \phi(y)]\}\). It is a Lie subalgebra of \(\gl_\gg\) (indeed the proof above that \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) is a Lie algebra only requires the product on \(A\) to be bilinear). Note that if \(A\) is an associative algebra, then \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(A) \subseteq \Der_\mathbb{k}(\gg_A)\) since if \(\delta \in \Der_\mathbb{k}(A)\) then \(\delta(ab - ba) = \delta(a) \cdot b + a \cdot \delta(b) - \delta(b) \cdot a - b \cdot \delta(a) = [\delta(a), b] + [a, \delta(b)]\).
  3. One way of interpreting the Jacobi identity is that, assuming the alternating property, it is equivalent to the condition that, for any \(x \in \gg\), the operation \(\ad(x) \in \gl_\gg\) given by \(\ad(x)(y) = [x, y]\) lies in \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(\gg)\). Indeed \[\ad(x)([y, z]) = [\ad(x)(y), z] + [y, \ad(x)(z)]\] \[\iff [x, [y, z]] = [[x, y], z] + [y, [x, z]]\] \[\iff [x, [y, z]] - [y, [x, z]] - [[x, y], z] = 0\] \[\iff [x, [y, z]] + [y, [z, x]] + [z, [x, y]] = 0\] where the equivalence between the third and fourth equalities follows from the alternating property of a Lie bracket.
  4. The Jacobi identity is also equivalent, again assuming the alternating property, to the fact that \(\ad \colon \gg \to \gl_\gg\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras: Indeed, for all \(x, y, z \in \gg\) we have \[[\ad(x), \ad(y)](z) = [x, [y, z]] - [y, [x, z]]\] \[= [x, [y, z]] + [y, [z, x]]\] \[= -[z, [x, y]]\] \[= \ad([x, y])(z).\] where the second and fourth equality uses the alternating property, and the third the Jacobi identity.
  5. If \((A, *)\) is any \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra then \(A^{\mathrm{op}}\) is the \(\mathbb{k}\)-algebra with product \(*^{\mathrm{op}}\), where \(a *^{\mathrm{op}} b = b * a\). If \(A\) is commutative, then \(A^{\mathrm{op}}\) is isomorphic to \(A\). In the case of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) then \(\gg^{\mathrm{op}}\) is the Lie algebra \((\gg, -[\cdot, \cdot])\). In fact \(\gg\) is canonically isomorphic to \(\gg^{\mathrm{op}}\): if we let \(m \colon \gg \to \gg^{\mathrm{op}}\) be the map \(m(x) = -x\), then \[m([x, y]) = -[x, y] = [y, x] = [x, y]^{\mathrm{op}} = [-x, -y]^{\mathrm{op}} = [m(x), m(y)], \qquad x, y \in \gg.\] Hence \(m \colon \gg \to \gg^{\mathrm{op}}\) is an isomorphism from \(\gg\) to \(\gg^{\mathrm{op}}\).
*Remark 1.1.8.

Combining \(iii)\) and \(iv)\) in the above example we see that the adjoint representation \(x \mapsto \ad(x)\) is in fact a Lie algebra homomorphism from \(\gg\) to \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(\gg)\). This is, in a sense, where the Jacobi identity comes from: very roughly, the conjugation action of \(G\) on itself yields a group homomorphism \(G \to \gl_\gg\) (since conjugation preserves the identity \(e \in G\)) whose image lies in \(\operatorname{Aut}(\gg)\). The adjoint representation of \(\gg\) is then the derivative of this action, which yields the adjoint representation \(\ad\) which hence should have image in \(\Der_\mathbb{k}(\gg)\).

1.2 Ideals and isomorphism theorems

As one might expect if a Lie algebra is supposed to be an "infinitesimal" version of a Lie group, most notions for groups have analogues in the context of Lie algebras. It might be worth noting, however, that the linear structure of a Lie algebra comes from the basic properties of the derivative: it is the Lie bracket which reflects the "infinitesimal" versions of properties of a group. The existence of both the linear structure and the Lie bracket means that many of the notions we consider for a Lie algebra also have natural analogues for a ring (which is an algebra object equipped with an addition and an (associative) multiplication).

Definition 1.2.1.

An ideal in a Lie algebra \((\gg, [\cdot, \cdot]_\gg)\) is a subspace \(\mathfrak{a}\) such that for all \(x \in \gg\) and \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) we have \([a, x]_\gg \in \mathfrak{a}\). It is easy to check that if \(\phi \colon \gg_1 \to \gg_2\) is a homomorphism, then \[\ker(\phi) = \{a \in \gg_1 : \phi(a) = 0\}\] is an ideal of \(\gg_1\). We will write \(I \trianglelefteq \gg\) to indicate that \(I\) is an ideal in \(\gg\).

Remark 1.2.2.

Notice that because a Lie bracket is alternating, the condition that, for all \(x \in \gg\) and \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) one has \([a, x] \in \mathfrak{a}\), is equivalent to the condition that \([x, a] \in \mathfrak{a}\) for all \(x \in \gg\), \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\). Thus, similarly to commutative rings, the notions of a left, right or two-sided ideal in a Lie algebra are all the same.

Just as for rings, in fact any ideal is the kernel of a Lie algebra homomorphism:

Theorem 1.2.3.

(The universal property of quotients & the 1st isomorphism theorem): Let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be an ideal in a Lie algebra \(\gg\), and let \(q \colon \gg \to \gg/\mathfrak{a}\) be the quotient map of vector spaces. Then there is a unique Lie bracket on \(\gg/\mathfrak{a}\) with respect to which \(q\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, that is, for all \(x, y \in \gg\) \[[q(x), q(y)] = q([x, y]),\] i.e. \[[x + \mathfrak{a},\; y + \mathfrak{a}] = [x, y] + \mathfrak{a}.\] Moreover, if \(\varphi \colon \gg \to \mathfrak{k}\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism such that \(\varphi(\mathfrak{a}) = 0\), then \(\varphi\) induces a homomorphism \(\bar{\varphi} \colon \gg/\mathfrak{a} \to \mathfrak{k}\) such that \(\bar{\varphi} \circ q = \varphi\), so that \(\ker(\bar{\varphi}) = \ker(\varphi)/\mathfrak{a}\). In particular, if we set \(\mathfrak{a} = \ker(\varphi)\) then we see that \(\varphi\) induces an isomorphism \(\bar{\varphi} \colon \gg/\ker(\varphi) \xrightarrow{\sim} \im(\varphi)\).

Proof.

The proof is almost identical to the proof in the case of rings. The key point is to see that the coset \([x, y] + \mathfrak{a}\) is independent of the choice of representative for the cosets \(x + \mathfrak{a}\), \(y + \mathfrak{a}\), and the condition that \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an ideal ensures this.

□
Definition 1.2.4.

If \(V, W\) are subspaces of a Lie algebra \(\gg\), then write \([V, W]\) for the linear span of the elements \(\{[v, w] : v \in V,\; w \in W\}\). Notice that if \(I, J\) are ideals in \(\gg\) then so is \([I, J]\). Indeed to check this, note that by part \(viii)\) of Example 1.1.5, if \(z \in \gg\), \(x \in I\), \(y \in J\) then we have \[[z, [x, y]] = \ad(z)([x, y]) = [\ad(z)(x), y] + [x, \ad(z)(y)] \in [I, J]\] since \(\ad(z)(x) = [z, x] \in I\) if \(x \in \gg\), and similarly \(\ad(z)(y) = [z, y] \in J\).

Remark 1.2.5.

If \(I\) and \(J\) are ideals in a Lie algebra \(\gg\) then it is easy to check that their intersection \(I \cap J\) is again an ideal in \(\gg\), and we have \([I, J] \subseteq I \cap J\). (Thus \([I, J]\) is the Lie algebra analogue of the product of ideals in a commutative ring.) For example, since \(\gg\) is an ideal in \(\gg\), its product with itself, \([\gg, \gg]\), is an ideal \(D\gg \subseteq \gg\), known as the derived subalgebra of \(\gg\). The quotient \(\gg/D\gg\) is the largest abelian quotient of \(\gg\), in the sense that if \(\phi \colon \gg \to \mathfrak{a}\) where \(\mathfrak{a}\) is abelian, then \(\phi\) factors through the quotient map \(q \colon \gg \to \gg/D\gg\) (we will prove this later in Lemma 4.1.4).

Similarly, it is easy to see that the linear sum \(I + J\) of \(I\) and \(J\) is also an ideal.

Definition 1.2.6.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and let \(\mathfrak{a} \leq \gg\) be a subalgebra. The normalizer of \(\mathfrak{a}\) in \(\gg\) is \[N_\gg(\mathfrak{a}) := \{x \in \gg : \ad(x)(\mathfrak{a}) \subseteq \mathfrak{a}\} = \{x \in \gg : \ad(a)(x) \in \mathfrak{a},\; \forall a \in \mathfrak{a}\}.\] This is a subalgebra of \(\gg\), as one can check using the formulation of the Jacobi identity given in Definition 1.2.4. It is the largest subalgebra of \(\gg\) within which \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an ideal.

Definition 1.2.7.

If a nontrivial Lie algebra has no nontrivial ideals we say that it is almost simple. If it is in addition not abelian, i.e. the Lie bracket is not identically zero, then we say that it is simple.

Just as for groups and rings, one can deduce the usual stable of isomorphism theorems from the first isomorphism theorem.

Theorem 1.2.8.
  1. If \(\hh\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\) and \(I\) is an ideal in \(\gg\) then \(\hh + I\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\) (containing \(I\) as an ideal), \(\hh \cap I\) is an ideal in \(\hh\), and \[(\hh + I)/I \cong \hh/(\hh \cap I).\]
  2. If \(J \subseteq I \subseteq \gg\) are ideals of \(\gg\) then we have: \[(\gg/J)\big/(I/J) \cong \gg/I.\]
Proof.

The proofs are identical to the corresponding results for groups. We give a proof of \(ii)\) as an example. Since \(J \subseteq I\) the quotient map \(q \colon \gg \to \gg/I\), which has kernel \(I\), induces a map \(\bar{q} \colon \gg/J \to \gg/I\). The kernel of this map is by definition \(\{x + J : x + I = I\}\), that is, \(I/J\). The result follows.

□

Chapter 2. Representations of Lie algebras

Just as for finite groups (or indeed groups in general) one way of studying Lie algebras is to try and understand how they can act on other (usually more concrete) objects. For Lie algebras, since they are already vector spaces over \(k\), it is natural to study their action on linear spaces, or in other words, their representations.

2.1 Definition and examples

Definition 2.1.1.

A representation of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) is a vector space \(V\) equipped with a linear action of \(\gg\), that is, a homomorphism of Lie algebras \(\rho \colon \gg \to \gl_V\). In other words, \(\rho\) is a linear map such that \[\rho([x, y]) = \rho(x) \circ \rho(y) - \rho(y) \circ \rho(x)\] where \(\circ\) denotes composition of linear maps.

We also refer to a representation of \(\gg\) as a \(\gg\)-representation or \(\gg\)-module. A representation is faithful if \(\ker(\rho) = 0\). When there is no danger of confusion we will normally suppress \(\rho\) in our notation, and write \(x(v)\) rather than \(\rho(x)(v)\), for \(x \in \gg\), \(v \in V\).

If \((V, \rho)\) and \((W, \sigma)\) are \(\gg\)-representations, we say that \(\phi \colon V \to W\) is a \(\gg\)-homomorphism (or homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations) if \(\phi \circ \rho(x) = \sigma(x) \circ \phi\) for all \(x \in \gg\). We will write \(\operatorname{Rep}(\gg)\) for the collection of all representations of \(\gg\).

We will study representation of various classes of Lie algebras in this course, but the following give some basic examples.

Example 2.1.2.
  1. If \(V\) is a \(k\)-vector space, then the identity map \(\gl_V \to \gl_V\) gives a representation of \(\gl_V\) on \(V\), which is known as the vector representation. Clearly any subalgebra \(\gg\) of \(\gl_V\) also inherits \(V\) as a representation, where then the action map \(\rho\) is just the inclusion map.
  2. Let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be an abelian Lie algebra. If \((V, \rho)\) is a representation of \(\mathfrak{a}\), then the image \(\rho(\mathfrak{a})\) of \(\mathfrak{a}\) generates a commutative subalgebra of \(\End_k(V)\): if \(a, b \in \mathfrak{a}\) then \(0 = \rho([a, b]) = \rho(a)\rho(b) - \rho(b)\rho(a)\), so that \[\rho(a)\rho(b) = \rho(b)\rho(a), \quad \forall\, a, b \in \mathfrak{a}.\]
  3. Given an arbitrary Lie algebra \(\gg\), there is a natural representation \(\ad\) of \(\gg\) on \(\gg\) itself known as the adjoint representation. The homomorphism \(\ad \colon \gg \to \gl_\gg\) from \(\gg\) to \(\gl_\gg\) is given by \[\ad(x)(y) = [x, y], \qquad \forall\, x, y \in \gg.\] Indeed, as noted in iv) of Example 1.1.7, the fact that this map is a homomorphism of Lie algebras is just a rephrasing of the Jacobi identity. Note that while the vector representation is clearly faithful, in general the adjoint representation is not. Indeed the kernel is known as the centre of \(\gg\): \[\mathfrak{z}(\gg) = \{x \in \gg : [x, y] = 0,\; \forall\, y \in \gg\}.\] Note that if \(x \in \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\) then for any representation \(\rho \colon \gg \to \gl(V)\) the endomorphism \(\rho(x)\) commutes with all the elements \(\rho(y) \in \End(V)\) for all \(y \in \gg\).
  4. If \(\gg\) is any Lie algebra, the pair \((k, 0)\) consisting of the vector space \(k\) together with the zero map \(0 \colon \gg \to \gl_1\) is a \(\gg\)-representation. This representation is called the trivial representation. It is the Lie algebra analogue of the trivial representation for a group (which sends every group element to the identity map \(1_V \in \GL(V)\)).
  5. If \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, then the sum of all subrepresentations of \(V\) isomorphic to \((k, 0)\) is a natural subrepresentation \[V^{\gg} = \{v \in V : \rho(x)(v) = 0,\; \forall\, x \in \gg\}\] known as the \(\gg\)-invariants in \(V\).
  6. If \((V, \rho)\) is a representation of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) and \(\theta \colon \hh \to \gg\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, then we define the pull-back of \((V, \rho)\) to the representation of \(\hh\) given by \((V, \rho \circ \theta)\). The most common example of a pull-back is restriction, when \(\hh\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\) (and thus \(\theta\) is the inclusion map from \(\hh\) to \(\gg\)).

The following definitions are useful when studying the structure of Lie algebra representations:

Definition 2.1.3.

A representation is said to be irreducible if it has no proper non-zero subrepresentations, and it is said to be completely reducible if it is isomorphic to a direct sum of irreducible representations. A representation \(V\) is said to be indecomposable if, whenever we have \(V = U_1 \oplus U_2\) with \(U_1, U_2\) subrepresentations, either \(U_1 = V\) or \(U_2 = V\) (and \(U_2 = 0\), \(U_1 = 0\) respectively).

It is easy to show (by induction on dimension) that any finite-dimensional representation is a direct sum of indecomposable subrepresentations.

Remark 2.1.4.

A representation \((V, \rho)\) is said to be semisimple if, given any subrepresentation \(U\) of \(V\) there is a complementary subrepresentation \(W\) of \(V\), that is, such that \(V = U \oplus W\). A finite-dimensional semisimple representation is completely reducible, and any representation which is the sum of its irreducible subrepresentations is semisimple, hence in particular, any completely reducible representation is semisimple.

Example 2.1.5.

In this example, we classify the representations of the simplest Lie algebra \(\gl_1\): a representation \((V, \rho)\) of \(\gl_1\) is given by a Lie algebra homomorphism \(\rho \colon \gl_1 \to \gl_V\). But we saw in vii) of Example 1.1.5, that there is a natural bijection between such homomorphisms and elements \(x \in \gl_V\) given by \(\rho \mapsto \rho(1)\). Through this correspondence the problem of classifying \(\gl_1\)-representations up to isomorphism becomes the problem of classifying vector spaces equipped with an endomorphism up to conjugacy.

If we assume \(k\) is algebraically closed this classification of linear endomorphisms is given by the Jordan canonical form. It is a useful exercise to translate statements about linear maps into statements about representations of \(\gl_1\). For example, the irreducible representations of \(\gl_1\) are the one-dimensional ones, and correspond to eigenvectors of \(\rho(1)\). What do the indecomposable representations correspond to?

Example 2.1.6.

Now suppose that \(\gg\) is any finite-dimensional Lie algebra and that \((L, \lambda)\) is a one-dimensional representation of \(\gg\). The canonical identifications \(\gl_L \cong k = \gl_1(k)\) given in v) of Example 1.1.5 identifies \(\lambda\) with a homomorphism \([\lambda] \colon \gg \to \gl_1\). But since \(k = \gl_1\) has the zero Lie bracket, this is just an element of \(\gg^*\) which vanishes on \(\{[x, y] : x, y \in \gg\}\), that is, \(\lambda\) is an element of \((D\gg)^0\), the annihilator of \(D(\gg) = [\gg, \gg]\), the derived subalgebra of \(\gg\) introduced in Remark 1.2.5.

2.2 Subrepresentations, quotients, duals, and composition series

There are a number of standard ways of constructing new representations from old, all of which have their analogues in the context of group representations. We begin with some definitions.

Definition 2.2.1.

Let \(V\) be a \(k\)-vector space and \(U \leq V\) a subspace. Write \(i \colon U \to V\) for the inclusion map and \(p \colon V \to V/U\) for the quotient map. Let \[\mathfrak{b}_U = \{x \in \gl_V : x(U) \subseteq U\} = \{x \in \gl_V : p \circ x \circ i = 0\}.\] Then we have linear maps \(i^* \colon \mathfrak{b}_U \to \End(U)\) and \(p^* \colon \mathfrak{b}_U \to \End(V/U)\). Here \(i^*(x) = x \circ i\) is simply the restriction of \(x\) to \(U\). For the map \(p^*\) first note that if \(x \in \mathfrak{b}_U\) then \(U \subseteq \ker(p \circ x)\), and so by the universal property of a quotient map \(p \circ x\) induces a map \(\bar{\alpha} \colon V/U \to V/U\) which satisfies \(p \circ x = \bar{x} \circ p\), and we set \(p^*(\alpha) = \bar{\alpha}\).

Lemma 2.2.2.

If \(\rho \colon \gg \to \gl_V\) is a \(\gg\)-representation and \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{b}_U\) for some subspace \(U \leq V\), then \(U\) and \(V/U\) become \(\gg\)-representations with action maps \(i^* \circ \rho\) and \(p^* \circ \rho\) respectively.

Proof.

It is clear that \(\mathfrak{b}_U\) is an associative subalgebra of \(\End_k(V)\) and both \(i^*\) and \(p^*\) are homomorphisms of associative algebras, hence they are also homomorphisms of the associated Lie algebras. The Lemma follows immediately.

□

It will be useful later to have the following definition:

Definition 2.2.3.

Let \(V\) be a vector space, and let \(\mathcal{F} = (F_i)_{i=0}^k\) be a flag in \(V\), that is \[\mathcal{F} = (V = F_0 \supset F_1 \supset F_2 \supset \cdots \supset F_k = 0)\] is a nested sequence of subspaces with \(\dim(F_{i+1}) < \dim(F_i)\) for \(1 \leq i \leq k\). If \(\mathcal{F}_1\) and \(\mathcal{F}_2\) are flags in \(V\) then we say that \(\mathcal{F}_2\) is a refinement of \(\mathcal{F}_1\) if every subspace in \(\mathcal{F}_1\) occurs in \(\mathcal{F}_2\). If \(\dim(F_i) = i\) for all \(i\) (so that \(\dim(V) = k\)) then \(\mathcal{F}\) is called a complete flag (as it cannot be refined any further). It is clear (since any linearly independent set can be extended to a basis) that any flag can be refined to a complete flag.

We let \(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{F} = \bigcap_{1 \leq i \leq k-1} \mathfrak{b}_{F_i} = \{x \in \gl_V : x(F_i) \subseteq F_i\}\). This is an associative subalgebra of \(\End(V)\), and hence a Lie subalgebra. If \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, the elements of the flag are subrepresentations of \(V\) if and only if \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{F}\).

Definition 2.2.4.

If \(V\) is a \(k\)-vector space and \(a \in \gl_V\), then \(a\) induces a linear map \(a^\intercal \colon V^* \to V^*\) which we call the adjoint (or transpose) of \(a\), given by \(a^\intercal(f)(v) = (f \circ a)(v)\). However, if \(a, b \in \gl_V\), \(v \in V\), \[(ab)^\intercal(f)(v) = (f \circ (ab))(v) = ((f \circ a) \circ b)(v) = b^\intercal(f \circ a)(v) = b^\intercal \circ a^\intercal(f)(v),\] so that \(a \mapsto a^\intercal\) is an algebra anti-homomorphism from \(\End_k(V)\) to \(\End_k(V^*)^{\mathrm{op}}\) (see 1.1.5 (v)), so that it is also a Lie algebra homomorphism \(\gl_V \to \gl_{V^*}^{\mathrm{op}}\). But, again by 1.1.5 (v) the map \(x \mapsto -x\) is an isomorphism from \(\gg \to \gg^{\mathrm{op}}\) for any Lie algebra, it follows that \(x \mapsto -x^\intercal\) is an isomorphism of Lie algebras from \(\gl_V\) to \(\gl_{V^*}\). For \(x \in \gl_V\), we will write \(x^* = -x^\intercal\). Moreover, if \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, then we may define \(\rho^*(x) = (\rho(x))^*\), so that \(\rho^* \colon \gg \to \gl_{V^*}\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism and hence \((V^*, \rho^*)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, the dual representation to \((V, \rho)\).

Recall that the annihilator of a subspace \(U \leq V\) is the subspace of \(V^*\) given by \[U^0 = \{f \in V^* : f(u) = 0,\; \forall\, u \in U\}.\] It is clear from the definition that the correspondence \(U \mapsto U^0\) is order-reversing for containment, that is, if \(U_1 \leq U_2\) then \(U_2^0 \leq U_1^0\). By considering a basis of \(V\) and the corresponding dual basis of \(V^*\), one can also check that \(\dim(U) + \dim(U^0) = \dim(V)\), and

Lemma 2.2.5.

If \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation with dual representation \((V^*, \rho^*)\), then the map \(U \mapsto U^0\) gives an order-reversing correspondence between the subrepresentations of \(V\) and \(V^*\) respectively. Since \(V\) is finite-dimensional, \((V^*)^*\) is canonically isomorphic to \(V\), and via that canonical identification, this correspondence is an involution, that is, \((U^0)^0 = U\). Moreover, \(U^* \cong V^*/U^0\) and \((V/U)^* \cong U^0\) as \(\gg\)-representations.

Proof.

Let \(\iota \colon U \to V\) be the inclusion map of a subspace \(U\) and let \(p \colon V \to V/U\) be the quotient map. Then we have seen that \(\mathfrak{b}_U = \{x \in \gl_V \mid x(U) \subseteq U\} = \{x \in \gl_V : p \circ x \circ \iota = 0\}\). Now the adjoint of the inclusion map \(\iota^\intercal \colon V^* \to U^*\) clearly has kernel \(U^0\) and is, moreover, surjective (as one can check using dual bases for example). Moreover, the adjoint of the quotient map \(p^\intercal \colon (V/U)^* \to V^*\) is clearly injective with image \(U^0\).

It follows that \(\mathfrak{b}_{U^0} = \{y \in \gl_{V^*} \mid y(U^0) \subseteq U^0\} = \{y \in \gl_{V^*} \mid \iota^\intercal \circ y \circ p^\intercal = 0\}\). But if \(x \in \gl_V\) and \(x^* = -x^\intercal\) then \[\iota^\intercal \circ (x^*) \circ p^\intercal = -(\iota^\intercal \circ x^\intercal \circ p^\intercal) = -(p \circ x \circ \iota)^\intercal.\] Thus the isomorphism of Lie algebras from \(\gl_V\) to \(\gl_{V^*}\) given by \(x \mapsto x^* = -x^\intercal\) identifies \(\mathfrak{b}_U\) with \(\mathfrak{b}_{U^0}\), and \(U\) is a subrepresentation of \(V\) if and only if \(U^0\) is a subrepresentation of \(V^*\). Since the correspondence \(U \mapsto U^0\) is clearly order-reversing, the first sentence of the Lemma follows, and since the maps \(p^\intercal\) and \(\iota^\intercal\) are clearly \(\gg\)-homomorphisms when \(U\) is a subrepresentation of \(V\), the final sentence is also immediate. Finally, the canonical isomorphism \(d \colon V \to V^{**}\) clearly maps \(U\) into \((U^0)^0\), and the dimension formula \(\dim(U) + \dim(U^0) = \dim(V)\) shows they have the same dimension, hence \(d(U) = (U^0)^0\) as required.

□
Remark 2.2.6.

Note that the previous Lemma shows that the dual of an irreducible representation is irreducible.

2.2.1 Exact sequences of representations

Definition 2.2.7.

A sequence of maps of \(\gg\)-representations \[U \xrightarrow{\alpha} V \xrightarrow{\beta} W\] is said to be exact at \(V\) if \(\im(\alpha) = \ker(\beta)\). A sequence of maps \[0 \to U \xrightarrow{\alpha} V \xrightarrow{\beta} W \to 0\] is called a short exact sequence if it is exact at each of \(U\), \(V\) and \(W\), so that \(\alpha\) is injective and \(\beta\) is surjective and \(\im(\alpha) = \ker(\beta)\). If \(V\) is the middle term of such a short exact sequence, it contains a subrepresentation isomorphic to \(U\), such that the corresponding quotient representation is isomorphic to \(W\), and hence, roughly speaking, \(V\) is built by gluing together \(U\) and \(W\). Just as for Lie algebras, an exact sequence \[0 \to U \xrightarrow{\alpha} V \xrightarrow{\beta} W \to 0\] is said to be split if \(\beta\) admits a right inverse \(s \colon W \to V\), that is, a \(\gg\)-homomorphism \(s\) such that \(\beta \circ s = \operatorname{id}_W\).

Lemma 2.2.8.

Suppose that \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \[0 \to U \xrightarrow{\alpha} V \xrightarrow{\beta} W \to 0\] is a short exact sequence of \(\gg\)-representations. Then the sequence is split if and only if \(V\) is isomorphic to \(U \oplus W\).

Proof.

If the short exact sequence is split, then \(s(W) \cap \alpha(U) = \{0\}\), since \(\beta\) is injective on \(\im(s)\) and \(\im(\alpha) = \ker(\beta)\), as if \(v \in V\), then for any \(v \in V\) we have \(v = (v - s \circ \beta(v)) + s \circ \beta(v)\), where \(s \circ \beta(v)\) lies in the image of \(s\), and since \(\beta \circ s = \operatorname{id}_W\), \(v - s \circ \beta(v) \in \ker(\beta) = \im(\alpha)\). It follows that \(V = \alpha(U) \oplus s(W)\), and since \(\alpha\) and \(s\) are injective, the result follows.

□
Example 2.2.9.

To see a non-split extension, let \(\gg = \mathfrak{n}_2\) be the one-dimensional Lie algebra, thought of as the (nilpotent) Lie algebra of \(2 \times 2\) strictly upper triangular matrices. Then its natural 2-dimensional representation on \(k^2\) given by the inclusion \(\mathfrak{n}_2 \to \gl_2(k)\) gives a non-split extension \[0 \to k_0 \xrightarrow{i} k^2 \to k_0 \to 0\] where \(k_0\) is the trivial representation, and \(i \colon k_0 \to k^2\) is the inclusion \(t \mapsto (t, 0)\). The extension cannot be trivial, because the image of \(\mathfrak{n}_2\) is non-zero. It is in fact easy to see using linear algebra that for \(\gl_1(k) = \mathfrak{n}_2\), an extension of one-dimensional representations \(k_\alpha\) and \(k_\beta\) automatically splits if \(\alpha \neq \beta\) while there is, up to isomorphism, one non-split extension of \(k_\alpha\) with itself (\(\alpha, \beta \in (\gl_1(k))^*\)).

The notion of a composition series, which was used to study finite groups in Part A Group Theory, has an analogue for representations of a given Lie algebra \(\gg\).

Definition 2.2.10.

Let \(V\) be a \(\gg\)-representation. A nested sequence \(\mathcal{C} = (V = F_0 \supset F_1 \supset \cdots \supset F_d = 0)\) is said to be a composition series for \(V\) if each \(F_i\) is a subrepresentation, and the subquotients \(F_{i-1}/F_i\) are irreducible (for each \(i \in \{1, \ldots, d\}\)). Thus a composition series admits no proper refinement. It is easy to show (say by induction on dimension) that any flag of subrepresentations can be refined to a composition series (and so, in particular, composition series exist). The isomorphism classes of irreducibles which occur as one of these irreducible subquotients are called the composition factors, and the multiplicity with which such an irreducible, say \(S\), occurs is known as its composition multiplicity. We write \([S : \mathcal{C}]\) for the multiplicity with which \(S\) occurs as a composition factor of \(\mathcal{C}\).

Remark 2.2.11.

A composition series can also be viewed as the vestige of how the representation \(V\) was built up from its composition factors \(S_i = F_{i-1}/F_i\). Indeed for each \(k \in \{1, \ldots, d\}\) we have \[0 \to F_k \xrightarrow{\alpha} F_{k-1} \xrightarrow{\beta} S_k \to 0\] Thus starting with \(S_1\) one constructs \(F_0/F_2\) by extending it by \(S_2\). One obtains \(F_0/F_3\) by extending \(F_0/F_2\) by \(S_3\) and so on, until finally we get \(V\) by extending \(F_0/F_1\) by \(F_1 = S_1\) to obtain \(V\) itself!

Proposition 2.2.12 (Jordan–Hölder theorem).

Let \(S\) be an irreducible finite-dimensional \(\gg\)-representation and let \((V, \rho)\) be any finite-dimensional \(\gg\)-representation. The multiplicity with which a given isomorphism class \(S\) of simple module occurs in a composition series for \(V\) is independent of the composition series, hence we may denote it by \([S : V]\).

Proof.

We prove the proposition by induction on the minimal length \(\ell(V)\) of a composition series for a representation \(V\). If \(\ell(V) = 1\) then \(V\) is irreducible, and hence it has a unique composition series.

Now suppose that \(\ell(V) = k > 1\) and that \(\mathcal{C} = (F_i)_{i=0}^k\) and \(\mathcal{C}' = (G_j)_{j=0}^l\) are composition series for \(V\) (where \(\mathcal{C}\) is of minimal length). Consider \(F_k < V\), a simple submodule. Consider the intersections \((G_j \cap F_k)_{j=0}^l\). These form a weakly decreasing sequence of submodules of \(F_k\), and hence as \(F_k\) is simple, there is a unique \(s\) such that \(G_j \cap F_k = F_k\) for \(j \leq s\) and \(G_j \cap F_k = 0\) for \(j > s\). But then \(F_k \subseteq G_s\) but \(F_k \cap G_{s+1} = \{0\}\), so that by the second isomorphism theorem \[F_k = F_k / (F_k \cap G_{s+1}) \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;} (F_k + G_{s+1})/G_{s+1} \hookrightarrow G_s / G_{s+1}.\] Since \(G_s/G_{s+1}\) is simple, it follows that \(F_k \cong G_s/G_{s+1}\) and hence \(G_s = F_k \oplus G_{s+1}\). But then if \(q \colon V \to V/F_k\) is the quotient map, it restricts to an isomorphism \(q|_{G_{s+1}} \colon G_{s+1} \to G_s/F_k\), and hence \((q(G_j))_{0 \leq j \leq l,\, j \neq s}\) is a composition series for \(V/F_k\). Now clearly \((q(F_{i+1}))_{i=0}^{k-1}\) is also a composition series for \(V/F_k\) which, moreover, has length \(k - 1\), so that \(\ell(V/F_k) \leq k - 1\), and thus by induction, the composition series \((F_i/F_k)_{i=0}^{k-1}\) and \((q(G_j))_{j \neq s}\) have the same composition multiplicities.

Since these differ from the composition multiplicities of \(\mathcal{C}\) and \(\mathcal{C}'\) respectively only in having one fewer copy of the isomorphism class of the simple module \(F_k\), it follows that \(\mathcal{C}\) and \(\mathcal{C}'\) have the same composition multiplicities as required.

□

Composition factors are compatible with duality for representations. More precisely, we have the following:

Lemma 2.2.13.

Suppose that \(V\) is a \(\gg\)-representation and that \(\{S_i : 1 \leq i \leq k\}\) are its composition factors. Then \(V^*\) has composition factors \(\{S_i^* : 1 \leq i \leq k\}\) and moreover \([S_i : V] = [S_i^* : V^*]\).

Proof.

We use induction on \(\ell(V)\) the length of a composition series for \(V\), the result being trivial if \(\ell(V) = 0\), that is, if \(V = \{0\}\). If \(\ell(V) = d\) and \(\mathcal{C} = (F_i)_{i=0}^d\) is a composition series for \(V\), then by taking annihilators we obtain a flag \(\mathcal{C}^* = (F_{d-k}^0)_{k=0}^d\) of subrepresentations of \(V^*\). By Lemma 2.2.5, \(F_{d-1}^0 \cong (V/F_{d-1})^*\) and \(V^*/F_{d-1}^0 \cong F_{d-1}^*\). Since \(((V/F_{d-1})/(F_i/F_{d-1}))^* \cong (V/F_i)^* \cong F_i^0\), the result then follows by applying induction to \(V/F_{d-1}\) and its composition series \((F_k/F_{d-1})_{k=0}^{d-1}\).

□

Although we will not use "characters" of Lie algebra representations (that is, the function on \(\gg\) given by \(x \mapsto \tr_V(\rho(x))\)), we will instead use "trace forms" and the next Lemma will imply that the trace form only depends on the composition factors of the representation.

Lemma 2.2.14.

Suppose that \(V\) is a finite-dimensional \(k\)-vector space and let \(\mathcal{F} = (F_k)_{k=0}^d\) be a flag of subspaces of \(V\), \(V = F_0 > F_1 > \cdots F_{d-1} > F_d = \{0\}\). If \(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{F} = \{a \in \End(V) : x(F_i) \subseteq F_i : 0 \leq i \leq d\}\) then any \(a \in \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{F}\) induces a linear map \(a_i \colon S_i \to S_i\) where \(S_i \coloneqq F_i/F_{i+1}\), and we have \[\tr_V(a) = \sum_{i=0}^{d-1} \tr_{S_i}(a_i)\]

Proof.

We use induction on \(d\) the length of the composition series. If \(d = 0\) then there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, \(F_{d-1} \neq 0\) and \(x(F_{d-1}) \subseteq F_{d-1} = S_{d-1}\), and hence \(x\) induces a linear map \(\bar{x} \colon V/F_d \to V/F_d\), and if \(B_1\) is a basis of \(F_{d-1}\), extended to a basis of \(V\) by adding the vectors in a set \(B_2\). Then if \(q \colon V \to V/F_d\) is the natural quotient map, \(q(B_2)\) is a basis of \(q(V)\), so that it is clear that \(\tr_V(x) = \tr_{S_{d-1}}(\bar{a}) + \tr_{V/F_{d-1}}(\bar{a})\), and then by induction applied to \(V/F_{d-1}\) and the flag \((F_i/F_{d-1})_{i=0}^{d-1}\) gives the result.

□
Definition 2.2.15.

If \(V\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, we let \(V^s = \sum_{S \leq V} S\) the socle of \(V\), be the sum of all irreducible subrepresentations of \(V\). This is a semisimple subrepresentation of \(V\) and hence it can be written as the direct sum of irreducible subrepresentations of \(V\). It is maximal among semisimple subrepresentations of \(V\) in the partial order given by containment.

2.2.2 Direct sums and Hom-spaces

Now suppose that \(V = V_1 \oplus V_2\) is a \(k\)-vector space. For \(j = 1, 2\), we have natural inclusion maps \(\iota_j \colon V_j \to V\) and projection maps \(p_j \colon V \to V_j\) (with kernel \(V_{3-j}\)). We claim, for any vector space \(U\), we have natural isomorphisms

  1. \(\mathscr{L}(V, U) \cong \mathscr{L}(V_1, U) \oplus \mathscr{L}(V_2, U)\)
  2. \(\mathscr{L}(U, V) \cong \mathscr{L}(U, V_1) \oplus \mathscr{L}(U, V_2)\).

In the case of i), the map simply takes the restriction of \(\phi \in \mathscr{L}(V, U)\) to \(V_1\) and \(V_2\) respectively. In terms of our inclusion and projection maps, for \(r = 1, 2\) we have \(\phi|_{V_r} = \phi \circ \iota_r\). To see that this map is an isomorphism, note that any \(v \in V\) can be written uniquely as \(v = v_1 + v_2\) with \(v_1 \in V_1\) and \(v_2 \in V_2\). Indeed \(v_r = \iota_r \circ p_r(v)\), hence \(\phi(v) = \phi(\iota_1 \circ p_1(v) + \iota_2 \circ p_2(v)) = \sum_{r=1}^2 (\phi \circ \iota_r) \circ p_r(v)\). In other words, the inverse to \(\phi \mapsto (\phi \circ \iota_r)_{r=1,2}\) is the map \((\psi_r)_{r=1,2} \mapsto \sum_{r=1}^2 \psi_r p_r\).

In the case of ii), the morphism simply takes, for \(\phi \in \mathscr{L}(U, V)\), the components of \(\phi(u)\) in \(V_1\) and \(V_2\) respectively, that is \(\phi \mapsto (p_s \circ \phi)_{s=1,2}\). Clearly the inverse of this map is given by \((\eta_s)_{s=1,2} \mapsto \sum_{s=1,2} \iota_s \circ \eta_s\).

Now consider \(\End(V) = \mathscr{L}(V, V)\) where \(V = V_1 \oplus V_2\). We may use i) and ii) (twice) to obtain \[\mathscr{L}(V, V) \cong \bigoplus_{r=1}^{2} \mathscr{L}(V_r, V) \cong \bigoplus_{r=1}^{2} \left( \bigoplus_{s=1}^{2} \mathscr{L}(V_r, V_s) \right) = \bigoplus_{r,s=1}^{2} \mathscr{L}(V_r, V_s)\] by \(\phi \mapsto (\phi^r_s)\) where \(\phi^r_s = p_s \circ \phi \circ \iota_r \in \mathscr{L}(V_r, V_s)\). This decomposition is just that of a matrix into block submatrices, so it can be useful to arrange it in that form: \[\End(V) = \gl_V \ni \phi \mapsto \begin{pmatrix} \phi^1_1 & \phi^1_2 \\ \phi^2_1 & \phi^2_2 \end{pmatrix} \in \begin{pmatrix} \Hom_k(V_1, V_1) = \gl_{V_1} & \Hom_k(V_2, V_1) \\ \Hom_k(V_1, V_2) & \Hom_k(V_2, V_2) = \gl_{V_2} \end{pmatrix}\]

This shows that \(\gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2}\) is naturally isomorphic to a subalgebra of \(\gl_V = \gl_{V_1 \oplus V_2}\), and hence \(V_1 \oplus V_2\) is a representation of \(\gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2}\). More interestingly, the summands \(\mathscr{L}(V_1, V_2)\) and \(\mathscr{L}(V_2, V_1)\) are clearly all preserved by the action of \(\gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2}\), so that in particular, \(\mathscr{L}(V_1, V_2)\) is a representation of \(\gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2}\), where if \(x = (x_1, x_2) \in \gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2}\) and \(\phi \in \mathscr{L}(V_1, V_2)\), then \(x(\phi) = x_2 \circ \phi - \phi \circ x_1\).

It follows that if \((V_1, \rho_1)\) and \((V_2, \rho_2)\) are \(\gg\)-representations, then the composition \[\gg \xrightarrow{\Delta} \gg \oplus \gg \xrightarrow{\rho_1 \oplus \rho_2} \gl_{V_1} \oplus \gl_{V_2} \to \gl(\mathscr{L}(V_1, V_2))\] gives \(\mathscr{L}(V_1, V_2)\) the structure of a \(\gg\)-representation, where \(\Delta \colon \gg \to \gg \oplus \gg\) is the diagonal map \(\Delta(x) = (x, x)\). Explicitly, if \(x \in \gg\) and \(\phi \in \mathscr{L}(V, W)\) then \(x(\phi) = \rho_2(x) \circ \phi - \phi \circ \rho_1(x)\).

Remark 2.2.16.

Note that the previous example actually includes that of dual spaces: if \(V\) is a \(k\)-vector space then \(V^* = \mathscr{L}(V, k)\) becomes a \(\gl_V \oplus \gl_1\)-representation, and then simply using the inclusion \(\gl_V \to \gl_V \oplus \gl_1\) we see that \(V^*\) becomes a \(\gl_V\)-representation, and indeed we obtain the same action: for any \(f \in V^*\) and \(x \in \gl_V\) we have \(x(f) \coloneqq (x, 0)(f) = 0 \circ f - f \circ x = -x^\intercal(f)\).

2.3 Tensor products

Just as with group representations, tensor products of \(\gg\)-representations can be given the structure of a \(\gg\)-representation. The key for this is the following general Lemma:

Lemma 2.3.1.

Let \(\gg_1, \gg_2\) be Lie algebras over \(k\) and let \(\gg = \gg_1 \oplus \gg_2\) be their direct sum (so each of \(\gg_1, \gg_2\) is an ideal in \(\gg\)). If \((U, \rho)\) is a representation of \(\gg\), and we set \(\rho_i = \rho|_{\gg_i}\), then each \(\rho_i\) is a representation of \(\gg_i\) for \(i = 1, 2\), and \([\rho_1(x), \rho_2(y)] = 0\) for any \(x \in \gg_1\), \(y \in \gg_2\). Conversely, if \(\rho_i \colon \gg_i \to \gl_U\) are Lie algebra homomorphisms for \(i = 1, 2\) and \([\rho_1(x), \rho_2(y)] = 0\) for all \(x \in \gg_1\), \(y \in \gg_2\), then \(\rho(x, y) = \rho_1(x) + \rho_2(y)\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism from \(\gg\) to \(\gl_U\).

Proof.

Given a representation \((U, \rho)\) of \(\gg\), the asserted properties of \(\rho_1, \rho_2\) are immediate. For the converse, note that if \((x_1, x_2), (y_1, y_2) \in \gg\), where \(x_1, y_1 \in \gg_1\) and \(x_2, y_2 \in \gg_2\), then \begin{align*} [\rho(x_1, x_2), \rho(y_1, y_2)] &= [\rho_1(x_1) + \rho_2(x_2),\, \rho_1(y_1) + \rho_2(y_2)] \\ &= [\rho_1(x_1), \rho_1(y_1)] + [\rho_1(x_1), \rho_2(y_2)] - [\rho_1(y_1), \rho_2(x_2)] + [\rho_2(x_2), \rho_2(y_2)] \\ &= [\rho_1(x_1), \rho_1(y_1)] + [\rho_2(x_2), \rho_2(y_2)] \\ &= \rho((x_1, x_2), (y_1, y_2)) \end{align*} so that \(\rho\) is a homomorphism as required.

□

Now \(\gl_V\) and \(\gl_W\) are naturally subalgebras of \(\gl_{V \otimes W}\), via the embeddings \(i_V\) and \(i_W\) respectively, where \(i_V(\alpha) = \alpha \otimes 1_W\) and \(i_W(\beta) = 1_V \otimes \beta\) respectively. Since for any \(\alpha \in \gl_V\), \(\beta \in \gl_W\) we have \[i_W(\beta) \circ i_V(\alpha) = (1_V \otimes \beta) \circ (\alpha \otimes 1_W) = \alpha \otimes \beta = (\alpha \otimes 1_W) \circ (1_V \otimes \beta) = i_V(\alpha) \circ i_W(\beta)\] it follows by Lemma 2.3.1 that \(d \colon \gl_V \oplus \gl_W \to \gl_{V \otimes W}\) given by \[d(x, y) = i_V(x) + i_W(y) = x \otimes 1_W + 1_V \otimes y\] is a Lie algebra homomorphism, and hence \(V \otimes W\) is naturally a \(\gl_V \oplus \gl_W\)-representation. It follows immediately that if \((V, \rho)\) is a representation of \(\gg_1\) and \((W, \sigma)\) is a representation of \(\gg_2\) then \(V \otimes W\) is a representation of \(\gg_1 \oplus \gg_2\) via \(d \circ (\rho \oplus \sigma)\) and if \(\gg_1 = \gg_2 = \gg\) then \(V \otimes W\) is a representation of \(\gg\) via \(d \circ \Delta\), where \(\Delta(x) = (x, x) \in \gg \oplus \gg\). More explicitly, if \((V, \rho)\) and \((W, \sigma)\) are \(\gg\)-representations then \(V \otimes W\) is a \(\gg\)-representation with \[x(v \otimes w) = \rho(x) \otimes w + v \otimes \sigma(w), \qquad \forall\, v \in V,\; w \in W.\]

2.3.1 Tensoring with one-dimensional representations

If \((V, \rho)\) is any \(\gg\)-representation, then by Example II.7, we have an isomorphism of vector spaces \(V \otimes k_\lambda \to V\) given by the map \(v \otimes \lambda \mapsto \lambda \cdot v\). Via this map, one can think of the \(\gg\)-representation \(V \otimes k_\lambda\) as the same vector space \(V\) but now equipped with a new action \(\rho_\lambda\) of \(\gg\), where \(\rho_\lambda(x) = \rho(x) + \lambda(x) \cdot I_V\) (where we write \(I_V\) for the identity map). Note that, in particular, if \(\lambda, \mu \in D(\gg)^0\) then this shows that \(k_\lambda \otimes k_\mu \cong k_{\lambda + \mu}\).

2.3.2 Homomorphisms, \(\gg\)-homomorphisms, and tensor products

The properties asserted of the maps described in this section are proved in detail in Appendix II.

Examining the formula (2.3.1) for the action on a tensor product of representations given above we see that, just as for group representations, if \(V\) and \(W\) are \(\gg\)-representations, then the isomorphism \(\sigma \colon V \otimes W \to W \otimes V\) given by \(\sigma(v \otimes w) = w \otimes v\), (\(v \in V\), \(w \in W\)) is compatible with the action of \(\gg\) and hence induces an isomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations. In the case \(V = W\), \(\sigma\) becomes an involution on \(V \otimes V\) commuting with the \(\gg\)-action. In other words, \(S_2\), the symmetric group on two letters acts on \(V \otimes V\) and the isotypic decomposition of \(V \otimes V\) under this action, (equivalently the \((+1)\)- and \((-1)\)-eigenspaces of \(\sigma\)) shows that \(V \otimes V\) is the direct sum of the subrepresentations of symmetric tensors and skew-symmetric tensors, that is \(V \otimes V = \operatorname{Sym}^2(V) \oplus \operatorname{Alt}^2(V)\) where \[\operatorname{Sym}^2(V) = \span_k \left\{\tfrac{1}{2}(v_1 \otimes v_2 + v_2 \otimes v_1) : v_1, v_2 \in V\right\},\] \[\operatorname{Alt}^2(V) = \span_k \left\{\tfrac{1}{2}(v_1 \otimes v_2 - v_2 \otimes v_1) : v_1, v_2 \in V\right\}.\]

Let \(V\) and \(W\) be \(k\)-vector spaces. There is a natural linear map \(\theta \colon V^* \otimes W \to \Hom(V, W)\), given by \(\theta(f \otimes w) = f \cdot w\) where \((f \cdot w)(v) = f(v) \cdot w\) for all \(v \in V\), \(f \in V^*\) and \(w \in W\). This map is injective, and its image is precisely the space of finite-rank linear maps from \(V\) to \(W\). In particular, if \(\dim(V) < \infty\) then we have \(\End(V) \cong V^* \otimes V\). Similarly, there is a natural map \(m \colon V^* \otimes W^* \to (V \otimes W)^*\), where \[m(f \otimes g)(v \otimes w) = f(v) \cdot g(w), \qquad \forall\, v \in V,\; w \in W,\; f \in V^*,\; g \in W^*.\] The map \(m\) is also injective and hence, by considering dimensions, it is an isomorphism when \(V\) and \(W\) are finite-dimensional. This tensor product description of \(\End(V) = \Hom(V, V)\) gives a natural description of the trace map: Notice that we have a natural bilinear map \(V^* \times V \to k\) given by \((f, v) \mapsto f(v)\). By the universal property of the tensor product, this induces a linear map \(\iota \colon V^* \otimes V \to k\). Under the identification with \(\Hom(V, V)\) this map is identified with the trace of a linear map.

Remark 2.3.2.

It is worth noticing that this gives a coordinate-free way of defining the trace, and also some explanation for why one needs some finiteness condition in order for the trace to be defined.

Remark 2.3.3.

If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \(V\) and \(W\) are \(\gg\)-representations, then it is also easy to check from the definitions that the natural map \(\theta \colon V^* \otimes W \to \Hom(V, W)\) defined in Lemma II.10 is also a map of \(\gg\)-representations, as is the contraction map \(\iota \colon V^* \otimes V \to k\), where we view \(k\) as the trivial representation of \(\gg\). For example, for \(\iota\) we have: \[\iota(x(f \otimes v)) = \iota\!\left(x(f) \otimes v + f \otimes x(v)\right) = -f(x(v)) + f(x(v)) = 0, \qquad \forall\, x \in \gg,\; v \in V,\; f \in V^*.\] Thus all the maps between tensor products of vector spaces discussed in Appendix II yield maps of \(\gg\)-representations.

The following example will be very useful in a number of places later in the course.

Example 2.3.4.

If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, then \(\rho\) induces a natural bilinear map \(a_\rho \colon \gg \times V \to V\), namely \((x, v) \mapsto \rho(x)(v)\). By the universal property of tensor products this yields a linear map \(\tilde{a}_\rho \colon \gg \otimes V \to V\). We claim this map is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations (where \(\gg\) is viewed as the adjoint representation). To see this, first notice that the bilinear map \(a_\rho \colon \gg \times V \to V\) is equal to \(a_V \circ (\rho \times 1_V)\) where \(a_V \colon \gl_V \times V \to V\) is the natural action of \(\gl_V\) on \(V\), \((\phi, v) \mapsto \phi(v)\). Thus it suffices to check the claim for \(\gl_V\) and its vector representation \(V\). Let \(\tilde{a}_V \colon \gl_V \otimes V \to V\) be the linear map induced by \(a_V\). Then if \(x, y \in \gl_V\) and \(v \in V\) we have \(x(\tilde{a}_V(y \otimes v)) = x(y(v))\), while \[\tilde{a}_V(x(y \otimes v)) = [x, y] \otimes v + y \otimes x(v) = (xy - yx)(v) + yx(v) = xy(v) = x(\tilde{a}_V(y \otimes v))\] hence as \(y \otimes v\) was arbitrary we have \(\tilde{a}_V \circ x = x \circ \tilde{a}_V\) for all \(x \in \gl_V\) so that \(\tilde{a}_V \in \mathscr{L}_{\gl_V}(\gl_V \otimes V, V)\) as required.

Remark 2.3.5.

In fact one can also deduce that \(\tilde{a}_\rho\) is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations by observing that under the identification \(\gl_V \cong V^* \otimes V\) the map \(\tilde{a}_\rho\) corresponds to the linear map \(\iota_{13} \colon V^* \otimes V \otimes V \to V\), that is, the linear map which is the identity on the 2nd tensor factor and is the contraction map \(\iota\) on the first and third factors: so that \(\iota(f_1 \otimes v_2 \otimes v_3) = f_1(v_3) \cdot v_2\) where \(v_2, v_3 \in V\), \(f_1 \in V^*\). Since \(\iota\) is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations, it follows \(\tilde{a}_\rho\) is also.

Chapter 3. Classifying Lie algebras

The goal of this course is to study the structure of Lie algebras, and attempt to classify them. The most ambitious “classification” result would be to give a description of all finite-dimensional Lie algebras up to isomorphism. In very low dimensions this is actually possible: For dimension 1 clearly there is a unique (up to isomorphism) Lie algebra since the alternating condition demands that the bracket is zero. In dimension two, one can again have an abelian Lie algebra, but there is another possibility: if \(\gg\) has a basis \(\{e, f\}\) then we may set \([e, f] = f\), and this completely determines the Lie algebra structure. All two-dimensional Lie algebras which are not abelian are isomorphic to this one (check this). It is also possible to classify three-dimensional Lie algebras, but it becomes rapidly intractable to do this in general as the dimension increases.

This reveals an essential tension in seeking any kind of classification result for mathematical objects: a classification result should describe all such objects (or at least those in a natural, and likely reasonably “large” class) up to some notion of equivalence. Clearly, using a stricter notion of equivalence will mean any classification theorem you can prove will provide finer information about the objects you are studying, but this must be balanced against the intrinsic complexity of the objects which may make such a classification (even for quite small classes) extremely complicated. Hence it is likely reasonable to accept a somewhat crude notion of equivalence in order to have any chance of obtaining a classification theorem which has a relatively simple statement.

3.1 Classification by composition factors

The proofs in this section are non-examinable, and may not be discussed in detail in lectures. They are included here for completeness.

Our approach will follow the strategy often used in finite groups: In that context, the famous Jordan–Hölder theorem shows that any finite group can be given by gluing together finite simple groups, in the sense that we may find a decreasing chain of subgroups \[ G = G_0 \rhd G_1 \rhd \cdots G_{n-1} \rhd G_n = \{e\}, \] where, for each \(i\), \((1 \leq i \leq n)\), the subgroup \(G_i\) is normal in \(G_{i-1}\) and \(S_i = G_{i-1}/G_i\) is simple. That such a filtration of \(G\) exists is easy to prove by induction. The non-trivial part of the theorem is that, for any fixed finite simple group \(H\), the number of \(S_i\) which are isomorphic to \(H\) is independent of the choice of filtration. This is usually phrased as saying that the multiplicity with which a composition factor \(S_i\) occurs in the sequence \(\{G_{i-1}/G_i : 1 \leq i \leq n\}\) is well-defined.

One can thus give a somewhat crude classification of finite groups, where one considers two finite groups to be equivalent if they have the same composition factors, by giving a classification of finite simple groups. But even the question of classifying finite simple groups is not at all obviously tractable, and answering it was one of the spectacular mathematical achievements of the second half of the twentieth century.

For Lie algebras, we can attempt something similar. In fact, it turns out that, at least in characteristic zero, we obtain a far more complete answer about the structure of an arbitrary finite-dimensional Lie algebra than one could hope to obtain in a Part C course on finite group theory. One aspect of this finer information will reveal a sharp distinction between \(\gl_1\) and the non-abelian Lie algebras which have no proper ideals, which is one reason for the following definition:

Definition 3.1.1.

A nonzero Lie algebra \(\gg\) is said to be almost simple1 if it has no non-trivial proper ideals. If \(\gg\) is almost simple and \(\dim(\gg) > 1\) then we say that \(\gg\) is simple. Equivalently, an almost-simple Lie algebra is simple if it is non-abelian. Thus the only almost-simple Lie algebra which is not simple is \(\gl_1\).

1This is not standard terminology, but it is convenient to use here.

Definition 3.1.2.

A flag or \(\gg\)-flag in a finite-dimensional Lie algebra \(\gg\) is a nested chain \[ \mathcal{C} = (\gg = \gg_0 \trianglerighteq \gg_1 \trianglerighteq \cdots \trianglerighteq \gg_d = 0) \] of subalgebras where, for \(0 \leq i \leq d-1\), the subalgebra \(\gg_{i+1}\) is an ideal in \(\gg_i\) and the quotient \(\mathfrak{c}_k = \gg_k / \gg_{k+1}\) is called a factor of the flag. A flag is strict if the containments \(\gg_{k+1} \subseteq \gg_k\) are all proper, or equivalently, if the factors \(\mathfrak{c}_k\) are all nonzero. A flag \(\mathcal{C}' = (\mathfrak{h}_k)_{k=0}^{l}\) is said to be a refinement of \(\mathcal{C}\) if, for each \(k \in \{1, \ldots, d-1\}\) there exists \(j \in \{1, 2, \ldots, l-1\}\) such that \(\gg_k = \mathfrak{h}_j\). Any flag has an associated strict flag which is obtained by omitting repetitions, that is, if \(S = \{k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, d\} : \gg_k \supsetneq \gg_{k+1}\}\) and \(0 \leq i_0 < i_1 < \cdots < i_t \leq d\) are the elements of \(S\) in increasing order, then the strict flag associated to \(\mathcal{C}\) is \[ \widetilde{\mathcal{C}} = (\gg_{i_s})_{s=0}^{t}. \]

If, for each \(k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, d-1\}\), \(\mathfrak{c}_k\) is almost simple, then \(\mathcal{C}\) is a composition series and in this case the factors \(\gg_i / \gg_{i+1}\) are then called the composition factors of the composition series. If \(\mathfrak{s}\) is an almost simple Lie algebra, we define the multiplicity of \(\mathfrak{s}\) in the composition series \(\mathcal{C}\) to be \[ m_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{s}) := \#\{i \in \{1, \ldots, r\} : \mathfrak{s} \cong \gg_{i-1}/\gg_i\} \]

It is easy to check by induction on dimension that any strict flag in a Lie algebra can be refined to a composition series (one needs to use isomorphism theorems in the same manner as the proof in the context of finite groups). In particular, all finite-dimensional Lie algebras possess composition series.

Proposition 3.1.3.

Suppose that \(\gg\) has a composition series \(\mathcal{C} = (\gg = \gg_0 \rhd \gg_1 \rhd \cdots \rhd \gg_n = 0)\).

  1. Let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be an ideal in \(\gg\), and let \(q : \gg \to \gg/\mathfrak{a}\) be the quotient map. The strict flag \(\mathcal{C}'\) associated to the \(\mathfrak{a}\)-flag \((\mathfrak{a} \cap \gg_k)_{k=0}^{d}\) and the strict flag \(\mathcal{C}''\) associated to the \(\gg/\mathfrak{a}\)-flag \((q(\gg_k))_{k=0}^{d}\) are composition series for \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \(\gg/\mathfrak{a}\) respectively, and for any almost simple Lie algebra \(\mathfrak{s}\) we have \(m_{\mathcal{C}} = m_{\mathcal{C}'} + m_{\mathcal{C}''}\).
  2. If \(\mathfrak{s}\) is an almost simple Lie algebra, the multiplicities \(m_{\mathcal{C}}\) are independent of the choice of composition series and we set \([\mathfrak{s} : \gg] = m_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathfrak{s})\) for any composition series \(\mathcal{C}\) of \(\gg\).
Proof.

Let \(\mathfrak{a}_i := \mathfrak{a} \cap \gg_i\) \((0 \leq i \leq n)\), and let \(p_{i+1} : \gg_i \to \gg_i / \gg_{i+1}\) denote the quotient map from \(\gg_i\) to \(\gg_i/\gg_{i+1}\). Now the terms of the sequence \((\mathfrak{a}_i)_{i=0}^{n}\), while nested, need not be strictly decreasing. Since \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an ideal in \(\gg\), the intersection \(\mathfrak{a}_i = \mathfrak{a} \cap \gg_i\) is an ideal in \(\gg_i\) and, by the second isomorphism theorem, its image under the quotient map \(p_{i+1} : \gg_i \to \gg_i/\gg_{i+1}\) is \[ \mathfrak{a}_i / \mathfrak{a}_{i+1} \cong (\mathfrak{a}_i + \gg_{i+1})/\gg_{i+1} = p_{i+1}(\mathfrak{a}_i) \tag{3.1.1} \]

Similarly, we may consider the sequence \((q(\gg_i))_{i=0}^{n}\), where \(q : \gg \to \gg/\mathfrak{a}\) is the quotient map, then \(q(\gg_{i+1})\) is an ideal in \(q(\gg_i)\), and by the second isomorphism theorem \(q(\gg_i) \cong \gg_i / \gg_i \cap \mathfrak{a} = \gg_i / \mathfrak{a}_i\). Under this identification, \(q(\gg_{i+1})\) is isomorphic to \((\gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i)/\mathfrak{a}_i\), and hence \[ q(\gg_i)/q(\gg_{i+1}) \cong (\gg_i/\mathfrak{a}_i) \big/ (\gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i)/\mathfrak{a}_i \cong \gg_i/(\gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i). \tag{3.1.2} \]

Thus since \(\gg_i/\gg_{i+1}\) is almost simple and \(\gg_{i+1} \subseteq \gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i \subseteq \gg_i\), we must either have \(\gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i = \gg_i\), in which case Equations (3.1.1) and (3.1.2) show that \(q(\gg_i) = q(\gg_{i+1})\) and \(\mathfrak{a}_i/\mathfrak{a}_{i+1} \cong \gg_i/\gg_{i+1}\), or \(\gg_{i+1} + \mathfrak{a}_i = \gg_{i+1}\), in which case \(\mathfrak{a}_i = \mathfrak{a}_{i+1}\) and \(q(\gg_i)/q(\gg_{i+1}) \cong \gg_i/\gg_{i+1}\).

Thus removing repetitions from the sequences \((\mathfrak{a}_i)\) and \((q(\gg_i))\) yields composition series \(\mathcal{C}'\) and \(\mathcal{C}''\) for \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \(\gg/\mathfrak{a}\) respectively, where the composition factors of \(\mathcal{C}\) correspond to a composition factor of precisely one of \(\mathcal{C}'\) or \(\mathcal{C}''\).

For the final part, we use induction on the minimal length \(\ell(\gg)\) of a composition series for \(\gg\). Suppose that \(\mathcal{C} = (\gg_0 \rhd \gg_1 \rhd \cdots \rhd \gg_d = \{0\})\) is a composition series for \(\gg\) with \(d = \ell(\gg)\).
