Let \(\mathcal{D} = (\gg = \mathfrak{h}_0 \rhd \mathfrak{h}_1 \cdots \rhd \mathfrak{h}_l = \{0\})\) be another composition series for \(\gg\). Then we may apply the previous part to \(\mathcal{C}\) and \(\mathcal{D}\) with \(\mathfrak{a} = \gg_1\) to obtain composition series \(\mathcal{C}', \mathcal{D}'\) of \(\gg_1\) and \(\mathcal{C}'', \mathcal{D}''\) of \(\gg/\gg_1\). Now \(\mathcal{C}' = (\gg_k)_{k=1}^{d}\) and since any composition series of \(\gg_1\) of length \(r\) immediately yields one for \(\gg\) with length \(r+1\), we must have \(\ell(\gg_1) = d - 1\), and hence by induction \(m_{\mathcal{C}'} = m_{\mathcal{D}'}\). Moreover, since \(\gg/\gg_1\) is almost simple, it has a unique multiplicity function, so that \(m_{\mathcal{C}''} = m_{\mathcal{D}''}\). The result then follows immediately from part i).

□
Remark 3.1.4.

Later in this course, we will, at least in characteristic zero, establish a finer description of the structure of semisimple Lie algebras than the above result provides: we will show that any Lie algebra \(\gg\) contains an ideal \(\mathfrak{r}\) whose only composition factor is \(\gl_1\) (and therefore the multiplicity of that composition factor is just \(\dim(\mathfrak{r})\)) while the quotient, \(\gg/\mathfrak{r}\), is a direct sum of non-abelian simple Lie algebras.

3.2 Exact sequences of Lie algebras

Definition 3.2.1.

We say that the sequence of Lie algebras and Lie algebra homomorphisms \[ \gg_1 \xrightarrow{i} \gg \xrightarrow{q} \gg_2 \] is exact at \(\gg\) if \(\im(i) = \ker(q)\). A sequence of maps \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \gg_1 \xrightarrow{i} \gg \xrightarrow{q} \gg_2 \xrightarrow{} 0 \] is called a short exact sequence if it is exact at each of \(\gg_1\), \(\gg\) and \(\gg_2\), so that \(i\) is injective, \(q\) is surjective and \(\im(i) = \ker(q)\). In this case, we say that \(\gg\) is an extension of \(\gg_2\) by \(\gg_1\). The existence of a composition series for a finite-dimensional Lie algebra shows that any such Lie algebra is constructed through successive extensions by almost simple Lie algebras.

Definition 3.2.2.

An extension of Lie algebras \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \gg_1 \xrightarrow{i} \gg \xrightarrow{q} \gg_2 \xrightarrow{} 0 \] is said to be split if there is a homomorphism of Lie algebras \(s : \gg_2 \to \gg\) such that \(q \circ s = \operatorname{id}_{\gg_2}\).

Notice that in this case the image \(s(\gg_2)\) of the splitting map \(s\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\) which is isomorphic to \(\gg_2\) and is complementary to \(i(\gg_1)\), in the sense that \(\gg = i(\gg_1) \oplus s(\gg_2)\) as vector spaces. Indeed the homomorphism \(s\) is determined by \(s(\gg_2)\), its image, because it is the inverse of \(q|_{s(\gg)}\), the restriction of \(q\) to that image. Moreover, since \(i(\gg_1)\) is an ideal of \(\gg\), the adjoint action of \(\gg\) preserves \(i(\gg_1)\), and so it restricts to give an action of \(s(\gg_2)\) on \(i(\gg_1)\). This completely describes the Lie bracket on \(\gg\): For any \(x, y \in \gg\), there are unique \(x_1, y_1 \in \gg_1\) and \(x_2, y_2 \in \gg_2\) such that \(x = i(x_1) + s(x_2)\), \(y = i(y_1) + s(y_2)\). Then \[ [x, y] = [i(x_1) + s(x_2),\, i(y_1) + s(y_2)] = i([x_1, x_2]) + \ad(s(x_2))(i(y_1)) - \ad(s(y_2))(i(x_1)) + s([x_2, y_2]). \]

This motivates the following definition:

Definition 3.2.3.

Suppose that \(\gg, \hh\) are Lie algebras, and we have a homomorphism \(\phi : \gg \to \Der_k(\hh)\), the Lie algebra of derivations2 on \(\hh\). Then it is straightforward to check that we can form a new Lie algebra \(\hh \rtimes \gg\), the semi-direct product3 of \(\gg\) and \(\hh\) by \(\phi\), which as a vector space is just \(\gg \oplus \hh\), and where the Lie bracket is given by: \[ [(x_1, y_1), (x_2, y_2)] = ([x_1, x_2] + \phi(y_1)(x_2) - \phi(y_2)(x_1),\, [y_1, y_2]), \] where \(x_1, x_2 \in \hh\), \(y_1, y_2 \in \gg\). The Lie algebra \(\hh\), viewed as the subspace \(\{(x, 0) : x \in \hh\}\) of \(\hh \rtimes \gg\), is clearly an ideal of \(\hh \rtimes \gg\). Since it does not intersect \(\hh\), the quotient map \(q : \hh \rtimes \gg \to (\hh \rtimes \gg)/\hh\) induces an isomorphism \(\gg \to (\hh \rtimes \gg)/\hh\), hence \(\hh \rtimes \gg\) is a split extension of \(\gg\) by \(\hh\). It is not difficult to check that any split extension is of this form.

2Recall that the derivations of a Lie algebra are the linear maps \(\alpha : \hh \to \hh\) such that \(\alpha([x, y]) = [\alpha(x), y] + [x, \alpha(y)]\).

3This is the Lie algebra analogue of the semidirect product of groups, where you build a group \(H \rtimes G\) via a map from \(G\) to the automorphisms (rather than derivations) of \(H\).

Example 3.2.4.

Let \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) be a 2-dimensional Lie algebra with basis \(\{x, y\}\). Clearly \(D(\mathfrak{s}_2)\) is spanned by \([x, y]\). Thus either \(D(\mathfrak{s}_2) = 0\) and \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) is abelian, in which case any 1-dimensional subspace is an ideal, or \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) has a unique nonzero proper ideal, namely \(D(\mathfrak{s}_2)\). For any 1-dimensional ideal \(I\) in \(\mathfrak{s}_2\), if we pick \(z \in I \setminus \{0\}\) and \(w \notin I\), so that \(\{z, w\}\) is a basis of \(\mathfrak{s}_2\), then setting \(i(\lambda) = \lambda z\) and \(q(\lambda z + \mu w) = \mu\), we obtain a short exact sequence: \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \gl_1 \xrightarrow{i} \mathfrak{s}_2 \xrightarrow{q} \gl_1 \xrightarrow{} 0 \]

Now clearly this sequence is split, because if we define \(s : \gl_1 \to \mathfrak{s}_2\) by \(s(\lambda) = \lambda w\), for all \(\lambda \in k\), then \(q \circ s = 1_{\gl_1}\) and \(s\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism by vii) of Example 1.1.5. It follows that all two-dimensional Lie algebras can be realised as a semi-direct product of \(\gl_1\) with itself.

Now such a semidirect product is given by a homomorphism of Lie algebras \(\phi : \gl_1 \to \Der_k(\gl_1)\). But if \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an abelian Lie algebra, \(\Der_k(\mathfrak{a}) = \gl_{\mathfrak{a}}\), since any linear map is a derivation for the zero Lie bracket. Thus \(\Der_k(\gl_1) = \gl_{\gl_1} = \gl_1\), and \(\phi : \gl_1 \to \gl_1\) is just a scalar. It is then easy to check that if \(\phi\) is zero we obtain the 2-dimensional abelian Lie algebra, while if \(\phi \neq 0\) we obtain Lie algebras which are all isomorphic, as can be checked by, for example, rescaling the map \(i\).

Example 3.2.5.

Generalising the previous example, notice that any short exact sequence of the form \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \gg_1 \xrightarrow{i} \gg \xrightarrow{q} \gl_1 \xrightarrow{} 0 \] is automatically split. Indeed if we pick any \(x \in \gg\) with \(q(x) = 1 \in \gl_1(k)\) then setting \(s(\lambda) = \lambda x\) it is immediate that \(q \circ s = \operatorname{id}\), and, as in the previous example, \(s : \gl_1 \to \gg\) is automatically a homomorphism of Lie algebras. It follows that \(\gg\) is a semidirect product \(\gg_1 \rtimes \gl_1(k)\). It is important to note that, as this example shows, a splitting map can be far from unique: the set of \(x \in \gg\) with \(q(x) \neq 0\) all provide different splitting maps for \(q\) in the above short exact sequence.

Remark 3.2.6.

There is a close analogy with the notion of a short exact sequence of groups which you have seen in a previous course: here one has a sequence \[ 1 \xrightarrow{} G_1 \xrightarrow{i} G \xrightarrow{q} G_2 \xrightarrow{} 1 \] where we write \(1\) for the trivial group (rather than \(0\) for the trivial Lie algebra). Exactness at \(G\) means that \(\im(i) = \ker(q)\), and similarly at \(G_1\) and \(G_2\), so that \(i\) is injective and \(q\) is surjective. In Part A Groups you show that this sequence is split, that is, there exists a splitting map \(s : G_2 \to G\) such that \(q \circ s = \operatorname{id}_{G_2}\), if and only if \(G \cong G_1 \rtimes G_2\).

Remark 3.2.7.

There will be one other kind of extension which will play an important role in this course. This is an extension \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \mathfrak{z}_1 \xrightarrow{i} \gg \xrightarrow{q} \gg_2 \xrightarrow{} 0 \] where the image of \(\mathfrak{z}_1\) is assumed to lie in \(\mathfrak{z}(\gg) = \{x \in \gg : [x, y] = 0,\, \forall\, y \in \gg\}\), the centre of \(\gg\). Such extensions are (unsurprisingly) called central extensions.

Split and central extensions are in a loose sense complementary to each other: An extension of \(\gg_2\) by \(\gg_1\) which is both central and split is just the direct sum \(\gg_1 \oplus \gg_2\), where \(\gg_1 \cong \gl_1^{\oplus k}\) and \(k = \dim_k(\gg_1)\).

Example 3.2.8.

Let \(\mathfrak{n}_3 = \{x = (x_{ij}) \in \gl_3 : x_{ij} = 0\;\; \forall\, i \geq j\}\) be the Lie algebra of strictly upper triangular \(3 \times 3\) matrices. If we set \(x = E_{12}\), \(y = E_{23}\) and \(z = E_{13}\) then \([x, y] = z\) and \(z\) is central. Indeed \(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{n}_3) = k \cdot z\), and \(\mathfrak{n}_3/\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{n}_3)\) is a 2-dimensional abelian Lie algebra, and thus \[ 0 \xrightarrow{} \mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{n}_3) \xrightarrow{i} \mathfrak{n}_3 \xrightarrow{q} \gl_1^{\oplus 2} \xrightarrow{} 0 \]

It follows that \(\mathfrak{n}_3\) is a central extension of \(\gl_1^{\oplus 2}\), the two-dimensional abelian Lie algebra. Note that this short exact sequence does not split: indeed if \(s : \gl_1^{\oplus 2} \to \mathfrak{n}_3\) is a Lie algebra homomorphism and \(s(q(x)) = x_1\), \(s(q(y)) = x_2\) where \(x_1 = a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1 z\) and \(x_2 = a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2 z\), then \([x_1, x_2] = [a_1 x + b_1 y + c_1 z,\, a_2 x + b_2 y + c_2 z] = (a_1 b_2 - a_2 b_1)z\), so that \([x_1, x_2] = 0\) if and only if the matrix \(\begin{pmatrix} a_1 & b_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 \end{pmatrix}\) is singular, but then \(q(\im(s))\) is at most 1-dimensional, thus \(q \circ s\) cannot be equal to the identity on \(\gl_1^{\oplus 2}\).

On the other hand, if we let \(\mathfrak{a} = k \cdot x \oplus k \cdot z\) then \(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{n}_3) \subset \mathfrak{a} \subset \mathfrak{n}_3\) is a complete flag of ideals in \(\mathfrak{n}_3\) (i.e. a nested chain of ideals one of each dimension between \(1\) and \(\dim(\mathfrak{n}_3)\)) so that \(\mathfrak{n}_3\) is an iterated semidirect product \((k \cdot z \rtimes k \cdot x) \rtimes k \cdot y\).

Chapter 4. Solvable and nilpotent Lie algebras

Conventions: From this point onwards in these notes, we will assume that all Lie algebras and all representations are finite-dimensional over the field \(\kfield\), unless the contrary is explicitly stated, and from §4.3 onwards, \(\kfield\) will be algebraically closed of characteristic zero.

We now begin to study particular classes of Lie algebras. The first class we study, solvable Lie algebras, in terms of the discussion on classification of Lie algebras in the previous section, can be given as the class of Lie algebras which can be built using only \(\gl_1\), the simplest Lie algebra which possesses only the structure of the base field \(\kfield\) and the trivial Lie bracket.

4.1 Solvable Lie algebras

4.1.1 Definition and basic properties

Definition 4.1.1.

A Lie algebra \(\gg\) is solvable if its only composition factor is \(\gl_1(\kfield)\). This is equivalent to the condition that \(\gg\) has a nested sequence of subalgebras \[\gg = \gg_0 \supsetneq \gg_1 \supsetneq \cdots \supsetneq \gg_d = \{0\},\] where \(\gg_{k+1}\) is an ideal in \(\gg_k\) and \(\gg_k / \gg_{k+1}\) is abelian for each \(k\) (\(0 \le k \le d-1\)). Indeed if such a sequence of subalgebras exists, any refinement of it to a composition series will have \(\gl_1(\kfield)\) as its only composition factor, and conversely, a composition series with \(\gl_1(\kfield)\) as its only composition factor is an example of such a sequence of subalgebras.

If \(\gg = \gg_0 \supset \gg_1 \supset \cdots \supset \gg_n = \{0\}\) is a composition series for \(\gg\) with \(\gg_k / \gg_{k+1} \cong \gl_1\) for each \(k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\}\), so that \(\dim(\gg) = n\), then we have \(\gg_{n-1} \cong \gl_1\), and, for each \(k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, n-1\}\), we have a short exact sequence \[0 \longrightarrow \gg_{k+1} \xrightarrow{\iota_{k+1}} \gg_k \xrightarrow{q_k} \gl_1 \longrightarrow 0\] where \(\iota_{k+1}\) is the inclusion map and \(q_k\) the quotient map. Thus \(\gg_{k-1}\) is an extension of \(\gl_1\) by \(\gg_k\). By Remark 3.2.6, this short exact sequence must split, and so \(\gg_k\) is a semidirect product of \(\gg_{k+1}\) by \(\gl_1(\kfield)\), and so solvable Lie algebras are precisely the Lie algebras one obtains from the zero Lie algebra by taking iterated semidirect products with \(\gl_1(\kfield)\).

Example 4.1.2.
Example 3.2.4 shows that \(\mathfrak{s}_2\), the 2-dimensional non-abelian Lie algebra, is solvable.

We can rephrase the condition that a Lie algebra \(\gg\) is solvable in terms of a decreasing sequence of ideals in \(\gg\):

Definition 4.1.3.
Recall from Remark 1.2.5 that the derived subalgebra \(D(\gg)\) of \(\gg\) is defined to be the ideal \([\gg, \gg]\). We define the nested decreasing sequence of ideals \((D^k(\gg))_{k \ge 0}\) by setting \(D^k(\gg) = \gg\) for \(k = 0\) and \(D^k(\gg) = D(D^{k-1}(\gg)) = [D^{k-1}(\gg), D^{k-1}(\gg)]\) for \(k > 0\). The sequence of ideals \((D^k(\gg))_{k \ge 0}\) is called the derived series of \(\gg\).
Lemma 4.1.4.
Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra. Then \(D(\gg)\) is the smallest ideal in \(\gg\) such that \(\gg/D(\gg)\) is abelian. In particular, \(\gg\) is solvable precisely when the derived series \((D^k(\gg))_{k \ge 1}\) satisfies \(D^k(\gg) = 0\) for sufficiently large \(k\).
Proof.

For the first claim, suppose that \(I\) is an ideal for which \(\gg/I\) is abelian. Then, for all \(x, y \in \gg\), we must have \([x, y] \in I\), and hence \(D(\gg) \subseteq I\). Since this also shows \(\gg/D(\gg)\) is abelian, the claim follows.

Next note that we have a short exact sequence \[0 \longrightarrow D(\gg) \longrightarrow \gg \longrightarrow \gg/D(\gg) \longrightarrow 0\] that is, \(\gg\) is an extension of the abelian Lie algebra \(\gg/D(\gg)\) by \(D(\gg)\). It follows that if \(D^k(\gg) = \{0\}\) for some \(k\), then \(\gg\) has a filtration by ideals for which the subquotients are abelian, so it is certainly solvable. Conversely, if \(\gg\) is solvable, so that we have a nested sequence of subalgebras \(\gg = \gg_0 \supset \gg_1 \supset \cdots \supset \gg_n = \{0\}\), where \(\gg_{k+1}\) is an ideal in \(\gg_k\) and \(\gg_k / \gg_{k+1}\) is abelian. But then \(D(\gg_k) = [\gg_k, \gg_k] \subseteq \gg_{k+1}\), and so since \(\gg = \gg_0\), by induction it follows that \(D^k(\gg) \subseteq \gg_k\), and hence for \(k \ge n\) we have \(D^k(\gg) = 0\).

□
Remark 4.1.5.
Because the terms of the derived series are ideals in \(\gg\), it follows that if \(\gg\) is solvable, then there is a filtration of \(\gg\) whose terms are ideals in \(\gg\) not just subalgebras each of which is an ideal in the previous term of the filtration. In particular, if \(\gg\) is solvable, it follows \(\gg\) has a non-trivial abelian ideal, since the last non-zero term of the derived series must be such an ideal.
Definition 4.1.6.

If \(\gg\) is an arbitrary Lie algebra, we set \[D^\infty(\gg) = \bigcap_{k \ge 0} D^k(\gg)\] the "limit" of the derived series \((D^k(\gg))_{k \ge 0}\). Since \([D^k(\gg), D^k(\gg)] = D^{k+1}(\gg)\) it follows that \(D(D^\infty(\gg)) = \bigcap_{k \ge 1} D^k(\gg) = D^\infty(\gg)\).

If \(\dim_\kfield(\gg) < \infty\), then the sequence \((d_k)\) where \(d_k = \dim_\kfield(D^k(\gg))\) is weakly decreasing and bounded below by 0, hence \(\{m \in \NN : d_m < d_{m-1}\}\) is finite and if we let \(N\) be its maximal element then \(D^N(\gg) = D^{N+1}(\gg) = D^m(\gg)\) for all \(m \ge N\), and so \(D^\infty(\gg) = D^N(\gg)\), i.e. the derived series stabilizes to its limit at \(k = N\).

We say a Lie algebra \(\gg\) is perfect if it is nonzero and \(\gg = D(\gg)\), so that its derived series stabilizes immediately. If \(\gg\) is a finite-dimensional Lie algebra then since \(D^\infty(\gg) = D(D^\infty(\gg))\) we see that if \(\gg\) is not solvable then the limit of its derived series \(D^\infty(\gg)\) is a perfect ideal in \(\gg\). But if \(\aa \subseteq \gg\) is a perfect subalgebra in \(\gg\) then \(D(\gg) \supseteq D(\aa) = \aa\) and hence by induction \(D^k(\gg) \supseteq \aa\) for all \(k\), so that \(\aa \subseteq D^\infty(\gg)\). It follows that \(D^\infty(\gg)\) is the largest perfect subalgebra in \(\gg\) and hence we see that \[\gg \text{ not solvable} \iff \exists \text{ a perfect ideal } I \trianglelefteq \gg \iff \exists \text{ a perfect subalgebra } \hh \subseteq \gg\]

Recall the notion of a flag \(\mathcal{F} = (V = F_0 > \cdots F_d = \{0\})\) from Definition 2.2.3.

Lemma 4.1.7.

Let \(V\) be a finite dimensional vector space and let \(\mathcal{F} = (V = F_0 \supset F_1 \supset \cdots \supset F_d = \{0\})\) be a flag in \(V\), and set \(F_n = \{0\}\) if \(n \ge d\). Let, for any \(r \in \ZZ_{\ge 0}\), \[\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}} = \{x \in \gl_V : x(F_i) \subseteq F_{i+r},\ \forall\, i,\ 0 \le i \le d\}.\]

  1. If \(k, l \ge 0\), then \([\bb^k_{\mathcal{F}}, \bb^l_{\mathcal{F}}] \subseteq \bb^{k+l}_{\mathcal{F}}\).
  2. If \(\mathcal{F}\) is a complete flag, and \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}} = \bb^0_{\mathcal{F}}\), then \(D(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}) \subseteq \bb^1_{\mathcal{F}}\) and moreover \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}\) is solvable.
Proof.

First note that \(\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}} \subseteq \bb^s_{\mathcal{F}}\) if \(r \ge s\), and that if \(x \in \bb^k_{\mathcal{F}}\), \(y \in \bb^l_{\mathcal{F}}\), then clearly \(x \circ y\) and \(y \circ x\) lie in \(\bb^{k+l}_{\mathcal{F}}\).

It follows that the \(\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}}\) form a descending sequence of associative subalgebras of \(\End_\kfield(V)\), where the \(\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}}\) for \(r > 0\) are two-sided ideals in \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}} = \bb^0_{\mathcal{F}}\), since \(\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}} \cdot \bb^s_{\mathcal{F}} \subseteq \bb^{r+s}_{\mathcal{F}}\). But this immediately implies (i), that is, \([\bb^r_{\mathcal{F}}, \bb^s_{\mathcal{F}}] \subseteq \bb^{r+s}_{\mathcal{F}}\).

If \(\mathcal{F}\) is a complete flag, and \(x, y \in \bb_{\mathcal{F}}\), then for any \(i\), (\(1 \le i \le d\)), \(x\) and \(y\) induce linear maps on \(F_i / F_{i+1}\), and, since \(\mathcal{F}\) is complete, \(\dim(F_i / F_{i+1}) = 1\), so that \(\gl_{F_i / F_{i+1}}\) is abelian, and thus the map induced by \([x, y]\) on \(F_i / F_{i+1}\) is zero. But this exactly says that \([x, y] \in \bb^1_{\mathcal{F}}\), and hence \(D(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}) \subseteq \bb^1_{\mathcal{F}}\). But then \(D^k(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}) \subseteq D^{k-1}(\bb^1_{\mathcal{F}})\), and using (i) and induction \(D^{k-1}(\bb^1_{\mathcal{F}}) \subseteq \bb^{2^{k-1}}_{\mathcal{F}}\), which is \(\{0\}\) if \(2^{k-1} \ge d = \dim(V)\), and hence \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}\) is solvable.

□

We will see shortly that, in characteristic zero, any solvable linear Lie algebra \(\gg \subset \gl_V\), where \(V\) is finite dimensional, is a subalgebra of \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}\) for some complete flag \(\mathcal{F}\). We next note some basic properties of solvable Lie algebras. We establish them using the characterization of solvability in terms of the derived series, but it is also straight-forward to show them using composition series.

Lemma 4.1.8.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra, \(\phi : \gg \to \hh\) a homomorphism of Lie algebras.

  1. We have, for all \(k \ge 0\) \[\phi(D^k(\gg)) = D^k(\phi(\gg)) \subseteq D^k(\hh).\] In particular \(\phi(\gg)\) is solvable if \(\gg\) or \(\hh\) is and thus any quotient or subalgebra of a solvable Lie algebra is solvable.
  2. If \(\im(\phi)\) and \(\ker(\phi)\) are solvable then so is \(\gg\). Thus if \(I\) is an ideal and \(I\) and \(\gg/I\) are solvable, so is \(\gg\).
Proof.

For the claims about the terms of the derived series we use induction on \(k\), the case \(k = 0\) being trivial. If the result is known for \(k\) then we have \[\phi(D^{k+1}(\gg)) = \phi([D^k(\gg), D^k(\gg)]) = [\phi(D^k(\gg)), \phi(D^k(\gg))]\] \[= [D^k(\phi(\gg)), D^k(\phi(\gg))]\] \[\subseteq [D^k(\hh), D^k(\hh)] = D^{k+1}(\hh).\] where we use the inductive hypothesis for the equality on the second line and the containment in the third. Since \([D^k(\phi(\gg)), D^k(\phi(\gg))] = D^{k+1}(\phi(\gg))\). The assertions in the final sentence of (i) now follow by applying the compatibility of the derived series to the case where \(\phi\) is a quotient map and the inclusion of a subalgebra respectively.

For (ii) note that if \(\im(\phi)\) is solvable then for some \(k_1 \ge 0\) we have \(\phi(D^{k_1}(\gg)) = D^{k_1}(\phi(\gg)) = \{0\}\) and hence \(D^{k_1}(\gg) \subseteq \ker(\phi)\). But then as \(\ker(\phi)\) is solvable we have \(D^{k_2}(\ker(\phi)) = \{0\}\) and hence \[D^{k_1 + k_2}(\gg) = D^{k_2}(D^{k_1}(\gg)) \subseteq D^{k_2}(\ker(\phi)) = \{0\}\] and hence \(\gg\) is solvable as required.

□

4.2 Nilpotent Lie algebras

In this section we continue our study of Lie algebras which are built from \(\gl_1\), but now by using central extensions rather than arbitrary extensions.

Definition 4.2.1.
A Lie algebra \(\gg\) is said to be nilpotent if it can be obtained from \(0\), the trivial Lie algebra, by iterated central extensions. If \(\gg\) can be obtained by precisely \(k\) iterated extensions, we say \(\gg\) is \(k\)-step nilpotent. Thus, for example, a Lie algebra is 1-step nilpotent if and only if it is abelian.

To make this more concrete, suppose that \(\gg\) is a nilpotent Lie algebra. Then, for some \(k \ge 0\) there are abelian Lie algebras \((\mathfrak{c}_i)_{i=0}^k\) and, for each \(i \ge 1\) a short exact sequence \[0 \longrightarrow \mathfrak{c}_{k+1} \xrightarrow{p_i} \gg_k \xrightarrow{q_i} \gg_{k-1} \longrightarrow 0\] where \(\gg_0 = \mathfrak{c}_0\) and \(\mathfrak{c}_i \subseteq \mathfrak{z}(\gg_i)\), that is, \(\gg_i\) is a central extension of \(\gg_{i-1}\) by \(\mathfrak{c}_i\), and \(\gg = \gg_k\). It follows that \(q_k : \gg = \gg_k \to \gg_{k-1}\), and if we set \(Q_i = q_{i+1} \circ q_{i+1} \circ \cdots \circ q_k\), then \(Q_i : \gg \to \gg_i\) exhibits \(\gg_i\) as a quotient of \(\gg\). Set \(\mathfrak{q}_i = \ker(Q_i)\), so that if we set \(\mathfrak{q}_0 = \gg\), then \((\mathfrak{q}_i)_{i=0}^k\) gives a descending sequence of ideals in \(\gg\), and \(\mathfrak{q}_i / \mathfrak{q}_{i-1} \cong \mathfrak{c}_i\) is central in \(\gg / \mathfrak{q}_{i-1}\). The sequence of central extensions constructing \(\gg\) can thus be reconstructed from the sequence of ideals \((\mathfrak{q}_i)_{i=0}^k\).

Definition 4.2.2.
For \(\gg\) a Lie algebra, let \(C^0(\gg) = \gg\), and \(C^i(\gg) = [\gg, C^{i-1}(\gg)]\) for \(i \ge 1\). This sequence of ideals of \(\gg\) is called the lower central series of \(\gg\).
Remark 4.2.3.
Notice that \(C^1(\gg) = [\gg, \gg]\) is the derived subalgebra \(D(\gg)\) though subsequent terms of the lower central series will normally be distinct from the terms of the derived series.
Proposition 4.2.4.

Suppose that \(\gg\) is nilpotent and \((\mathfrak{q}_i)_{i=0}^k\) the sequence of ideals associated to a realization of \(\gg\) as an iterated sequence of central extensions. Then

  1. For each \(i \ge 0\) we have \(C^i(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{q}_i\) and hence \(C^k(\gg) = 0\).
  2. Conversely, if \(\gg\) is such that, for some \(N \ge 0\) we have \(C^N(\gg) = 0\), then \(\gg\) is at most \(N\)-step nilpotent.
Proof.

Suppose \(\gg\) is any Lie algebra, and \(\bb \subseteq \aa\) are ideals in \(\gg\). If \(\aa/\bb\) is central in \(\gg/\bb\), then for any \(x \in \gg\) and \(y \in \aa\) we must have \([x, y] \in \bb\) and hence \([\gg, \aa] \subseteq \bb\). Since \(\aa / [\gg, \aa]\) is certainly central in \(\gg / [\gg, \aa]\) it follows that \([\gg, \aa]\) is the smallest ideal of \(\gg\) contained in \(\aa\) for which \(\aa\) becomes central in the quotient algebra.

Applying this observation to \(C^i(\gg)\) inductively yields (i). For (ii), the converse, observe that the previous paragraph also shows that \[0 \longrightarrow C^k(\gg)/C^{k+1}(\gg) \longrightarrow \gg/C^{k+1}(\gg) \longrightarrow \gg/C^k(\gg) \longrightarrow 0\] exhibits \(\gg / C^{k+1}(\gg)\) as a central extension of \(\gg / C^k(\gg)\). It follows that if \(C^N(\gg) = 0\) for some \(N\) then \(\gg\) is at most \(N\)-step nilpotent.

□
Lemma 4.2.5.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and let \(\phi : \gg \to \hh\) be a homomorphism of Lie algebras. Then

  1. \(\phi(C^k(\gg)) = C^k(\phi(\gg)) \subseteq C^k(\hh)\).

    In particular, any subalgebra or quotient of a \(k\)-step nilpotent Lie algebra is \(l\)-step nilpotent for some \(l \le k\).

  2. If \(\gg\) is nilpotent then \(\mathfrak{z}(\gg) \ne \{0\}\) and \(\gg / \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\) is nilpotent if and only if \(\gg\) is nilpotent.
Proof.

For part (i), we use induction on \(k\), where the case \(k = 0\) is trivial. Now \[\phi(C^{k+1}(\gg)) = \phi([\gg, C^k(\gg)]) = [\phi(\gg), \phi(C^k(\gg))] = [\phi(\gg), C^k(\phi(\gg))] \subseteq [\hh, C^k(\hh)] = C^{k+1}(\hh)\] where we use the inductive hypothesis in the third equality and the containment.

For part (ii), it is clear that \(\mathfrak{z}(\gg)\) is always non-zero for \(\gg\) a non-zero nilpotent Lie algebra, indeed if \(\gg\) is \(k\)-step nilpotent then \([\gg, C^{k-1}(\gg)] = C^k(\gg) = \{0\}\) and hence \(C^{k-1}(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\). Moreover, this shows that \(\gg / \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\) is a quotient of \(\gg / C^{k-1}(\gg)\) and hence is \((k-1)\)-step nilpotent.

□
Remark 4.2.6.
Notice that if \(\aa\) is an arbitrary ideal in \(\gg\), and \(\aa\) and \(\gg/\aa\) are nilpotent it does not follow that \(\gg\) is nilpotent. Indeed recall from Example 3.2.4 the non-abelian 2-dimensional Lie algebra \(\mathfrak{s}_2\), with basis \(\{x, y\}\) where \([x, y] = y\). Then \(\kfield \cdot y\) is a 1-dimensional ideal in \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) but it is not central. Indeed \(\mathfrak{z}(\mathfrak{s}_2) = 0\) so \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) is not nilpotent, even though the ideal \(\kfield \cdot y\) and the quotient \(\mathfrak{s}_2 / \kfield \cdot y\) are (since they are both abelian). Note that this shows that \(\mathfrak{s}_2\) cannot be written as a central extension of \(\gl_1\) by itself.
Remark 4.2.7.

The characterisation of the property of nilpotence in terms of the lower central series is similar to the characterisation of solvable Lie algebras in terms of the derived series. This is one reason it is commonly used. There is, however, another natural nested sequence of ideals which can be used to characterize nilpotence: If \(\gg\) is any Lie algebra, set \(Z_0(\gg) = \gg\), and, assuming \(Z_k(\gg)\) is defined, let \(q_k : \gg \to \gg / Z_k(\gg)\) be the quotient map, and set \(Z_{k+1}(\gg) = q_k^{-1}(\mathfrak{z}(\gg_k))\). This process yields an increasing sequence of ideals of \(\gg\) known as the upper central series. If it exhausts \(\gg\), that is, if for some \(n \ge 0\) we have \(Z_k(\gg) = \gg\) for all \(k\) large enough, then \(\gg\) is nilpotent. If \(\gg\) is not nilpotent, the upper central series will stabilize at a maximal nilpotent ideal of \(\gg\). Note that if \(\gg\) is \(k\)-step nilpotent, then \(Z_{k-1}(\gg) \subsetneq Z_k(\gg) = \gg\) but the ideals which occur in the upper central series need not coincide with those which occur in the lower central series.

In terms of the adjoint representation, the centre of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) can be viewed as \(\ker(\ad)\), the kernel of the adjoint action, but it can also be viewed as the invariants in \(\gg\), that is \[\gg^\gg = \{z \in \gg : \ad(x)(z) = 0,\ \forall\, x \in \gg\}.\] Using either the upper or lower central series, it is easy to see that the only composition factor of \((\gg, \ad)\) is the trivial representation.

We now wish to show that the notion of a flag in a vector space gives us a large supply of nilpotent Lie algebras. In the next Lemma we use the notation of Lemma 4.1.7.

Lemma 4.2.8.
Suppose that \(\mathcal{F}\) is a (not necessarily complete) flag in a finite-dimensional vector space \(V\). Then the Lie algebra \(\nn_{\mathcal{F}} = \bb^1_{\mathcal{F}} \subseteq \gl_V\) is nilpotent.
Proof.
By (i) of Lemma 4.1.7, \([\nn_{\mathcal{F}}, \nn_{\mathcal{F}}] \subseteq \bb^2_{\mathcal{F}}\), and by induction \([\nn_{\mathcal{F}}, C^k(\nn_{\mathcal{F}})] \subseteq \bb^{k+1}_{\mathcal{F}}\), so that \(C^k(\gg) \subseteq \bb^k_{\mathcal{F}}\), and hence \(C^k(\nn) = 0\) if \(\bb^k_{\mathcal{F}} = 0\), which is true whenever \(k \ge d = \dim(V)\).
□
Example 4.2.9.

When \(\mathcal{F}\) is a complete flag, so that \(\dim(V) = d\), if we pick a basis \(\{e_1, e_2, \ldots, e_d\}\) of \(V\) such that \(F_k = \langle \{e_{k+1}, e_2, \ldots, e_d\} \rangle_\kfield\), then the matrix \(A\) representing an element \(x \in \nn_{\mathcal{F}}\) with respect to this basis is strictly upper triangular, that is, \(a_{ij} = 0\) for all \(i \ge j\). But then if \(\nn_d \subseteq \gl_d\) denotes the space of strictly upper-triangular matrices, it is easy to see that \(\dim(\nn_d) = \binom{d}{2}\). When \(d = 2\) we just get the 1-dimensional Lie algebra \(\gl_1\), thus the first nontrivial case is when \(n = 3\) and in this case \(\nn_3\) is the 3-dimensional 2-step nilpotent Lie algebra we constructed previously as a central extension.

On the other hand, if \(\bb_d \subseteq \gl_d\) denotes the upper-triangular matrices, i.e. \(\bb_d\) corresponds to the subalgebra \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}\) of \(\gl_V\), and we set \(\mathfrak{t}_d\) to be the set of diagonal matrices in \(\bb_n\), then it is straight-forward to show by considering the subalgebra \(\mathfrak{t}_n\) of diagonal matrices in \(\bb_n\) that \([\mathfrak{t}_d, \nn_d] = \nn_d\), so that, as \(\bb_d = \mathfrak{t}_d \oplus \nn_d\), it follows that \(\bb_d\) is not nilpotent.

Remark 4.2.10.
Note that in Lemma 4.1.7, unlike in Lemma 4.2.8, it is essential that \(\mathcal{F}\) is a complete flag. If \(\mathcal{F}\) is not a complete flag the corresponding subalgebra \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}}\) will not be solvable (since, for example, if \(\dim(F_i / F_{i+1}) > 1\), then there is a surjective homomorphism \(\bb_{\mathcal{F}} \to \gl_{F_i / F_{i+1}}\), which is not solvable.)
Remark 4.2.11.
Note that the subalgebra \(\mathfrak{t}_d \subset \gl_d\) of diagonal matrices is abelian, and hence nilpotent, but the only nilpotent endomorphism of \(\kfield^d\) that lies in \(\mathfrak{t}_d\) is \(0\). Thus a nilpotent linear Lie algebra need not consist of nilpotent endomorphisms. It turns out that, in some sense, the example of \(\mathfrak{t}_d\) is the only way in which a nilpotent Lie algebra \(\nn \subseteq \gl_d\) can fail to consist of nilpotent endomorphisms. We will make this precise in §4.4.

4.2.1 Nilpotent representations

Definition 4.2.12.
Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and \((V, \rho)\) a representation of \(\gg\). We say that \((V, \rho)\) is nilpotent if, for all \(x \in \gg\), the endomorphism \(\rho(x) \in \gl_V\) is a nilpotent linear map (that is, for some \(n \ge 1\), \(\rho(x)^n = 0\)).
Lemma 4.2.13.
Let \(A\) be an associative algebra, and suppose \(a, b \in A\) are nilpotent, i.e. for some \(n > 0\), we have \(a^n = b^n = 0\). Then if \(a\) and \(b\) commute, \(a + b\) is also nilpotent.
Proof.

This follows from the binomial theorem: Indeed we have \[(a + b)^m = \sum_{k=0}^{m} \binom{m}{k} a^k b^{m-k}.\] But now if \(m \ge 2n\), then we must have either \(k \ge n\) or \(m - k \ge n\), hence in either case, each of the terms on the left-hand side vanishes, hence so does the right-hand side, and hence \(a + b\) is nilpotent as required.

□
Lemma 4.2.14.

Suppose \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \((V, \rho)\) and \((W, \sigma)\) are representations of \(\gg\).

  1. If \(x \in \gg\) is such that both \(\rho(x)\) and \(\sigma(x)\) are nilpotent, then the action of \(x\) on \(V \otimes W\) is also nilpotent. Moreover, the action of \(x\) on \(V^*\) is also nilpotent. Thus if \(V\) and \(W\) are nilpotent, so are \(V^*\), \(V \otimes W\) and \(\mathcal{L}(V, W) \cong V^* \otimes W\).
  2. If \(V\) is nilpotent, then any subrepresentation and any quotient representation of \(V\) is also nilpotent.
Proof.

By definition, the action of \(x\) on \(V \otimes W\) is given by \(\rho(x) \otimes 1_W + 1_V \otimes \sigma(x)\). Since the two terms in this sum commute, the claim follows from Lemma 4.2.13 (taking \(A = \End(V \otimes W)\).)

To see that \(x\) acts nilpotently on \(V^*\), note that if \(f \in V^*\), then \[x^n(f)(v) = (-1)^n f(\rho(x)^n(v)) = \pm f(0) = 0, \qquad \forall\, v \in V,\ f \in V^*.\]

For part (ii), if \(U\) is a subrepresentation of \(V\) then, as in Lemma 2.2.2, \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \bb_U = \{x \in \gl_V : x(U) \subseteq U\}\). But \(\bb_U\) is an associative subalgebra of \(\End(V)\) and the maps \(i_*\) and \(p_*\) from \(\bb_U\) to \(\End(U)\) and \(\End(V/U)\) are also maps of associative algebras, hence if \(\rho(x) \in \bb_U\) is nilpotent, its images in \(\End(U)\) and \(\End(V/U)\) are also.

□

The next proposition is the key result in this section. For the proof we will need the notion of the normalizer \(N_\gg(\aa)\) of a subalgebra \(\aa\) of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) given in Definition 1.2.6. We have \[N_\gg(\aa) = \{x \in \gg : [x, a] \in \aa,\ \forall\, a \in \aa\},\] so that \(N_\gg(\hh)\) is the largest subalgebra of \(\gg\) in which \(\aa\) is an ideal.

Proposition 4.2.15.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra, and let \((V, \rho)\) be a nilpotent representation of \(\gg\).

  1. The invariant subspace \[V^\gg = \{v \in V : \rho(x)(v) = 0,\ \forall\, x \in \gg\}\] is non-zero.
  2. There is a complete flag \(\mathcal{F}\) in \(V\) such that \(\gg \subseteq \nn_{\mathcal{F}}\). In particular, the image \(\rho(\gg)\) is a nilpotent Lie algebra.
Proof.

To prove (i), we use induction on \(d = \dim(\gg)\), the case \(d = 1\) being clear. Now if \(\rho\) is not faithful, i.e. \(\ker(\rho) \ne 0\), then \(\dim(\rho(\gg)) < \dim(\gg)\), and we are done by induction applied to the image \(\rho(\gg)\), hence we may assume \(\rho\) gives an embedding of \(\gg\) into \(\gl_V\) as a subalgebra, and we may thus identify \(\gg\) with its image in the rest of this proof.

Now let \(\mathcal{S} = \{\bb \subsetneq \gg : \bb \text{ is a proper subalgebra of } \gg\}\) denote the set of proper subalgebras of \(\gg\), and pick \(\aa \in \mathcal{S}\). Now by Lemma 4.2.14, \(\aa \subsetneq \gg \subseteq \gl_V = V^* \otimes V\) are all nilpotent representations of \(\aa\), since the restriction of \((V, \rho)\) to \(\aa\) is. But then, by the same Lemma, \(\gg / \aa\) is also a nilpotent representation, and since \(\dim(\aa) < \dim(\gg)\), it follows by induction that the \(\aa\)-invariants \((\gg / \aa)^\aa\) form a non-zero subrepresentation. Let \(x \in \gg\) be such that \(0 \ne x + \aa \in (\gg / \aa)^\aa\). Then \(\ad(a)(x) \in \aa\) for all \(a \in \aa\), or equivalently, since \(\ad(a)(x) = -\ad(x)(a)\), for all \(a \in \aa\), we have \(\ad(x)(a) \in \aa\), that is, \(x \in N_\gg(\aa)\). Thus the normalizer of \(\aa\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\) which is strictly larger than \(\aa\).

Thus if we take \(\aa \in \mathcal{S}\) of maximal dimension, we must have \(N_\gg(\aa) = \gg\), that is \(\aa\) is an ideal in \(\gg\). But then if \(z \in \gg \setminus \aa\), it is easy to see that \(\kfield \cdot z \oplus \aa\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\), hence again by maximality, we must have \(\gg = \kfield \cdot z \oplus \aa\). By induction, we know that \(V^\aa = \{v \in V : a(v) = 0,\ \forall\, a \in \aa\}\) is a nonzero subspace of \(V\). We claim that \(z\) preserves \(V^\aa\). Indeed \[a(z(v)) = [a, z](v) + z(a(v)) = 0, \qquad \forall\, a \in \aa,\ v \in V^\aa,\] since \([a, z] \in \aa\). But the restriction of \(z\) to \(V^\aa\) is nilpotent, so the subspace \(U = \{v \in V^\aa : z(v) = 0\}\) is nonzero. Since \(U = V^\gg\) we are done.

For (ii), let \(\mathcal{C} = (V = F_m > F_{m-1} > \cdots > F_1 > F_0 = \{0\})\) be a composition series for \(V\). It suffices to show that each of the composition factors are trivial. But if \(1 \le k \le m\), then \(F_k\) is a subrepresentation of \(V\) and hence it is nilpotent. Similarly \(Q_k = F_k / F_{k+1}\), as a quotient of \(F_k\) must be nilpotent. But then by part (i), its invariants \(Q_k^\gg\) are a non-zero subrepresentation of \(Q_k\), and since \(Q_k\) is simple it follows that \(Q_k\) is the trivial representation as required.

□
Corollary 4.2.16.
(Engel's theorem.) A Lie algebra \(\gg\) is nilpotent if and only if \(\ad(x)\) is nilpotent for every \(x \in \gg\), i.e. the adjoint representation is nilpotent.
Proof.
If \(\gg\) is nilpotent, then since by definition \(\ad(x)(C^i(\gg)) \subseteq C^{i+1}(\gg)\), we see that \(\ad(x)^k = 0\) for all \(x \in \gg\) if \(\gg\) is \(k\)-step nilpotent. Now suppose that \(\ad(x)\) is nilpotent for all \(x \in \gg\). Then \((\gg, \ad)\) is a nilpotent representation, and hence by part (ii) of Proposition 4.2.15, we see that \(\ad(\gg)\) is nilpotent. But since \(\ad(\gg) \cong \gg / \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\) it follows that \(\gg\) is nilpotent as required.
□

4.3 Representations of solvable Lie algebras

In this section we will assume that our field \(\kfield\) is algebraically closed of characteristic zero.

4.3.1 Lie's theorem

Our first goal is the following theorem:

Theorem 4.3.1.
(Lie's theorem) Let \(\gg\) be a solvable Lie algebra and \(V\) a \(\gg\)-representation. Then there is a homomorphism \(\lambda : \gg \to \gl_1(\kfield)\) and a nonzero vector \(v \in V\) such that \(x(v) = \lambda(x) \cdot v\) for all \(x \in \gg\). Equivalently, any finite-dimensional irreducible representation of a solvable Lie algebra is one-dimensional.

We first explain the equivalence asserted in the last sentence of the statement. Note that the existence of a non-zero \(v \in V\) such that \(x(v) = \lambda(x) \cdot v\) for all \(x \in \gg\) is equivalent to the assertion that the line \(\kfield \cdot v\) is a subrepresentation of \(V\). Thus the statement of the theorem shows that any representation contains a one-dimensional subrepresentation, and hence any irreducible representation must itself be one-dimensional. Since any representation contains an irreducible representation, the equivalence follows.

The crucial observation that is needed to prove Lie's theorem is given in the following Lemma:

Lemma 4.3.2.
(Lie's Lemma) Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra, let \(I \subset \gg\) be an ideal, and let \(V\) be a finite dimensional \(\gg\)-representation. Suppose that \(\lambda : I \to \gl_1(\kfield)\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras for which the subspace \(V_{\lambda,I} = \{v \in V : h(v) = \lambda(h) \cdot v,\ \forall\, h \in I\}\) is nonzero. Then \(\lambda\) vanishes on \([\gg, I] \subset I\), and \(V_{\lambda,I}\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation of \(V\).
Proof.

Fix \(x \in \gg\) and \(v \in V_{\lambda,I} \setminus \{0\}\). For each \(m \in \NN\), let \(W_m = \langle \{v, x(v), \ldots, x^m(v)\} \rangle_\kfield\). The \(W_m\) form an increasing sequence of subspaces of \(V\) with \(\dim(W_{m+1}/W_m) \le 1\), from which it is easy to see that there is some \(d\) with \(W_{-1} := \{0\} < W_0 < W_1 < \cdots < W_{d-1} = W_d\), where \(x(W_d) \subseteq W_d\).

Claim: \[h(x^m(v)) \in \lambda(h) \cdot x^m(v) + W_{m-1}, \qquad \forall\, h \in I,\ m \ge 0\]

Proof of claim: This is obvious for \(m = 0\) and if \(m \ge 1\), since \([h, x] \in I\), by induction we have \[hx^m(v) = xhx^{m-1}(v) + (hx - xh)x^{m-1}(v)\] \[\in x\bigl(\lambda(h) \cdot x^{m-1}(v) + W_{m-2}\bigr) + \lambda([h, x])x^{m-1}(v) + W_{m-2}\] \[\subseteq \lambda(h)x^m(v) + W_{m-1},\] since \(x(W_{m-2}) \subseteq W_{m-1}\) and \([h, x] \in I\).

It follows that \(W_d\) is stable under the action of \(I\) and \(x\), and that if \(h \in I\), then the matrix of the action of \(h\) on \(W_d\) with respect to the basis \(\{v, x(v), \ldots, x^{d-1}(v)\}\) is upper-triangular with each diagonal entry equal to \(\lambda(h)\). In particular, \(\tr(h|_{W_d}) = \lambda(h) \cdot \dim(W_d) = \lambda(h) \cdot d\). But then \[0 = \tr([h, x]) = \lambda([h, x]) \cdot d,\] and since \(d > 0\) and \(\operatorname{char}(\kfield) = 0\), it follows that \(\lambda([h, x]) = 0\). But now considering the claim with \(m = 1\) we see that \(\lambda([h, x]) = 0\) implies that \(h \cdot x(v) = \lambda(h) \cdot x(v)\), so that \(x(v) \in V_{\lambda,I}\) if \(v \in V_{\lambda,I}\) as required.

□

Completion of the proof of Lie's Theorem:

Use induction on \(\dim(\gg)\). Since \(\gg\) is solvable, \(D(\gg)\) is a proper ideal in \(\gg\), and by induction the theorem holds for \(D(\gg)\). But then let \(\lambda : D(\gg) \to \kfield\) be a homomorphism of Lie algebras such that \[W = \{v \in V : h(v) = \lambda(h) \cdot v,\ \forall\, h \in D(\gg)\} \ne \{0\}.\] But then by Lemma 4.3.2, \(W\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation, and if we let \(\rho : \gg \to \gl_W\), then \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \gl_W\) has \(\lambda(D(\gg)) \cdot I_W = \rho(D(\gg)) = D(\rho(\gg))\). But \(D(\gl_W) = \spl(W)\), and since \(\operatorname{char}(\kfield) = 0\), \(\kfield \cdot I_W \cap \spl(W) = \{0\}\), so that \(D(\gg_1) \subseteq \spl(W) \cap \kfield \cdot I_W = \{0\}\). It follows that the action of \(\gg\) on \(W\) factors through \(\gg / D(\gg)\), which is abelian, and the result is then clear, since commuting linear maps on a non-zero vector space always have a common eigenvector.

Remark 4.3.3.
The proof of Lemma 4.3.2 relies on a trick which permeates the course, namely that one can often compute a trace in two different ways to obtain important information. One way will be by observing that one is computing the trace of a commutator, which is therefore zero. The other will, in one fashion or another, follow from consideration of the generalised eigenspaces of the linear map in question.
Corollary 4.3.4.
Let \(\gg\) be a solvable Lie algebra and let \((V, \rho)\) be a \(\gg\)-representation. Then there is a complete flag \(\mathcal{F} = (V = F_0 \supset F_1 \supset \cdots \supset F_d = \{0\})\) where each \(F_i\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation, so that \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \bb_{\mathcal{F}}\). In particular, if \(\gg\) is solvable, then it has a composition series each of whose terms is an ideal in all of \(\gg\).
Proof.
Take any composition series \(\mathcal{F}\) for \(V\). Since Lie's theorem shows that the irreducible representations of \(\gg\) are all one-dimensional, the resulting chain of subrepresentations will form a complete flag and \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \bb_{\mathcal{F}}\). The final sentence follows by applying this to the adjoint representation \((\gg, \ad)\), since \(I \subseteq \gg\) is an ideal if and only if it is a subrepresentation of the adjoint representation.
□
Definition 4.3.5.

Recall from Example 2.1.6 that the elements of \((\gg / D(\gg))^* = D(\gg)^0\) naturally parameterise the isomorphism classes of one-dimensional representations of a Lie algebra \(\gg\). Indeed a homomorphism \(\lambda : \gg \to \gl_1\) is just a linear map \(\lambda : \gg \to \kfield\) which vanishes on \(D(\gg)\). Recall that we write \(\kfield_\lambda\) for the representation \((\kfield, \lambda)\). We will refer to an element of \(D(\gg)^0\) (equivalently, an isomorphism class of 1-dimensional \(\gg\)-representations) as a weight of \(\gg\). In the case where \(\gg\) is solvable, Lie's theorem shows that the weights are exactly the isomorphism classes of irreducible \(\gg\)-representations.

4.4 Representations of nilpotent Lie algebras

In this section we assume that \(\kfield\) is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.

Definition 4.4.1.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and let \(\mathcal{S}\) be a set of irreducible representations of \(\gg\). Let \[\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg) = \{V \in \operatorname{Rep}(\gg) : [T : V] > 0 \text{ if and only if } \exists\, S \in \mathcal{S},\ T \cong S\}\] \[\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg, V) = \{W \le V : W \in \operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg)\}.\] If \(\mathcal{S} = \{S\}\) then we will write \(\operatorname{Rep}_S(\gg)\), \(\operatorname{Rep}_S(\gg, V)\) rather than \(\operatorname{Rep}_{\{S\}}(\gg)\), \(\operatorname{Rep}_{\{S\}}(\gg, V)\) respectively. If \(V \in \operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg)\) then it is easy to see that any subrepresentation or quotient representation of \(V\) also lies in \(\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg)\).

Proposition 4.4.2.
Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and \((V, \rho)\) a representation of \(\gg\). If \(\mathcal{S}\) is a set of irreducible \(\gg\)-representations then \(\operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg, V)\) has a unique element \(V_{\mathcal{S}}\) which is maximal with respect to containment, that is \(V_{\mathcal{S}} \in \operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg, V)\) and if \(U \in \operatorname{Rep}_{\mathcal{S}}(\gg, V)\) then \(U \le V_{\mathcal{S}}\).
Proof.

First note that if it exists, such a maximal element is automatically unique, since if \(W_1, W_2\) are both maximal with respect to containment we must have \(W_1 \le W_2 \le W_1\) and hence \(W_1 = W_2\).

Next note that if \(V_1, V_2 \in \mathcal{V}_S\) then \(V_1 + V_2 \in \mathcal{V}_S\). Indeed by the second isomorphism theorem, \((V_1 + V_2)/V_1 \cong V_2/(V_1 \cap V_2)\), so that any composition factor of \(V_1 + V_2\) must be a composition factor of \(V_1\) or of \(V_2 / (V_1 \cap V_2)\), and hence is a composition factor of \(V_1\) or \(V_2\). Now pick \(W \in \mathcal{V}_S\) with \(\dim(W) \ge \dim(U)\) for all \(U \in \mathcal{V}_S\) (such a \(W\) exists if \(V\) is finite-dimensional, as we always assume). We claim that \(W\) is maximal for containment. Indeed if \(U \in \mathcal{V}_S\) then we have just shown that \(W + U \in \mathcal{V}_S\), hence \(\dim(W) \le \dim(W + U) \le \dim(W)\) by our choice of \(W\), and hence \(U \le W\) and \(W\) is maximal for containment as required. Thus \(W = V_{\mathcal{S}}\) is the unique maximal subrepresentation in \(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{S}}\).

□
Example 4.4.3.
If \(\gg = \gl_1\) and \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, let \(\alpha = \rho(1)\). The simple representations of \(\gg\) are the lines spanned by eigenvectors of \(\alpha\), and are captured up to isomorphism by their eigenvalue. If we set \(\mathcal{S} = \{\lambda\}\) to be a single irreducible \(\gg\)-representation then \(V_\lambda\) is the generalized eigenspace for \(\alpha\) with eigenvalue \(\lambda\).
Definition 4.4.4.

Recall that the isomorphism classes of 1-dimensional representations of \(\gg\) can be identified with \(D(\gg)^0 \subseteq \gg^*\), and given \(\lambda \in D(\gg)^0\), we write \(\kfield_\lambda\) for the 1-dimensional representation \((\kfield, \lambda)\). Given a \(\gg\)-representation \((V, \rho)\), we will write \(V_\lambda\) and \(\operatorname{Rep}_\lambda(\gg, V)\) instead of \(V_{\kfield_\lambda}\) and \(\operatorname{Rep}_{\kfield_\lambda}(\gg, V)\). When \(\lambda \in D(\gg)^0\) we will refer to \(V_\lambda\) as the generalised \(\lambda\)-weight space of \(V\). If \(V\) is a finite-dimensional representation of a Lie algebra \(\gg\), let \[\Psi_V = \{\lambda \in D(\gg)^0 : \lambda \text{ is a composition factor of } V\}\] and let \(m_V : D(\gg)^0 \to \ZZ_{\ge 0}\) be the function which assigns to \(\lambda \in D(\gg)^0\) the multiplicity of \(\lambda\) as a composition factor of \(V\). Thus \(\Psi_V\) is the finite set of the one-dimensional representations \(\lambda\) of \(\gg\) for which \(m_V(\lambda) \ne 0\). If \(\gg\) is solvable and \(\operatorname{char}(\kfield) = 0\) then by Lie's Theorem \(\Psi_V\) contains all the composition factors of \(V\).

If \(\varphi : \gg_1 \to \gg_2\) and \((V, \rho)\) is a representation of \(\gg_2\), then \((V, \varphi^*(\rho))\) is a representation of \(\gg_1\), where \(\varphi^*(\rho) = \rho \circ \varphi\). Since \(\varphi(D(\gg_1)) \subseteq D(\gg_2)\), the transpose \(\varphi^\top : \gg_2^* \to \gg_1^*\) restricts to give a map \(\varphi^\top : D(\gg_2)^0 \to D(\gg_1)^0\). Clearly, the irreducible \(\gg_1\)-representation \((\kfield, \lambda)\) restricts via \(\varphi\) to the irreducible representation \((\kfield, \varphi^\top(\lambda))\), and hence \(\Psi_{\varphi^*(V)} \supseteq \varphi^\top(\Psi_V)\).

More precisely, \(m_{\varphi^*(V)}(\mu) \ge \sum_{\lambda \in \Psi_V : \varphi^\top(\lambda) = \mu} m_V(\lambda)\). In the case where \(\Psi_V\) is the complete set of composition factors of \(V\), such as when \(\gg_1\) is solvable, this inequality is an equality. Now if \(x \in \gg\) and \(i_x : \gl_1 \to \gg\) is the homomorphism \(i_x(t) = t \cdot x\) (\(\forall\, t \in \kfield = \gl_1\)), then if \(\lambda \in D(\gg)^0\), we have \(i_x^\top(\lambda) = \lambda(x)\). The weights of the \(\gl_1\)-representation \(\rho \circ i_x\) are just the eigenvalues of \(\rho(x)\), as in Example 2.1.5, it follows that the eigenvalues of \(\rho(x)\) are \(\{\lambda(x) : \lambda \in \Psi_V\}\), and the \(\mu\)-generalised eigenspace of \(\rho(x)\) is \(\bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V : \lambda(x) = \mu} V_\lambda\).

Lemma 4.4.5.

Suppose that \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \(\lambda, \mu \in D(\gg)^0\) are weights of \(\gg\). If \(V\) and \(W\) are \(\gg\)-representations then

  1. \(V_\lambda \otimes W_\mu \subseteq (V \otimes W)_{\lambda + \mu}\).
  2. If \(\phi : V \to W\) is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations, then \(\phi(V_\lambda) \subseteq W_\lambda\).
Proof.

For part (i), we may assume that \(V = V_\lambda\) and \(W = W_\mu\), hence there are composition series \((F_k)_{k=0}^r\) and \((G_l)_{l=0}^s\), where \(F_k / F_{k+1} \cong \kfield_\lambda\) for each \(k\), and \(G_l / G_{l+1} \cong \kfield_\mu\), for all \(l \in \{0, 1, \ldots, r\}\) and \(k \in \{0, 1, \ldots, s\}\). Pick bases \(\{e_i : 0 \le i \le r-1\}\) and \(\{f_j : 0 \le j \le s-1\}\) of \(V\) and \(W\) respectively such that \(F_k = \langle \{e_i : i \ge k\} \rangle_\kfield\) and \(G_l = \langle \{f_j : j \ge l\} \rangle_\kfield\). If we set \(H_k = \sum_{r+s=k} F_r \otimes G_s\), then \(H_k\) is a subrepresentation of \(V \otimes W\) and we have \[x(e_k) \otimes f_l \in \lambda(x) e_k \otimes f_l + F_{k+1} \otimes G_l;\] \[e_k \otimes x(f_l) \in \mu(x) \cdot e_k \otimes f_l + F_k \otimes G_{l+1}\] \[\implies x(e_k \otimes f_l) = x(e_k) \otimes f_l + e_k \otimes x(f_l) \in (\lambda + \mu)(x)(e_k \otimes f_l) + H_{k+l+1}\] and thus \(H_k / H_{k+1} \cong \kfield_{\lambda+\mu}^{\dim(H_k) - \dim(H_{k+1})}\). It follows \(V \otimes W\) has \(\kfield_{\lambda+\mu}\) as its unique composition factor.

For part (ii), since \(V_\lambda \in \operatorname{Rep}_\lambda(\gg)\), and \(\phi(V_\lambda) \cong V_\lambda / \ker(\phi|_{V_\lambda})\) is isomorphic to a quotient of \(V_\lambda\), it lies in \(\operatorname{Rep}_\lambda(\gg, W)\) and so by the maximality of \(W_\lambda\) it follows that \(\phi(V_\lambda) \subseteq W_\lambda\).

□

The adjoint representation of a nilpotent Lie algebra \(\gg\) has the trivial representation as its only composition factor, that is, \(\gg = \gg_0\). This has the following important consequence:

Proposition 4.4.6.
Let \(\gg\) be a nilpotent Lie algebra, \(\hh \subseteq \gg\) a subalgebra of \(\gg\), and \((V, \rho)\) a representation of \(\gg\). Then if \(\mu \in (\hh / D(\hh))^* \cong D(\hh)^0 / \hh^0 \subseteq \gg^* / \hh^0 \cong \hh^*\) is a weight of \(\hh\), and \(V_\mu\) is the \(\mu\)-isotypic subrepresentation of \(\operatorname{Res}^\gg_\hh(V)\), the restriction of \(V\) to \(\hh\), then \(V_\mu\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation of \(V\). In particular, taking \(\hh = \kfield \cdot x\) for \(x \in \gg \setminus \{0\}\), any generalised eigenspace \(V_{\mu,x}\) of \(\rho(x)\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation.
Proof.
Since \(\gg\) is nilpotent, we have \(\gg = \gg_0\) as an \(\hh\)-representation. But then by Lemma 4.4.5, we have \(\gg \otimes V_\mu = \gg_0 \otimes V_\mu \subseteq (\gg \otimes V)_\mu\), and since the map \(\tilde{a}_\rho : \gg \otimes V \to V\) given by \(\tilde{a}_\rho(x \otimes v) = \rho(x)(v)\) is a homomorphism of \(\hh\)-representations by Example 2.3.4, it follows that \(\tilde{a}_\rho(\gg \otimes V_\mu) = \rho(\gg)(V_\mu) \subseteq V_\mu\), that is, \(V_\mu\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation as required.
□
Definition 4.4.7.

Let \(\gg\) be a nilpotent Lie algebra and let \((V, \rho)\) be a representation of \(\gg\). Say \(x \in \gg\) is \(V\)-generic if, for all \(\lambda, \mu \in \Psi_V\) we have \(\lambda(x) = \mu(x)\) if and only if \(\lambda = \mu\).

If \(D_V = \{\lambda - \mu : \lambda, \mu \in \Psi_V\} \setminus \{0\}\), then \(x\) is \(V\)-generic if and only if \(x \notin \bigcup_{\nu \in D_V} \ker(\nu)\). If \(\kfield\) is infinite, any nonzero vector space over \(\kfield\) is not a union of finitely many hyperplanes, hence \(V\)-generic elements of \(\gg\) exist for any finite-dimensional \(\gg\)-representation \(V\).

Theorem 4.4.8.

Let \(\gg\) be a nilpotent Lie algebra and \((V, \rho)\) a finite-dimensional representation of \(\gg\). For each \(\lambda \in (\gg / D(\gg))^*\), let \[W_\lambda = \bigcap_{x \in \gg} V_{\lambda(x),x}, \qquad V_{\lambda(x),x} = \{v \in V : \exists\, n > 0 \text{ such that } (\rho(x) - \lambda(x))^n(v) = 0\}.\] If \(x_0 \in \gg\) is \(V\)-generic, then we have \(V_{\lambda(x_0),x_0} = V_\lambda = W_\lambda\) and hence \(V = \bigoplus_\lambda V_\lambda\) is the direct sum of its generalised weight spaces.

Proof.

Since \(\gg\) is nilpotent, it is solvable, hence for any \(\gg\)-representation \((U, \sigma)\) its composition factors all lie in \(\Psi_U\) and, as in Definition 4.4.4, if \(x \in \gg\) then \(\sigma(x)\) has spectrum \(\{\lambda(x) : \lambda \in \Psi_U\}\). In particular, taking \(U = V_\lambda\) we see that \(\rho(x)|_{V_\lambda}\) has \(\lambda(x)\) as its sole eigenvalue, that is, \(V_\lambda \subseteq V_{\lambda(x),x}\). It follows that \(V_\lambda \subseteq W_\lambda\).

Now if \(x \in \gg\), we have \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda(x) : \lambda \in \Psi_V} V_{\lambda(x),x}\) and by Proposition 4.4.6 each \(V_{\lambda(x),x}\) is a \(\gg\)-subrepresentation of \(V\), hence taking \(U = V_{\lambda(x),x}\) we see that if \(\kfield_\nu\) is a composition factor, then \(\nu(x) = \lambda(x)\). It follows that if we take \(x_0\) to be \(V\)-generic, the generalised eigenspace \(V_{\lambda(x_0),x_0}\) has \(\lambda\) as its unique composition factor, so that \(V_{\lambda(x_0),x_0} \subseteq V_\lambda\). Hence \(V_{\lambda(x_0),x_0} = V_\lambda = W_\lambda\) and \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\).

□

Chapter 5. Cartan subalgebras and the Cartan criteria

5.1 Nilpotent Lie algebras as measurements

In this section we work over an algebraically closed field \(k\). In particular, \(k\) is infinite.

In the previous Chapter, we saw that the representation theory of a nilpotent Lie algebra \(\mathfrak{n}\) is a natural generalization of the theory for \(\gl_1\): any representation \((V, \rho)\) decomposes \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\) into a direct sum of subrepresentations \(V_\lambda\), where \(\Psi_V \subseteq D(\mathfrak{n})^0\) is the set of composition factors of \(V\) as an \(\mathfrak{n}\)-representation, and the subrepresentation \(V_\lambda\) is precisely the vectors which are simultaneous generalised eigenvectors for every \(\rho(x)\), \((x \in \gg)\), with eigenvalue \(\lambda(x)\). In this Chapter, we will use this result to study the structure of arbitrary finite-dimensional Lie algebras.

Thus suppose \(\gg\) is an arbitrary Lie algebra and let \(\mathfrak{n}\) be a nilpotent subalgebra (since any 1-dimensional subspace is isomorphic to \(\gl_1\) as a Lie subalgebra, such subalgebras always exist).

Lemma 5.1.1.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra, \(\mathfrak{n} \subseteq \gg\) a nilpotent subalgebra, and \((V, \rho)\) a \(\gg\)-representation. Then the \(\mathfrak{n}\)-representations \(\gg\) and \(V\) decompose into direct sums

\[\gg = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha, \qquad V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda,\]

where the \(\gg_\alpha\), \(V_\lambda\) is the \(\alpha\) (resp. \(\lambda\)) isotypical subrepresentation of \(\gg\) (resp. \(V\)) as an \(\mathfrak{n}\)-representation. Moreover,

  1. \(\mathfrak{n} \subseteq \gg_0\);
  2. \([\gg_\alpha, V_\lambda] \subseteq V_{\alpha + \lambda}\), \(\quad (\forall\, \alpha \in \Phi_0,\, \lambda \in \Psi_V)\).

In particular, using \(ii)\) with \(V = (\gg, \ad)\) in \(ii)\) we see that \(\gg_0\) is a subalgebra and hence each \(\gg_\alpha\), \(V_\lambda\) is a \(\gg_0\)-subrepresentation of \(\gg\) and \(V\) respectively.

Proof.

Restricting the adjoint representation of \(\gg\) to \(\mathfrak{n}\) we obtain the associated isotypical decomposition \(\bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha\). Since the adjoint representation of a nilpotent Lie algebra has the trivial representation as its only composition factor, \(\mathfrak{n} \subseteq \gg_0\). Finally, since \(\tilde{a} \colon \gg \otimes V \to V\) is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations, it follows from Lemma 4.4.5 that \([\gg_\alpha, V_\lambda] \subseteq V_{\alpha + \lambda}\). Taking \(V\) to be the adjoint representation, this shows that \([\gg_\alpha, \gg_\beta] \subseteq \gg_{\alpha + \beta}\), which immediately implies that \(\gg_0\) is a subalgebra and \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha\), \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\) decompositions into \(\gg_0\)-subrepresentations.

□

5.1.1 Cartan subalgebras

Now the fact that \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha\) is a decomposition of \(\gg\) as a \(\gg_0\)-representation suggests that to understand the structure of \(\gg\) using this decomposition we may need to understand the structure of representations of (not necessarily nilpotent) Lie algebra \(\gg_0\). Since we already understand the representation theory of \(\mathfrak{n}\), and \(\mathfrak{n} \subseteq \gg_0\), one might hope that it was possible to arrange that \(\gg_0 = \mathfrak{n}\), so that the decomposition \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha\), so that the \(\gg_\alpha\) are not representations of any larger subalgebra of \(\gg\).

Lemma 5.1.2.

\(\mathfrak{n} = \gg_0\) if and only if \(N_\gg(\mathfrak{n}) = \mathfrak{n}\).

Proof.

First that \(\gg/\mathfrak{n}\) is a \(\mathfrak{n}\)-representation, and, since \(\mathfrak{n} \subseteq \gg_0\) we see that \(\gg/\hh \cong \gg_0/\hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) (where we write \(\Phi\) for \(\Phi_0 \setminus \{0\}\)) and hence \(\gg_0/\hh = (\gg/\hh)_0\) is the isotypical summand of \(\gg/\hh\) corresponding to the trivial representation of \(\mathfrak{n}\). But now if \(x + \hh\) spans a copy of the trivial representation of \(\mathfrak{n}\) in \(\gg/\mathfrak{n}\) then \([h, x] \in \mathfrak{n}\) for all \(h \in \mathfrak{n}\), that is, \(x \in N_\gg(\mathfrak{n})\).

Thus we see that \(\mathfrak{n}\) will be all of \(\gg_0\) if and only if \(\mathfrak{n} = N_\gg(\mathfrak{n})\), that is, if and only if \(\mathfrak{n}\) is its own normalizer in \(\gg\).

□

This motivates the following definition:

Definition 5.1.3.

A subalgebra \(\hh\) is said to be a Cartan subalgebra if it is

  1. nilpotent and
  2. self-normalizing, that is, \(N_\gg(\hh) = \hh\).

We will call a pair \((\gg, \hh)\) a Cartan pair if \(\hh\) is a Cartan subalgebra of a Lie algebra \(\gg\), and the isotypical decomposition \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi_0} \gg_\alpha\) of \(\gg\) as an \(\hh\)-representation the Cartan decomposition of the pair. By Lemma 5.1.2, \(\hh = \gg_0\) and hence the decomposition is usually written \(\gg = \hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) where \(\Phi = \Phi_0 \setminus \{0\}\). The weights in \(\Phi\) are called the roots of \(\gg\).

Remark 5.1.4.

It is not clear from this definition whether a Lie algebra necessarily contains a Cartan subalgebra. We will for the moment assume this result, in order to show how they provide a powerful tool to study the structure of an arbitrary finite-dimensional Lie algebra. In fact, however, when \(k\) is algebraically closed with \(\operatorname{char}(k) = 0\) it is known that the set of all Cartan subalgebras of a \(k\)-Lie algebra \(\gg\) form a single orbit under the group of inner automorphisms of \(\gg\). This shows that the Cartan Decomposition of \(\gg\) is unique up to automorphisms of \(\gg\).

5.1.2 Existence of Cartan subalgebras

Note that, if \(\hh\) is a Cartan subalgebra of \(\gg\), then Theorem 4.4.8 shows that there is an \(x_0 \in \hh\) such that \(\hh = \gg_{0,x_0}\). This motivates the following definition:

Definition 5.1.5.

If \(x \in \gg\), let \(\gg_{0,x}\) be the generalized \(0\)-eigenspace of \(\ad(x)\). Note that we always have \(x \in \gg_{0,x}\) so that \(\dim(\gg_{0,x}) \geq 1\). We say that \(x \in \gg\) is regular if \(\gg_{0,x}\) is of minimal dimension.

Proposition 5.1.6.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra over a field \(k\).

  1. If \(x \in \gg\) is any element, then \(\gg_{0,x}\) is a self-normalizing subalgebra of \(\gg\).
  2. If \(x \in \gg\) is a regular element, then \(\gg_{0,x}\) is nilpotent and so a Cartan subalgebra of \(\gg\).
Proof.

Part \(i)\) is straight-forward: It follows immediately from Proposition 4.4.6 applied to the adjoint representation that \(\hh = \gg_{0,x}\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\). To see that \(\hh\) is self-normalizing in \(\gg\): indeed if \(z \in N_\gg(\hh)\) then \([x, z] \in \hh\) (since certainly \(x \in \hh\)), so that for some \(n\) we have \(\ad(x)^n([x, z]) = 0\), and hence \(\ad(x)^{n+1}(z) = 0\) and \(z \in \hh\) as required.

To establish part \(ii)\), assume that \(x\) is regular, and let \(\hh = \gg_{0,x}\). To see that \(\hh\) is nilpotent, by Engel's theorem it suffices to show that, for each \(y \in \hh\), the map \(\ad(y)\) is nilpotent as an endomorphism of \(\hh\). To see this, we consider the characteristic polynomials of \(\ad(y)\) on \(\gg\), \(\hh\) and \(\gg/\hh\): Since \(\hh\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\), the characteristic polynomial \(\chi_y(t) \in k[t]\) of \(\ad(y)\) on \(\gg\) is the product of the characteristic polynomials of \(\ad(y)\) on \(\hh\) and \(\gg/\hh\), which we will write as \(\chi_y^1(t)\) and \(\chi_y^2(t)\) respectively.

We may write \(\chi_y(t) = \sum_{k=0}^{n} c_k(y) t^k\), where \(n = \dim(\gg)\). Pick \(\{h_1, h_2, \ldots, h_r\}\) a basis of \(\hh\) (so that \(\dim(\hh) = r\)). Then if we write \(y = \sum_{i=1}^{r} y_i h_i\), the coefficients \(\{c_k(y)\}_{k=0}^{n}\) of \(\chi_y(t)\) are polynomial functions of the coordinates \(\{y_i : 1 \leq i \leq r\}\). Similarly we have

\[\chi_y^1(t) = \sum_{i=0}^{r} d_i(y) t^i, \qquad \chi_y^2(t) = \sum_{j=0}^{n-r} e_j(y) t^j\]

where the \(d_i, e_j \in k[x_1, \ldots, x_n]\) are polynomials and \(d_i(y) = d_i(y_1, \ldots, y_n)\) where \(y = \sum_{i=1}^{n} y_i h_i\). Since \(\ad(x)(x) = 0\), we have \(x \in \gg_{0,x}\). But \(\ad(x)\) is invertible on \(\gg/\hh\), since all its eigenvalues are non-zero on \(\gg/\hh\), hence \(\chi_x^2(t)\) has \(e_0(x) \neq 0\), and thus the polynomial \(e_0\) is nonzero.

Now let \(s = \min\{i : d_i(x_1, \ldots, x_n) \neq 0\}\). Then we may write \(\chi_y^1(t) = t^s \sum_{k=0}^{r-s} d_{s+k}(y) t^k\), and hence

\[\chi_y(t) = t^s (d_s + d_{s+1} t + \cdots)(e_0 + e_1 t + \cdots) = t^s d_s e_0 + \cdots\]

For any endomorphism of a vector space, the dimension of its \(\lambda\)-generalised eigenspace is the largest power of \((t - \lambda)\) dividing its characteristic polynomial. In particular this implies that, for any \(y \in \hh\), we have \(\dim(\gg_{0,y}) = \min\{i : c_i(y) \neq 0\}\). But since \(e_0 \cdot d_s \in k[x_1, \ldots, x_n]\) is nonzero, there is some \(z \in \hh\) such that \(d_s(z) \cdot e_0(z) \neq 0\), and hence \(\dim(\gg_{0,z}) = s\). Now by definition \(s \leq r = \dim(\gg_{0,x})\), hence since \(x\) is regular, we must have \(s = r\), and hence \(\chi_y^1(t) = t^r\), for all \(y \in \hh\). Hence every \(\ad(y)\) is nilpotent on \(\hh\), so that \(\hh\) is a Cartan subalgebra as required.

□

In the course of the proof of the above Proposition we used the fact that the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial were polynomial functions of the coordinates of \(y \in \hh\) with respect to a basis of \(\hh\). This was crucial because, whereas the product of two arbitrary nonzero functions may well be zero, the product of two nonzero polynomials (over a field) is never zero. Lemma I.4 in Appendix I establishes a slightly more general statement which applied to \(V = \gg\), \(A = \hh\) and \(\varphi = \ad\) gives a proof of this polynomial property.

5.2 Trace forms and Cartan's criterion for solvability

In this section we introduce certain symmetric bilinear forms, which will play an important role in the rest of the course. A brief review of the basic theory of symmetric bilinear forms is given in §II.2 in Appendix 1 of these notes.

5.2.1 Invariant bilinear forms

Let \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) be the space of bilinear forms on \(V\), that is,

\[\operatorname{Bil}(V) = \{B \colon V \times V \to k : B \text{ bilinear}\}.\]

From the definition of tensor products it follows that \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) can be identified with \((V \otimes V)^*\). The involution \(\sigma \colon V \times V \to V \times V\) given by \((v, w) \mapsto (w, v)\) induces an involution (which we will also denote by \(\sigma\)) on \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) and on \(V \otimes V\). We say that a bilinear form \(B\) is symmetric if \(B \circ \sigma = B\), that is, if \(B(v, w) = B(w, v)\) for all \(v, w \in V\).

If \(V\) is a \(\gg\)-representation, the identification of \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) with \((V \otimes V)^*\) shows that \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) also has the structure of \(\gg\)-representation: explicitly, if \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\), then it yields a linear map \(b \colon V \otimes V \to k\) by the universal property of tensor products, and if \(y \in \gg\), it acts on \(B\) as follows:

\[\begin{aligned} y(B)(v, w) &= y(b)(v \otimes w) \\ &= -b(y(v \otimes w)) \\ &= -b(y(v) \otimes w + v \otimes y(w)) \\ &= -B(y(v), w) - B(v, y(w)). \end{aligned}\]

Notice that the involution \(\sigma \in \End(V \otimes V)\) commutes with the action of \(\gg\) (this is a special case of the fact that, for any two \(\gg\)-representations, the map \(\tau \colon V \otimes W \to W \otimes V\) given by \(\tau(v \otimes w) = w \otimes v\) is a \(\gg\)-homomorphism). It follows that the action of \(\gg\) preserves the space \(S^2(V)\) of symmetric bilinear forms.

Definition 5.2.1.

We say that a bilinear form \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(\gg)\) is invariant if it is an invariant vector for the action of \(\gg\) on \(\operatorname{Bil}(\gg) \cong (\gg \otimes \gg)^*\), that is, if \(B(\ad(x)(y), z) = B(y, -\ad(x)(z)) = 0\) for all \(x, y, z \in \gg\). This is often written as

\[B([x, y], z) = B(x, [y, z]), \qquad \forall\, x, y, z \in \gg.\]
Remark 5.2.2.

If \((V, \rho)\) is a \(\gg\)-representation and \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\) is a bilinear form, then it defines a linear map \(\theta \colon V \to V^*\) where \(\theta(v)(w) = B(v, w)\) \((\forall\, v, w \in V)\). If \(B\) is invariant, that is \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)^\gg\), then we have \(\theta(\rho(x)(v))(w) = B(\rho(x)(v), w) = B(v, -\rho(x)(w)) = \rho^*(x)(\theta(v))(w)\) for all \(v, w \in V\), hence \(\theta(\rho(x)(v)) = \rho(x)^*(\theta(v))\), that is, \(\theta \in \mathscr{L}(V, V^*)^\gg = \mathscr{L}_\gg(V, V^*)\) is a homomorphism of \(\gg\)-representations.

If \(\theta\) is an isomorphism, we say \(B\) is nondegenerate and in that case, for any linear map \(\alpha \in \End(V)\) we may define \(\alpha^* = \theta^{-1} \circ \alpha^\intercal \circ \theta \in \End(V)\), the adjoint of \(\alpha\) with respect to \(B\). If \(V\) is a \(\gg\)-representation and \(B\) is nondegenerate, then the condition that \(B\) is invariant can be expressed as \(\rho(x)^* = -\rho(x)\) for all \(x \in \gg\), where \(\rho \colon \gg \to \gl_V\) is the action map, that is, \(\rho(\gg)\) consists of skew-adjoint endomorphisms with respect to the bilinear form \(B\).

Definition 5.2.3.

If \(\alpha \colon \gg_1 \to \gg_2\) is a homomorphism of Lie algebras, and \(B\) is a bilinear form on \(\gg_2\), then we may pull-back \(B\) using \(\alpha\) to obtain a bilinear form on \(\gg_1\). Indeed viewing \(B\) as an element of \((\gg_2 \otimes \gg_2)^*\), we obtain an element \(\alpha^*(B)\) of \((\gg_1 \otimes \gg_1)^*\) given by \(\alpha^*(B)(x, y) = B(\alpha(x), \alpha(y))\). It is immediate from the definitions that if \(B\) is an invariant form for \(\gg_2\), then \(\alpha^*(B)\) is an invariant form for \(\gg_1\).

It follows that if we can find an invariant form \(b_V\) on a general linear Lie algebra \(\gl_V\), then any representation \(\rho \colon \gg \to \gl_V\) of a Lie algebra \(\gg\) on \(V\) will yield an invariant bilinear form \(t_V = \rho^*(b_V)\) on \(\gg\). The next Lemma shows that there is in fact a very natural invariant bilinear form, indeed an invariant symmetric bilinear form, on a general linear Lie algebra \(\gl_V\):

Lemma 5.2.4.

Let \(V\) be a \(k\)-vector space. The trace form \(b_V \colon \gl_V \otimes \gl_V \to k\) given by

\[b_V(a, b) = \tr(a \cdot b), \qquad \forall\, a, b \in \gl_V,\]

is a nondegenerate invariant symmetric bilinear form on \(\gl_V\).

Proof.

Let \(\vartheta \colon V \otimes V^* \to \gl_V\) be given by \(\vartheta(w \otimes f) = f.w \in \gl_V\), where \((f.w)(v) = f(v).w\) for all \(v \in V\). For \(V\) finite-dimensional, this map is an isomorphism and if \(\iota \colon V \otimes V^* \to k\) is the natural "contraction" map induced by the evaluation map \((v, f) \mapsto f(v)\), then \(\tr(\vartheta(v \otimes f)) = \tr(f.v) = f(v) = \iota(v \otimes f)\) (see Lemma II.10 for details).

Moreover, the isomorphism \(\vartheta \otimes \vartheta \colon V \otimes V^* \otimes V \otimes V^* \to \gl_V \otimes \gl_V\) identifies the composition map \((a, b) \to a \circ b\) with the contraction map on the 2nd and 3rd factors. Indeed for any \(v_1, v_2 \in V\), \(f_1, f_2 \in V^*\) we have

\[\vartheta(v_1 \otimes f_1) \circ \vartheta(v_2 \otimes f_2) = (f_1.v_1) \circ (f_2.v_2) = f_1(v_2) \cdot (f_2.v_1) = \vartheta(\iota_{23}(v_1 \otimes f_1 \otimes v_2 \otimes f_2))\]

Thus we see that \((a, b) \mapsto \tr(ab)\) corresponds under \(\vartheta \otimes \vartheta\) to the map \(\iota_{14} \otimes \iota_{32} \colon V \otimes V^* \otimes V \otimes V^* \to k\), where we write \(\iota_{kl}\) for the contraction map acting on the \(k\)-th and \(l\)-th tensor factors if the \(k\)-th is \(V\) and the \(l\)-th is \(V^*\). The composition \((a, b) \mapsto ab\) gives the contraction \(\iota_{23}\) and then taking trace corresponds to the contraction \(\iota_{14}\). Taking \(\tr(ba)\) gives the same value since \((a, b) \mapsto \tr(ba)\) simply contracts the factors in the opposite order, so that \(\tr(ab) = \tr(ba)\) and thus \(b_V\) is a symmetric bilinear form. To show it is invariant, since \(b_V = \iota_{14} \otimes \iota_{23}\) it suffices to check that \(\iota\) is invariant. But this is clear, since \(x(v \otimes f) = x(v) \otimes f - v \otimes (f \circ x)\), thus \(\iota(x(v \otimes f)) = f(x(v)) - (f \circ x)(v) = 0\), while \(x(\iota(v \otimes f)) = x(f(v)) = 0\), since \(\gl_V\) acts by \(0\) on \(k\), the trivial representation. We leave it as an exercise to check the nondegeneracy of \(b_V\).

□
Remark 5.2.5.

One can also of course check the invariance property by a direct calculation: for \(a, b, c \in \gl_V\) we have

\[\begin{aligned} \tr([a, b] \cdot c) &= \tr((ab - ba) \cdot c) = \tr(a \cdot (bc)) - \tr(b \cdot (ac)) \\ &= \tr(a \cdot (bc)) - \tr((ac) \cdot b) \\ &= \tr(a \cdot (bc - cb)) \\ &= \tr(a \cdot [b, c]). \end{aligned}\]

where going from the first to the second line we used the symmetry property of \(\tr\) to replace \(\tr(b \cdot (ac))\) with \(\tr((ac) \cdot b)\).

Definition 5.2.6.

If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra, and let \((V, \rho)\) be a representation of \(\gg\). We may define a bilinear form \(t_V \colon \gg \times \gg \to k\) on \(\gg\), known as a trace form of the representation \((V, \rho)\), to be \(\rho^*(b_V)\). Explicitly, we have

\[t_V(x, y) = \tr_V(\rho(x) \rho(y)), \qquad \forall\, x, y \in \gg.\]

In fact the trace form of a representation depends only on the composition factors of the representation:

Lemma 5.2.7.

Let \((V, \rho)\) be a \(\gg\)-representation. If \(\{S_i : 1 \leq i \leq k\}\) are the composition factors and \([V : S_i]\) denotes the multiplicity of the composition factor \(S_i\), then

\[t_V = \sum_{i=1}^{k} [V : S_i]\, t_{S_i}\]
Proof.

Pick a composition series \(\mathcal{C} = (F_k)_{k=0}^{d}\) for \(V\) and let \(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C} = \{x \in \gl_V : x(F_k) \subseteq F_k,\, \forall k,\, 0 \leq k \leq d\}\). Then \(\rho(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\) and since \(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\) is an associative subalgebra of \(\End(V)\), for any \(x, y \in \gg\), the composition \(\rho(x)\rho(y) \in \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\) and the result then follows from Lemma 2.2.14 where we set \(\alpha = \rho(x)\rho(y)\).

□
Definition 5.2.8.

The Killing form \(\kappa \colon \gg \times \gg \to k\) is the trace form given by the adjoint representation, that is:

\[\kappa(x, y) = \tr(\ad(x) \ad(y)).\]

Note that if \(\mathfrak{a} \subseteq \gg\) is a subalgebra, the Killing form of \(\mathfrak{a}\) is not necessarily equal to the restriction of that of \(\gg\). We will write \(\kappa_\gg\) when it is not clear from context which Lie algebra is concerned.

If \(\mathfrak{a}\) is an ideal in \(\gg\), then in fact the Killing form is unambiguous, as the following Lemma shows.

Lemma 5.2.9.

Let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be an ideal of \(\gg\). The Killing form \(\kappa_\mathfrak{a}\) of \(\mathfrak{a}\) is given by the restriction of the Killing form \(\kappa_\gg\) on \(\gg\), that is:

\[\kappa_\gg\big|_\mathfrak{a} = \kappa_\mathfrak{a}.\]

Moreover, the subspace orthogonal to \(\mathfrak{a}\), that is, \(\mathfrak{a}^\perp = \{x \in \gg : \kappa(x, y) = 0,\, \forall\, y \in \mathfrak{a}\}\) is also an ideal.

Proof.

If \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) we have \(\ad(a)(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{a}\), thus the same will be true for the composition \(\ad(a_1)\ad(a_2)\) for any \(a_1, a_2 \in \mathfrak{a}\). Thus if we pick a vector space complement \(W\) to \(\mathfrak{a}\) in \(\gg\), the matrix of \(\ad(a_1)\ad(a_2)\) with respect to a basis compatible with the subspaces \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \(W\) will be of the form

\[\begin{pmatrix} A & B \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\]

where \(A \in \End(\mathfrak{a})\) and \(B \in \Hom_k(\mathfrak{a}, W)\). Then clearly \(\tr(\ad(a_1)\ad(a_2)) = \tr(A)\). Since \(A\) is clearly given by \(\ad(a_1)|_\mathfrak{a} \ad(a_2)|_\mathfrak{a}\), we are done. To see that \(\mathfrak{a}^\perp\) is an ideal, we must check that for any \(x \in \gg\) and \(y \in \mathfrak{a}^\perp\) we have \([x, y] \in \mathfrak{a}^\perp\). But if \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) then \(\kappa(a, [x, y]) = \kappa([a, x], y) = 0\) since \([a, x] \in \mathfrak{a}\).

□

5.2.2 Cartan criterion for solvable Lie algebras

For the rest of this section \(k\) is an algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.

We now wish to show how the Killing form yields a criterion for determining whether a Lie algebra is solvable or not. For this we need a couple of technical preliminaries. Recall that, if \((V, \rho)\) is a representation of a nilpotent Lie algebra \(\hh\), then it decomposes as the direct sum \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\), where \(\Psi_V \subseteq D(\hh)^0\) denotes the set of one-dimensional representations of \(\hh\) which occur as composition factors of \(V\), and \(V_\lambda\) is the maximal subrepresentation of \(V\) whose only composition factor is \(k_\lambda\). When \((\gg, \hh)\) is a Cartan pair and we view \(\gg\) as a \(\hh\) representation via the inclusion \(\hh \subseteq \gg\), the set \(\Psi_\gg = \{0\} \cup \Phi\), where \(\gg_0 = \hh\).

Definition 5.2.10.

Let \((\gg, \hh)\) be a Cartan pair and let \(\gg = \hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) be the associated decomposition of \(\gg\). For each \(\alpha \in \Phi\) we set \(\hh_\alpha = [\gg_\alpha, \gg_{-\alpha}] \subseteq \hh\). Note that if \(-\alpha \notin \Phi\), then \(\hh_\alpha = \{0\}\).

Lemma 5.2.11.

Let \((\gg, \hh)\) be a Cartan pair and let \(\Phi \subseteq D(\hh)^0\) be the roots of \(\gg\) associated to its Cartan decomposition. Let \((V, \rho)\) be a \(\gg\)-representation and let \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\) be its decomposition into its isotypical summands as an \(\hh\)-representation. If \(\alpha \in \Phi\) and \(\hh_\alpha \subseteq \hh\) is as in Definition 5.2.10, then for any \(\lambda \in \Psi_V\), there is an \(r_\lambda \in \QQ\) such that \(\lambda|_{\hh_\alpha} = r_\lambda \cdot \alpha|_{\hh_\alpha}\).

Proof.

The set of weights \(\Psi\) is finite, thus there are positive integers \(p, q\) such that \(V_{\lambda + t\alpha} \neq 0\) only for integers \(t\) with \(-p \leq t \leq q\); in particular, \(\lambda - (p+1)\alpha \notin \Psi\) and \(\lambda + (q+1)\alpha \notin \Psi\). Let \(M = \bigoplus_{-p \leq t \leq q} V_{\lambda + t\alpha}\).

If \(z \in [\gg_\alpha, \gg_{-\alpha}]\) is of the form \([x, y]\) where \(x \in \gg_\alpha\), \(y \in \gg_{-\alpha}\) then by Lemma 5.1.1,

\[\rho(x)(V_{\lambda + q\alpha}) \subseteq V_{\lambda + (q+1)\alpha} = \{0\}, \qquad \rho(y)(V_{\lambda - p\alpha}) \subseteq V_{\lambda - (p+1)\alpha} = \{0\}\]

we see that \(\rho(x)\) and \(\rho(y)\) preserve \(M\). Thus the action of \(\rho(z)\) on \(M\) is the commutator of the action of \(\rho(x)\) and \(\rho(y)\) on \(M\), and so \(\tr(\rho(z), M) = 0\). On the other hand, we may also compute the trace of \(\rho(z)\) on \(M\) directly: for any \(h \in \hh\), \(\rho(h)\) acts on an isotypical summand \(V_\mu\) with unique eigenvalue \(\mu(h)\), hence \(\tr_{V_\mu}(\rho(h)) = \dim(V_\mu) \cdot \mu(h)\). Applying this to \(\rho(z)\) we find

\[\begin{aligned} 0 = \tr(\rho(z), M) &= \sum_{-p \leq t \leq q} \tr(\rho(z), V_{\lambda + t\alpha}) = \sum_{-p \leq t \leq q} \dim(V_{\lambda + t\alpha})(\lambda + t\alpha)(z) \\ &= \dim(M) \cdot \lambda(z) + \left(\sum_{-p \leq t \leq q} t \cdot \dim(V_{\lambda + t\alpha})\right) \alpha(z) \end{aligned}\]

Since \(\dim(M) \geq \dim(V_\lambda) > 0\), this can be rearranged to give \(\lambda(z) = r_\lambda \cdot \alpha(z)\) as required.

□
Definition 5.2.12.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra over a field \(k\). We say that \(\gg\) is perfect if it satisfies \(\gg = D(\gg) = [\gg, \gg]\). A perfect Lie algebra therefore has no nontrivial abelian quotients.

Proposition 5.2.13.

Let \(V\) be a finite-dimensional \(k\)-vector space and let \(b_V \colon \gl_V \times \gl_V \to k\) be the trace form, \(b_V(x, y) = \tr_V(xy)\), for all \(x, y \in \gl_V\). If \(\mathfrak{a}\) is a non-zero perfect subalgebra of \(\gl_V\) then there is an \(x \in \mathfrak{a}\) such that \(b_V(x, x) \neq 0\), so that \(b_V\) does not vanish identically on \(\mathfrak{a}\).

Proof.

Suppose that \(\hh\) is a Cartan subalgebra of \(\mathfrak{a}\) so that \(\mathfrak{a} = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Phi \cup \{0\}} \mathfrak{a}_\lambda\) is the associated Cartan decomposition, where \(\hh = \mathfrak{a}_0\). Now \(\mathfrak{a} = D\mathfrak{a}\) so that \(\mathfrak{a}\) is not nilpotent, thus \(\Phi \neq \emptyset\). Now if the trivial representation was the only composition factor of \(V\), the same would be true for \(V^*\) and \(\gl_V\), hence if we let \(V = \bigoplus_{\mu \in \Psi} V_\mu\) be the decomposition of \(V\) into generalised \(\hh\)-weight spaces as in Theorem 4.4.8, there must be some non-zero \(\lambda \in \Psi_V\). Next observe that
