\[\mathfrak{a} = D\mathfrak{a} = [\mathfrak{a}, \mathfrak{a}] = \left[\bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Phi \cup \{0\}} \mathfrak{a}_\lambda,\, \bigoplus_{\mu \in \Phi \cup \{0\}} \mathfrak{a}_\mu\right] = \sum_{\lambda, \mu} [\mathfrak{a}_\lambda, \mathfrak{a}_\mu].\]

Since we know that \([\mathfrak{a}_\lambda, \mathfrak{a}_\mu] \subseteq \mathfrak{a}_{\lambda + \mu}\), and moreover \(\hh = \mathfrak{a}_0\), it follows that we must have

\[\hh = [h, \hh] + \sum_\alpha [\mathfrak{a}_\alpha, \mathfrak{a}_{-\alpha}] = D(\hh) + \sum_\alpha \hh_\alpha\]

where the sum runs over those roots \(\alpha\) such that \(-\alpha \in \Phi\). But by definition, \(\lambda\) vanishes on \(D\hh\), so that there must be some \(\alpha \in \Phi\) with \(\lambda(\hh_\alpha) \neq 0\). For such an \(\alpha\), let \(x \in \hh_\alpha = [\mathfrak{a}_\alpha, \mathfrak{a}_{-\alpha}]\) be such that \(\lambda(x) \neq 0\). Then we have

\[b_V(x, x) = \tr(x^2) = \sum_{\mu \in \Psi} \dim(V_\mu)\, \mu(x)^2.\]

But now by Lemma 5.2.11 for each \(\mu \in \Psi\) there is an \(r_\mu \in \QQ\) such that \(\mu(x) = r_\mu \cdot \alpha(x)\) for all \(x \in [\mathfrak{a}_\alpha, \mathfrak{a}_{-\alpha}]\). In particular, \(0 \neq \lambda(x) = r_\lambda \alpha(x)\) so that \(r_\lambda \neq 0\) and \(\alpha(x) \neq 0\). Hence we see that

\[t_V(x, x) = \left(\sum_{\mu \in \Psi} \dim(V_\mu)\, r_{\alpha,\mu}^2\right) \alpha(x)^2.\]

Since the terms in the sum are nonnegative, and the term corresponding to \(\lambda\) is positive, we conclude \(t_V(x, x) \neq 0\) as required.

□

Recall that for any finite dimensional Lie algebra, the derived series stabilizes to an ideal which we denote as \(D^\infty(\gg)\). It has the property that it is equal to its own derived subalgebra, i.e. \(D^\infty(\gg)\) is perfect.

Theorem 5.2.14.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and let \((V, \rho)\) be a \(\gg\)-representation. Then if \(t_V\) vanishes on \(D(\gg)\) then \(\rho(\gg)\) is solvable, or equivalently, \(D^\infty(\gg) \subseteq \ker(\rho)\).

Proof.

Since \(\rho(D(\gg)) = D(\rho(\gg))\), replacing \(\gg\) by its image \(\rho(\gg)\), we may assume that \(\gg \subseteq \gl_V\) and \(b_V\) vanishes on \(D(\gg)\). We must show that \(\gg\) is solvable, that is \(D^\infty(\gg) = \{0\}\). But if this is not the case, then setting \(\mathfrak{a} = D^\infty(\gg)\) it follows that \(\mathfrak{a}\) is a non-zero perfect subalgebra of \(\gl_V\). But then the previous Proposition shows there is some \(x \in \mathfrak{a}\) for which \(b_V(x, x) \neq 0\). But by assumption \(b_V\) vanishes identically on \(D(\gg) \supseteq \mathfrak{a}\), which gives a contradiction.

□
Corollary 5.2.15.

(Cartan's Criterion for Solvability) Let \(\gg\) be a (finite-dimensional) \(k\)-Lie algebra and let \(\kappa\) denote its Killing form. Then the following are equivalent:

  1. \(\kappa(D(\gg), D(\gg)) = 0\),
  2. \(\gg\) is solvable,
  3. \(\kappa(\gg, D(\gg)) = 0\), that is, \(D(\gg) \subseteq \rad(\kappa)\).
Proof.

Clearly \(iii) \implies i)\) so it suffices to show \(i) \implies ii)\) and \(ii) \implies iii)\).

For \(i) \implies ii)\) note that \(i)\) is the hypothesis of Theorem 5.2.14 for the representation \((\gg, \ad)\), and hence the theorem shows that \(D^\infty(\gg) \subseteq \ker(\ad) = \mathfrak{z}(\gg)\). But since \(D^\infty(\gg)\) is perfect, this shows that \(D^\infty(\gg) = D(D^\infty(\gg)) \subseteq D(\mathfrak{z}(\gg)) = \{0\}\) and hence \(ii)\) holds.

For \(ii) \implies iii)\) note that if \(\gg\) is solvable, by Lie's theorem if \(\mathcal{C} = (V = F_0 > F_1 > \cdots > F_d = \{0\})\) is a composition series for \((\gg, \ad)\) then each subquotient \(F_i / F_{i+1}\) must be one-dimensional, i.e. \(\mathcal{C}\) is a complete flag. Pick a basis \(\mathcal{B} = \{e_1, \ldots, e_d\}\) of \(\gg\) such that \(F_k = \langle e_i : i \leq d - k \rangle_k\), and let \(\alpha \mapsto [\alpha]_\mathcal{B}\) denote the isomorphism \(\gl_\gg \to \gl_d(k)\) where \([\alpha]_\mathcal{B} = (\alpha_{ij}^\mathcal{B})_{1 \leq i,j \leq d}\). This isomorphism identifies \(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C} \supset D(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}) = \mathfrak{n}_\mathcal{C}\) with \(\mathfrak{b}_d \supset \mathfrak{n}_d\) the space of upper-triangular matrices and strictly upper triangular matrices in \(\gl_d\) respectively. In particular, for any \(x, y \in \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\) we have \(\tr_\gg(xy) = \sum_{i=1}^{d} x_{ii}^\mathcal{B} \cdot y_{ii}^\mathcal{B}\), and in particular \(\tr_\gg(xy) = 0\) if \(x \in \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\), \(y \in \mathfrak{n}_\mathcal{C}\). But as \(\mathcal{C}\) is a composition series for \(\gg\), we have \(\ad(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}\), and hence \(\ad(D(\gg)) = D(\ad(\gg)) \subseteq D(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}) = \mathfrak{n}_\mathcal{C}\). It follows immediately that \(\kappa(\gg, D(\gg)) \subseteq \tr_\gg(\mathfrak{b}_\mathcal{C}, \mathfrak{n}_\mathcal{C}) = 0\) as required.

□

5.3 Semisimple Lie algebras

In this section we assume that our field \(k\) is algebraically closed of characteristic zero, and all representations are assumed to be finite dimensional over \(k\).

5.3.1 The solvable radical

Suppose that \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra, and \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \(\mathfrak{b}\) are solvable Lie ideals of \(\gg\). It is easy to see that \(\mathfrak{a} + \mathfrak{b}\) is again solvable (for example, because \(0 \subseteq \mathfrak{a} \subseteq \mathfrak{a} + \mathfrak{b}\), and \(\mathfrak{a}\) and \((\mathfrak{a} + \mathfrak{b})/\mathfrak{a} \cong \mathfrak{b}/(\mathfrak{a} \cap \mathfrak{b})\) are both solvable). It follows that if \(\gg\) is finite dimensional, then it has a largest solvable ideal \(\mathfrak{r}\). Note that this is in the strong sense: every solvable ideal of \(\gg\) is a subalgebra of \(\mathfrak{r}\) (c.f. Definition 4.4.1 where the same strategy was used to define the subrepresentation \(V_S\) of a \(\gg\)-representation given an irreducible representation \(S\) of \(\gg\)).

Definition 5.3.1.

Let \(\gg\) be a finite dimensional Lie algebra. The largest solvable ideal \(\mathfrak{r}\) of \(\gg\) is known as the (solvable) radical of \(\gg\), and will be denoted \(\rad(\gg)\). We say that \(\gg\) is semisimple if \(\rad(\gg) = 0\), that is, if \(\gg\) contains no non-zero solvable ideals.

Lemma 5.3.2.

The Lie algebra \(\gg / \rad(\gg)\) is semisimple, that is, it has zero radical.

Proof.

Suppose that \(\mathfrak{s}\) is a solvable ideal in \(\gg / \rad(\gg)\). Then if \(\mathfrak{s}'\) denotes the preimage of \(\mathfrak{s}\) in \(\gg\), we see that \(\mathfrak{s}'\) is an ideal of \(\gg\), and moreover it is solvable since \(\rad(\gg)\) and \(\mathfrak{s} = \mathfrak{s}' / \rad(\gg)\) are both solvable. But then by definition we have \(\mathfrak{s}' \subseteq \rad(\gg)\) so that \(\mathfrak{s}' = \rad(\gg)\) and \(\mathfrak{s} = 0\) as required.

□
Example 5.3.3.

The Lemma shows that any Lie algebra \(\gg\) contains a canonical solvable ideal \(\rad(\gg)\) such that \(\gg / \rad(\gg)\) is a semisimple Lie algebra. Thus we have a short exact sequence:

\[0 \longrightarrow \rad(\gg) \longrightarrow \gg \longrightarrow \gg / \rad(\gg) \longrightarrow 0,\]

so that any Lie algebra is an extension of the semisimple Lie algebra \(\gg / \rad(\gg)\) by the solvable Lie algebra \(\rad(\gg)\).

In characteristic zero, every Lie algebra \(\gg\) is built out of \(\rad(\gg)\) and \(\gg / \rad(\gg)\) as a semidirect product.

Theorem 5.3.4.

(Levi's theorem) Let \(\gg\) be a finite dimensional Lie algebra over a field \(k\) of characteristic zero, and let \(\mathfrak{r}\) be its radical. Then there exists a subalgebra \(\mathfrak{s}\) of \(\gg\) such that \(\gg \cong \mathfrak{r} \rtimes \mathfrak{s}\). In particular \(\mathfrak{s} \cong \gg / \mathfrak{r}\) is semisimple.

5.3.2 Cartan's Criterion for semisimplicity

The Killing form gives us a way of detecting when a Lie algebra is semisimple. Recall that, given a symmetric bilinear form \(B \colon V \times V \to k\), the radical of \(B\) is

\[\rad(B) = \{v \in V : \forall\, w \in V,\, B(v, w) = 0\} = V^\perp.\]

The form \(B\) is said to be nondegenerate if \(\rad(B) = \{0\}\). Cartan's criterion for semisimplicity is a consequence of the criterion for solvability and the following two simple Lemmas:

Lemma 5.3.5.

A finite dimensional Lie algebra \(\gg\) is semisimple if and only if it does not contain any non-zero abelian ideals.

Proof.

Clearly if \(\gg\) contains an abelian ideal, it contains a solvable ideal, so that \(\rad(\gg) \neq 0\). Conversely, if \(\mathfrak{s}\) is a non-zero solvable ideal in \(\gg\), then the last term in the derived series of \(\mathfrak{s}\) will be an abelian ideal of \(\gg\).

□
Lemma 5.3.6.

If \(\gg\) is a Lie algebra and \(\mathfrak{a} \trianglelefteq \gg\) is an abelian ideal then \(\mathfrak{a} \subseteq \rad(\kappa_\gg)\).

Proof.

Pick \(W \leq \gg\) a vector space complement to \(\mathfrak{a}\). Then decomposing \(\End(\gg) = \mathscr{L}(\mathfrak{a} \oplus W, \mathfrak{a} \oplus W)\), if \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) and \(x \in \gg\) it follows that

\[\ad(x) = \begin{pmatrix} x_1 & x_2 \\ 0 & x_3 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad \ad(a) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & a_2 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} \in \begin{pmatrix} \mathscr{L}_k(\mathfrak{a}, \mathfrak{a}) & \mathscr{L}_k(W, \mathfrak{a}) \\ \mathscr{L}_k(\mathfrak{a}, W) & \mathscr{L}_k(W, W) \end{pmatrix}.\]

hence \(\ad(x)\ad(a) = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & x_1 a_2 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\) and hence clearly \(\kappa_\gg(x, a) = 0\) for all \(x \in \gg\) and the result follows.

□

We have the following simple characterisation of semisimple Lie algebras.

Theorem 5.3.7.

A Lie algebra \(\gg\) is semisimple if and only if the Killing form is nondegenerate.

Proof.

If \(\gg^\perp = \rad(\kappa_\gg)\) then it is an ideal in \(\gg\) so that \(\kappa_{\gg^\perp} = \kappa_\gg|_{\gg^\perp \times \gg^\perp} \equiv 0\). Hence by Cartan's criterion for solvability, \(\gg^\perp\) is solvable. Thus if \(\gg\) is semisimple we must have \(\gg^\perp = \{0\}\), that is \(\kappa_\gg\) is non-degenerate.

Conversely, if \(\gg\) is not semisimple, then by Lemma 5.3.5 it contains a nontrivial abelian ideal, \(\mathfrak{a}\) say, and by Lemma 5.3.6 we see that \(\mathfrak{a} \subseteq \gg^\perp\) so that \(\gg^\perp\) is nontrivial.

□
Remark 5.3.8.

Note that the proof of Cartan's semisimplicity criterion given above establishes two facts: first, that \(\gg^\perp\) is a solvable ideal in \(\gg\) for any Lie algebra \(\gg\), and secondly, that any abelian ideal of \(\gg\) is contained in \(\gg^\perp\). Combined with the previous Lemma this shows that \(\gg^\perp = \{0\} \iff \rad(\gg) = \{0\}\), but in general the containment \(\gg^\perp \subseteq \rad(\gg)\) need not be an equality.

5.3.3 Simple and semisimple Lie algebras

Definition 5.3.9.

Recall from Definition 3.1.1 that a Lie algebra \(\gg\) is said to be almost simple if it has no non-trivial proper ideals. We say that \(\gg\) is simple if it is nonabelian and has no nontrivial proper ideal, i.e. \(\gg\) is almost simple and nonabelian. We now show that this notion is closely related to our notion of a semisimple Lie algebra.

Lemma 5.3.10.

Let \(V\) be a \(k\)-vector space equipped with a symmetric bilinear form \(B\). Then for any subspace \(U\) of \(V\) we have

  1. \(\dim(U) + \dim(U^\perp) \geq \dim(V)\),
  2. the restriction \(B|_U\) is non-degenerate if and only if \(U \oplus U^\perp = V\).
Proof.

See Lemma II.20 in Appendix II for a proof.

□
Proposition 5.3.11.

Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra, and let \(I\) be an ideal of \(\gg\).

  1. If \(\gg\) is semisimple then \(\gg = I \oplus I^\perp\), and both \(I\) and \(I^\perp\) are semisimple, hence any ideal and any quotient of \(\gg\) is semisimple.
  2. \(I\) is semisimple if and only if \(\gg = I \oplus I^\perp\).
Proof.

For part \(i)\) consider \(I \cap I^\perp\). The Killing form \(\kappa\) of \(\gg\) vanishes identically on \(I \cap I^\perp\) by definition, and since it is an ideal, the Killing form of \(I \cap I^\perp\) is just the restriction of the Killing form of \(\gg\). It follows from Cartan's Criterion that \(I \cap I^\perp\) is solvable, and hence since \(\gg\) is semisimple we must have \(I \cap I^\perp = 0\). But then by part i) of Lemma 5.3.10 we must have \(\gg = I \oplus I^\perp\). Since this is evidently an orthogonal direct sum, the Killing form must be nondegenerate on both \(I\) and \(I^\perp\), and since they are ideals, Cartan's criterion then implies they are both semisimple. Since the quotient map induces an isomorphism \(I^\perp \cong \gg / I\) it follows that any quotient of \(\gg\) is also semisimple.

For \(ii)\), note that by part \(ii)\) of Lemma 5.3.10, \(\kappa\) is non-degenerate on \(I\) if and only if \(\gg = I \oplus I^\perp\). But the restriction of \(\kappa_\gg\) to \(I\) is the Killing form of \(I\), and so Cartan's criterion completes the proof.

□
Corollary 5.3.12.

Let \(\gg\) be a semisimple Lie algebra and let \(\Der_k(\gg)\) be the Lie algebra of derivations of \(\gg\). Then \(\ad \colon \gg \to \Der_k(\gg)\) is an isomorphism, so that in particular any derivation of \(\gg\) is inner.

Proof.

Suppose that \(\delta \in \Der_k(\gg)\). Then we may form \(\gg_1 = \gg \rtimes_\delta \gl_1\), the semi-direct product of \(\gg\) and \(\gl_1\). Now \(\gg\) is a semisimple ideal in \(\gg_1\), so by Proposition 5.3.11, \(\gg_1 = \gg \oplus \gg^\perp\). But then \([\gg^\perp, \gg] \subseteq \gg^\perp \cap \gg = \{0\}\), and so if \(a \in \mathfrak{a}\) is such that \((a, -1) \in \gg^\perp\) then for all \(x \in \gg\) we have

\[0 = [(a, -t), (x, 0)] = \ad(a)(x) - \delta(x),\]

and hence \(\delta = \ad(a)\) is an inner derivation as required.

□
Theorem 5.3.13.

Let \(\gg\) be a semisimple Lie algebra.

  1. There exist ideals \(\gg_1, \gg_2, \ldots, \gg_k \subseteq \gg\) which are simple Lie algebras and for which the natural map: \[\gg_1 \oplus \gg_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus \gg_k \to \gg,\] is an isomorphism. In particular, if \(\gg\) is semisimple it is perfect, i.e. \(D(\gg) = \gg\).
  2. Any simple ideal \(\mathfrak{a}\) of \(\gg\) is equal to some \(\gg_i\) \((1 \leq i \leq k)\). In particular the decomposition in part \(i)\) is unique up to reordering.
Proof.

For part \(i)\) we use induction on the dimension of \(\gg\). Let \(\mathfrak{a}\) be a minimal non-zero ideal in \(\gg\). If \(\mathfrak{a} = \gg\) then \(\gg\) is simple, so we are done. Otherwise, by Proposition 5.3.11 we have \(\gg = \mathfrak{a} \oplus \mathfrak{a}^\perp\) and hence any ideal in \(\mathfrak{a}\) is also an ideal in \(\gg\), thus since \(\mathfrak{a}\) is a minimal ideal, it must be simple. But now \(\dim(\mathfrak{a}^\perp) < \dim(\gg)\) and so by induction \(\mathfrak{a}^\perp = \bigoplus_{i=1}^{k-1} \gg_i\) is a direct sum of simple ideals. Setting \(\gg_k = \mathfrak{a}\) it follows \(\gg = \bigoplus_{i=1}^{k} \gg_i\), a direct sum of simple ideals as required. Since a simple Lie algebra is trivially seen to be perfect, each \(\gg_i\) is perfect and hence so is \(\gg\).

For part \(ii)\), let \(\gg = \gg_1 \oplus \gg_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus \gg_k\) be as above and suppose that \(\mathfrak{a}\) is any simple ideal of \(\gg\). Now as \(\mathfrak{z}(\gg) = \{0\}\), we must have \(0 \neq [\gg, \mathfrak{a}] \subset \mathfrak{a}\), and hence by simplicity of \(\mathfrak{a}\) it follows that \([\gg, \mathfrak{a}] = \mathfrak{a}\). But then we have

\[\mathfrak{a} = [\gg, \mathfrak{a}] = \left[\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k} \gg_i, \mathfrak{a}\right] = [\gg_1, \mathfrak{a}] \oplus [\gg_2, \mathfrak{a}] \oplus \cdots \oplus [\gg_k, \mathfrak{a}],\]

where the sum of the ideals \([\gg_i, \mathfrak{a}]\) is direct because \([\gg_i, \mathfrak{a}] \subseteq \gg_i\) and the sum \(\bigoplus \gg_i\) is direct. But as \(\mathfrak{a}\) is simple, exactly one of the summands, say \([\gg_j, \mathfrak{a}]\) is nonzero and so \(\mathfrak{a} = [\gg_j, \mathfrak{a}]\). Finally, as \(\mathfrak{a}\) is nonzero and \(\mathfrak{a} = [\gg_j, \mathfrak{a}] \subseteq \gg_j\), the simplicity of \(\gg_j\) implies that \(\mathfrak{a} = [\gg_j, \mathfrak{a}] = \gg_j\) as required.

□

5.4 The representation theory of \(\spl_2\)

Definition 5.4.1.

The standard basis for \(\spl_2(k)\) is \(e = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\), \(h = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{pmatrix}\) and \(f = \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \end{pmatrix}\). The relations obeyed by \(\{e, h, f\}\) in \(\spl_2\) are \([e, f] = h\), \([h, e] = 2e\) and \([h, f] = -2f\). If \(\gg\) is an arbitrary Lie algebra, an \(\spl_2\)-triple is a triple \(\{E, H, F\}\) of elements of \(\gg\) which obey the same relations, so \([E, F] = H\), \([H, E] = 2E\) and \([H, F] = -2F\). Such a triple determines a homomorphism \(\theta \colon \spl_2 \to \gg\) where \(\theta(e) = E\), \(\theta(h) = H\) and \(\theta(f) = F\). Since \(\spl_2\) is simple, such a homomorphism is determined by its image up to a scalar.

In particular, in this section, when \(V\) is a representation of \(\spl_2\) we will write the action in terms of an \(\spl_2\)-triple \(\{E, H, F\}\) rather than a homomorphism \(\rho \colon \spl_2 \to \gl_V\), i.e., we will write \(E\) rather than \(\rho(e)\) etc.

The proofs of the results up to Lemma 5.4.6 are exercises for Problem Sheet 3.

Lemma 5.4.2.

Let \(A\) be an associative algebra and suppose that \(\{E, H, F\}\) is an \(\spl_2\)-triple in \(A\). Then we have:

  1. \([H, E^k] = 2k E^k\),
  2. \([H, F^k] = -2k F^k\),
  3. \([E, F^k] = k F^{k-1}(H - (k-1))\).
Lemma 5.4.3.

Let \(V\) be a representation of \(\spl_2\), let \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in k} V_{\lambda, H}\) be the generalised eigenspace decomposition of \(V\) with respect to \(H\), and let \(V_{\lambda, H}^s \subseteq V_{\lambda, H}\) denote the \(\lambda\)-eigenspace of \(H\). Then we have

\[E(V_{\lambda, H}) \subseteq V_{\lambda+2, H}, \qquad E(V_{\lambda, H}^s) \subseteq V_{\lambda+2, H}^s,\] \[F(V_{\lambda, H}) \subseteq V_{\lambda-2, H}, \qquad F(V_{\lambda, H}^s) \subseteq V_{\lambda-2, H}^s.\]
Definition 5.4.4.

Since \(\operatorname{char}(k) = 0\), the rational numbers \(\QQ\) is the minimal subfield of \(k\). We may thus partially order \(k\) by setting \(\lambda < \mu\) if \(\lambda - \mu \in \QQ_{>0}\). If \(V\) is a finite dimensional \(\spl_2\)-representation then the spectrum \(S(H)\) of \(H\) (that is, its set of eigenvalues) is finite, and hence contains at least one eigenvalue \(\lambda\) which is maximal for the partial order. By Lemma 5.4.3 we see that \(E(V_{\lambda, H}) \subseteq V_{\lambda+2, H} = \{0\}\), so that \(V_{\lambda, H} \subseteq \ker(E)\). A vector \(v \in V\) is said to be a highest weight vector if it is an \(H\)-eigenvector and \(E(v) = 0\). Any representation contains a highest weight vector because the elements of \(V_{\lambda, H}^s\) are highest weight vectors whenever \(\lambda\) is maximal in \(S(H)\).

Proposition 5.4.5.

Let \(V\) be an irreducible representation of \(\spl_2\), where the action is given by the \(\spl_2\)-triple \(\{E, H, F\} \in \gl_V\). Then if \(\dim(V) = n + 1\), \(V\) is generated by a highest weight vector \(v_n\) of weight \(n\), the action of \(H\) is diagonalisable with spectrum \(\{-n + 2k : 0 \leq k \leq n\}\). Moreover, there is a unique irreducible representation of \(\spl_2\) of dimension \(n + 1\) for every \(n \in \ZZ_{\geq 0}\).

Lemma 5.4.6.

Let \(V\) be a representation of \(\spl_2\) and let \(C = C_V = EF + FE + \frac{1}{2} H^2\) be the Casimir operator.

  1. The Casimir operator is an \(\spl_2\)-endomorphism of \(V\).
  2. If \(V = V(d)\) is the irreducible representation of dimension \(d + 1\), then \(C\) acts on \(V(d)\) by the scalar \(\lambda(d) = \frac{1}{2} d(d + 2)\).
  3. If \(V\) is an arbitrary \(\spl_2\)-representation and the generalised eigenspace decomposition of \(V\) with respect to \(C\) is given by \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in S(C)} V_{\lambda, C}\), then if \(V_{\lambda, C} \neq 0\) there is a unique \(d \in \ZZ_{\geq 0}\) such that \(\lambda = \lambda(d)\) and \(V_{\lambda, C} \in \operatorname{Rep}_{V_d}(\spl_2)\).
Corollary 5.4.7.

If \((V, \rho)\) is a finite-dimensional representation of \(\spl_2\) and \(W\) is a proper subrepresentation with \(\dim(W) = \dim(V) - 1\), then \(W\) has a complement in \(V\).

Proof.

By part \(iii)\) of Lemma 5.4.6 if \(V = \bigoplus_{d \in D} V_{\lambda(d), C}\) is the decomposition of \(V\) into the generalised eigenspaces of the Casimir \(C_V\), where \(D\) is the subset of \(\ZZ_{\geq 0}\) for which \(\lambda(d)\) is an eigenvalue of \(C_V\), the subspaces \(V_{\lambda(d), C}\) are \(\spl_2\)-subrepresentations with \(V(d)\) their only composition factor. Moreover, \(W = \bigoplus_{d \in D} V_{\lambda(d), C} \cap W\) (since it is a \(C_V\)-stable subspace). Since \(\dim(V/W) = 1\), we must have \(V/W \cong V(0)\), so that \(V/W \cong V_{0,C} / W \cap V_{0,C}\), thus we may assume that the only composition factor of \(V\) is the trivial representation. Let \(\mathcal{C} = (V = F_0 > F_1 > \cdots > F_d = \{0\})\) be a composition series for \(V\). Then by assumption \(\mathcal{C}\) is a complete flag and \(\rho(\spl_2) \subseteq \mathfrak{n}_\mathcal{C}\). But then \(\rho(\spl_2)\) must be nilpotent, and as \(\spl_2\) is simple this implies \(\rho(\spl_2) = \{0\}\) as required.

□
Theorem 5.4.8.

Let \((V, \rho)\) be a representation of \(\spl_2\). Then \(V\) is semisimple (and hence completely reducible).

Proof.

Suppose that \(U \leq V\) is a subrepresentation. Now \(\gl_V \cong V^* \otimes V\) is a \(\spl_2\) representation, and if we let

\[\begin{aligned} M_1 &= \{\phi \in \gl_V : \phi(V) \subseteq U,\, \exists\, c \in k,\, \phi|_U = c \cdot 1_U\}, \\ M_0 &= \{\phi \in \gl_V : \phi(V) \subseteq U,\, \phi(U) = 0\}. \end{aligned}\]

then \(M_0 \subseteq M_1\) with \(\dim(M_1 / M_0) = 1\), and if \(\phi \in M_1\), \(x \in \spl_2\) we have

\[[\rho(x), \phi](v) = \rho(x)(\phi(v)) - \phi(\rho(x)(v)) \in U, \qquad \forall\, v \in V,\]

and if \(v \in U\) then \(\phi(v) = cv\) and \(\phi(\rho(x)(v)) = c\rho(x)(v)\) so that \([\rho(x), \phi] \in M_0\). Thus \(M_1\) is an \(\spl_2\)-subrepresentation of \(\gl_V\) with \(M_0\) a subrepresentation with \(M_1 / M_0\) one-dimensional. But then by Corollary 5.4.7 it follows that \(M_0\) has a complement, \(S\) say. Now \(\dim(S) = 1\), so that \(S\) must be a copy of the trivial representation, and we may pick \(\phi_0 \in S\) such that \(\phi_0|_U = 1_U\). But then \([\rho(x), \phi_0] = 0\) for all \(x \in \spl_2\) says precisely that \(\phi_0 \in \End_{\spl_2}(V)\) and since \(\phi_0(V) \subseteq U\) with \(\phi_0|_U = 1_U\), it is an \(\spl_2\)-homomorphism and a projection map to \(U\), hence \(V = U \oplus \ker(\phi_0)\) and \(\ker(\phi_0)\) is a complementary subrepresentation to \(U\) as required.

□

We end this section with an application of the semisimplicity of finite-dimensional \(\spl_2\)-representations:

Theorem 5.4.9.

(Levi's theorem for \(\spl_2\)). Let \(\gg\) be a Lie algebra and let \(\mathfrak{r} = \rad(\gg)\). If \(\gg / \mathfrak{r} \cong \spl_2\), then there is a subalgebra \(\mathfrak{s}\) of \(\gg\) isomorphic to \(\spl_2\) so that \(\gg\) is the semidirect product of \(\mathfrak{r} \rtimes \mathfrak{s}\).

Proof.

(Non-examinable.) We consider three steps, with the core of the proof being step 2.

Step 1: \([\mathfrak{r}, \gg] = 0\), that is, \(\mathfrak{r}\) is the centre of \(\gg\). Then you can check (see Problem Sheet) that \(\gg\) is a representation of \(\spl_2\) and complete reducibility of \(\gg\) will provide a splitting.

Step 2: \([\mathfrak{r}, \gg] \neq 0\) and \(\mathfrak{r}\) has no proper ideals. Since \(\mathfrak{r}\) is solvable, this implies that \(D\mathfrak{r} = 0\) and hence \(\mathfrak{r}\) is abelian. Now \(\ad \colon \gg \to \gl_\gg\) and \(\gl_\gg\) acts on itself via its adjoint representation, hence their composition \(\ad_{\gl_\gg} \circ \ad_\gg\) gives \(\gl_\gg\) the structure of a \(\gg\)-representation. Let \(M_1 = \{\phi \in \gl_\gg : \phi(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{r},\, \phi|_\mathfrak{r} = c \cdot 1_\mathfrak{r}\}\) and \(M_0 = \{\phi \in \gl_\gg : \phi(\gg) \subseteq \mathfrak{r},\, \phi(\mathfrak{r}) = 0\}\), so that \(M_1 \supseteq M_0\) and both contain \(\ad(\mathfrak{r})\).

Now if \(x \in \gg\), \(\phi \in M_1\) then for all \(y \in \gg\) we have \(x(\phi)(y) = [x, \phi(y)] - \phi([x, y]) \in \mathfrak{r}\) and if \(y \in \mathfrak{r}\) then \(x(\phi)(y) = 0\) since \(\phi(y) = cy\), \(\phi([x, y]) = c[x, y]\). Moreover, if \(x \in \mathfrak{r}\) then \(x(\phi)(y) = -\phi([x, y]) = -c \cdot \ad(x)(y)\) so that \(\mathfrak{r}(M_1) \subseteq \ad(\mathfrak{r})\). It follows that \(\gg(M_1) \subseteq M_0\) and \(\mathfrak{r}(M_1) \subseteq \ad(\mathfrak{r})\). Thus \(M_1 / \ad(\mathfrak{r})\) is a \(\gg / \mathfrak{r} \cong \spl_2\)-representation. It follows that \(M_0 / \ad(\mathfrak{r})\) has a complement in \(M_1 / \ad(\mathfrak{r})\), and since \(\dim(M_0) = \dim(M_1) - 1\), this complement is \(k \cdot \phi_0 + \ad(\mathfrak{r})\), a copy of the trivial representation of \(\spl_2\), where by scaling \(\phi_0\) appropriately we may assume that \(\phi_0|_\mathfrak{r} = -1_\mathfrak{r}\), that is, \(\phi_0\) is a projection to \(\mathfrak{r}\).

Consider \(\mathfrak{s} = \{x \in \gg : x(\phi_0) = 0\}\). Then certainly \(\mathfrak{s}\) is a subalgebra of \(\gg\), and if \(x \in \mathfrak{r}\) then as we saw above \(x(\phi_0) = \ad(x) = 0\), so that \(k \cdot x\) is an ideal in \(\mathfrak{r}\), contradicting our assumption that \(\mathfrak{r}\) had no proper ideals. Thus \(\mathfrak{s} \cap \mathfrak{r} = \{0\}\). But if \(x \in \gg\), then \(\phi_0(x) \in \mathfrak{r}\), and \(y(\phi_0) = \ad(y)\) for all \(y \in \mathfrak{r}\), so that

\[(x - x(\phi_0))(\phi_0)(y) = x(\phi_0)(y) - x(\phi_0)(\phi_0)(y) = x(\phi_0)(y) - x(\phi_0)(y) = 0,\]

so that \(x = (x - x(\phi_0)) + x(\phi_0) \in \mathfrak{s} + \mathfrak{r}\). Since \(\mathfrak{s} \cap \mathfrak{r} = \{0\}\), it follows that \(\gg = \mathfrak{s} \oplus \mathfrak{r}\) as vector spaces, so that \(\gg = \mathfrak{s} \rtimes \mathfrak{r}\) as required.

Step 3: Now suppose that \(\gg\) is arbitrary, and prove the theorem by induction on \(\dim(\mathfrak{r})\). If \(\dim(\mathfrak{r}) = 0\) there is nothing to prove, so suppose \(\dim(\mathfrak{r}) > 0\). If \(\mathfrak{r}\) has no proper nonzero ideals then \([\mathfrak{r}, \gg] = \mathfrak{r}\) or \(\{0\}\), and we are done by steps 1 and 2 respectively. Otherwise \(\mathfrak{r}\) has a proper nonzero ideal, \(I\) say, and if we let \(\gg_1 = \gg / I\) then \(\rad(\gg_1) = \mathfrak{r} / I\) has strictly smaller dimension. But then by induction there is a subalgebra \(\mathfrak{s}_1 \subseteq \gg / I\) which is isomorphic to \(\spl_2\), with \(\mathfrak{s}_1 \oplus \mathfrak{r} / I = \gg_1\). But then if \(\tilde{s} = \{s \in \gg : s + I \in \mathfrak{s}_1\}\), then \(\rad(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}) = I\) and \(\dim(I) < \dim(\mathfrak{r})\) so that by induction again we may find a subalgebra \(\mathfrak{s}\) of \(\tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\) with \(\mathfrak{s} \oplus I = \tilde{\mathfrak{s}}\), and \(\mathfrak{s} \cong \tilde{\mathfrak{s}} / I \cong \spl_2\) as required.

□

Chapter 6. The structure of semisimple Lie algebras

If \((\gg, \hh)\) is a Cartan pair, the amount of information captured by the Cartan decomposition \(\gg = \hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) depends on \(\gg\). At one extreme, when \(\gg\) is nilpotent, we have the trivial decomposition \(\hh = \gg\). The semisimple case is in some sense at the opposite extreme: the Cartan subalgebra \(\hh\) turns out to be abelian and the decomposition of \(\gg\) is as fine as possible – \(\gg\) is a semisimple \(\hh\)-representation.

6.1 The Killing form and the Cartan decomposition

Proposition 6.1.1.

Let \((\gg, \hh)\) be a Cartan pair and let \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Phi_0} \gg_\lambda\) be the associated Cartan decomposition of \(\gg\), where \(\Phi_0 = \{0\} \cup \Phi\), \(\gg_0 = \hh\), and let \(\kappa\) denote the Killing form of \(\gg\). For \(\alpha \in \Phi_0\) let \(\pi_\alpha \colon \gg \to \gg_\alpha\) be the projection map with kernel \(\bigoplus_{\beta \in \Phi_0 \setminus \{\alpha\}} \gg_\beta\) and let \(\theta \colon \gg \to \gg^*\) be given by \(\theta(x)(y) = \kappa(x, y)\).

  1. We have \(\kappa(\gg_\lambda, \gg_\mu) = 0\) unless \(\lambda + \mu = 0\), hence if \(\theta_\alpha\) denotes the restriction of \(\theta\) to \(\gg_\alpha\), then \(\theta_\alpha\) maps \(\gg_\alpha\) to \(\left(\bigoplus_{\beta \in \Phi_0 \setminus \{-\alpha\}} \gg_\beta\right)^0 = \pi_{-\alpha}^\intercal(\gg_{-\alpha}^*)\).
  2. The Killing form \(\kappa\) is nondegenerate, and hence \(\gg\) semisimple, if and only if \(\theta_\alpha\) is an isomorphism for each \(\alpha \in \Phi_0\), so that in particular \(\kappa\) is nondegenerate on \(\hh\) and \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = \dim(\gg_{-\alpha})\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\).
Proof.

For \(i)\) first recall that, for any \(\alpha_1, \alpha_2 \in D(\hh)_0\) we have \([\gg_{\alpha_1}, \gg_{\alpha_2}] \subseteq \gg_{\alpha_1 + \alpha_2}\). It follows that if \(x \in \gg_\alpha\), \(y \in \gg_\beta\) where \(\alpha + \beta = \delta \neq 0\) then if \(\psi = \ad(x) \circ \ad(y)\) we have \(\psi(\gg_\gamma) \subseteq \gg_{\gamma + \delta}\) for any \(\gamma \in \Phi_0\). By induction it follows that \(\psi^k(\gg_\gamma) \subseteq \gg_{\gamma + k\delta}\) for all \(k \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}\). Now if we let \(\partial\Phi_0 := \{\gamma_1 - \gamma_2 : \gamma_1, \gamma_2 \in \Phi_0\}\) then since \(\Phi_0\) is a finite set, \(\partial\Phi_0\) is also. But if \(k = \min\{k \in \mathbb{N} : k\delta \notin \partial\Phi_0\}\) then it follows immediately that \(\psi^k = 0\) as required. It follows immediately that \(\theta(\gg_\alpha) \subseteq \left(\bigoplus_{\beta \in \Phi_0 \setminus \{-\alpha\}} \gg_\beta\right)^0 = \pi_{-\alpha}^\intercal(\gg_{-\alpha}^*)\).

For \(ii)\) note that \(\theta\) is an isomorphism if and only if \(\theta_\alpha\) is for each \(\alpha \in \Phi_0\), hence \(\kappa\) is nondegenerate on \(\hh\) and \(\theta(\gg_\alpha) = \gg_{-\alpha}^*\) and hence \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = \dim(\gg_{-\alpha})\).

□
Remark 6.1.2.

When \(\gg\) is semisimple, by Proposition 6.1.1, \(\kappa\) induces an isomorphism \(\theta_0 \colon \hh \to \hh^*\). Given \(\lambda \in \hh^*\), we will write \(t_\lambda \in \hh\) for \(\theta_0^{-1}(\lambda)\), so that \(t_\lambda\) is uniquely determined by the condition that \(\kappa(t_\lambda, h) = \lambda(h)\) for all \(h \in \hh\).

Lemma 6.1.3.

If \((\gg, \hh)\) is a Cartan pair and \(\kappa\) is the Killing form of \(\gg\), then we have \[\kappa(h_1, h_2) = \sum_{\alpha \in \Phi} \dim(\gg_\alpha)\,\alpha(h_1)\,\alpha(h_2), \qquad \forall\, h_1, h_2 \in \hh. \tag{6.1.1}\] If \(\gg\) is semisimple, it follows that

  1. \(\langle \Phi \rangle_k = \hh^*\) and hence \(\hh\) is abelian.
  2. Recall \(\hh_\alpha = [\gg_\alpha, \gg_{-\alpha}] \subseteq \hh\). We have \(\hh_\alpha \cap \ker(\alpha) = \{0\}\), and hence \(\dim(\hh_\alpha) \le 1\) and \(\alpha(\hh_\alpha) \neq 0\) if and only if \(\hh_\alpha \neq \{0\}\).
Proof.

Since \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Phi_0} \gg_\lambda\) as an \(\hh\)-representation, \(\kappa(h_1, h_2) = \sum_{\lambda \in \Phi_0} t_{\gg_\lambda}(h_1, h_2)\). Equation (6.1.1) thus follows immediately by applying Lemma 2.2.14 to the \(\hh\)-representations \(\gg_\lambda\). (Note that \(\ad(h_1)\ad(h_2)\) lies in the associative algebra of linear maps which preserve a composition series of \(\gg_\lambda\) even though it may not lie in \(\ad(\hh)\).)

Now for claim \(i)\), suppose that \(\gg\) is semisimple and let \(S = \langle \Phi \rangle_k\). Since \(\Phi \subseteq D(\hh)_0 \subseteq \hh^*\), clearly \(S \subseteq D(\hh)_0\). Let \(S^0 = \Phi^0 = \{h \in \hh : \alpha(h) = 0,\; \forall\, \alpha \in \Phi\}\), where, since \(\hh\) is finite-dimensional, we view \(S^0\) as a subspace of \(\hh\) via the canonical isomorphism \((\hh^*)^* \cong \hh\). It is clear from (6.1.1) that \(S^0 \subseteq \rad(\kappa|_\hh)\), but if \(\gg\) is semisimple, part \(ii)\) of Proposition 6.1.1 shows that this is \(\{0\}\). But then \(S = (S^0)^0 = \hh^*\), so that \(\Phi\) spans \(\hh^*\). But then \(\hh^* = S \subseteq D(\hh)_0\) and hence \(D(\hh) = \{0\}\), that is, \(\hh\) is abelian.

Finally, by Lemma 5.2.11 applied to the adjoint representation of \(\gg\), if \(\beta \in \Phi\), then we have \(\beta|_{\hh_\alpha} = r_\beta \cdot \alpha|_{\hh_\alpha}\) for some \(r_\beta \in \mathbb{Q}\). But then if \(z \in \hh_\alpha \cap \ker(\alpha)\) it follows \(\beta(z) = r_\beta \cdot \alpha(z) = 0\) for all \(\beta \in \Phi\). Hence \(\ker(\alpha) \cap \hh_\alpha \subseteq \rad(\kappa) = \{0\}\) and hence \(\alpha|_{\hh_\alpha}\) is injective and \(ii)\) clearly follows.

□

6.1.1 The roots of a semisimple Lie algebra

Definition 6.1.4.

Recall that, as in Definition 2.2.15, if \(W\) is an \(\hh\)-representation then we write \(W^s\) for the socle of \(W\), that is, the sum of all irreducible \(\hh\)-subrepresentations of \(W\). If \((\gg, \hh)\) is a Cartan pair and \(\gg = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Phi_0} \gg_\lambda\) is the decomposition of \(\gg\) as an \(\hh\)-representation, then the \(\hh\)-representation \(\gg_\lambda\) has \(k_\lambda\) as its only composition factor and hence \(\gg_\lambda^s = \{x \in \gg_\lambda : [h, x] = \lambda(h) \cdot x,\; \forall\, h \in \hh\}\) must be non-zero. If \(x \in \gg_\lambda^s\) and \(x \neq 0\) then \(k \cdot x \cong k_\alpha\) as \(\hh\)-representations. For example, \(\gg_0^s = \gg^\hh\), i.e. \(\gg_0^s\) is the subrepresentation of \(\hh\)-invariants in \(\gg\), which, by Lemma 6.1.3, is all of \(\hh\) when \(\gg\) is semisimple.

The following Proposition shows that the Cartan decomposition is as fine as possible in that each \(\gg_\alpha\) is 1-dimensional, and moreover each \(\gg_\alpha\) sits naturally in a subalgebra of \(\gg\) isomorphic to \(\spl_2\).

Proposition 6.1.5.

Let \((\gg, \hh)\) be a Cartan pair such that \(\gg\) is semisimple, and let \(\gg = \hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) be the associated Cartan decomposition.

  1. If \(e_\alpha \in \gg_\alpha^s\) is nonzero, then \(\ad(e_\alpha)(\gg_{-\alpha}) = k \cdot t_\alpha = \hh_\alpha\) so that \(\hh = \hh_\alpha \oplus \ker(\alpha)\). In particular, if \(h_\alpha \in \hh_\alpha\) is given by \(\alpha(h_\alpha) = 2\), there is an \(f_\alpha \in \gg_{-\alpha}\) such that \([e_\alpha, f_\alpha] = h_\alpha\).
  2. \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = 1\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\) and if \(k \in \mathbb{Z}\) and \(\alpha \in \Phi\) then \(k \cdot \alpha \in \Phi\) if and only if \(k \in \{\pm 1\}\). In particular, the maps \(\{\ad(h) : h \in \hh\}\) are simultaneously diagonalisable with the Cartan decomposition giving their simultaneous eigenspaces.
  3. For each \(\alpha \in \Phi\) let \(\spl_\alpha := \gg_\alpha \oplus \hh_\alpha \oplus \gg_{-\alpha}\). Then \(\spl_\alpha = k \cdot e_\alpha \oplus k \cdot h_\alpha \oplus k \cdot f_\alpha \cong \spl_2\).
Proof.

For \(i)\) take any \(e_\alpha \in \gg_\alpha^s\), \(y \in \gg_{-\alpha}\) and \(h \in \hh\). Then for all \(h \in \hh\), \[\theta_0([e_\alpha, y])(h) := \kappa(h, [e_\alpha, y]) = \kappa([h, e_\alpha], y) = \kappa(e_\alpha, y)\,\alpha(h).\] Thus \([e_\alpha, y] = \theta_0^{-1}(\kappa(e_\alpha, y)\,\alpha) = \kappa(e_\alpha, y)\,t_\alpha\). By part \(ii)\) of Lemma 6.1.3, we know \(\hh_\alpha \cap \ker(\alpha) = \{0\}\) so that \(\dim(\hh_\alpha) \le 1\). But since \(\theta_\alpha(\gg_\alpha) = \gg_{-\alpha}^*\) it follows that we may find \(y_0 \in \gg_{-\alpha}\) with \([e_\alpha, y_0] = t_\alpha\) and since \(t_\alpha \in \hh_\alpha \setminus \{0\}\), it follows that \(\alpha(t_\alpha) \neq 0\), hence if \(f_\alpha = 2y_0 / \alpha(t_\alpha)\) then \([e_\alpha, f_\alpha] = h_\alpha\) where \(\alpha(h_\alpha) = 2\).

Now consider \(M = k \cdot e_\alpha \oplus \hh_\alpha \oplus \bigoplus_{k > 0} \gg_{-k\alpha}\). Since \(\ad(e_\alpha)(e_\alpha) = 0\), \(\ad(e_\alpha)(h_\alpha) = -2e_\alpha\), \(\ad(e_\alpha)(\gg_{-\alpha}) = \hh_\alpha\) and \(\ad(e_\alpha)(\gg_{-k\alpha}) \subseteq \gg_{(-k+1)\alpha}\) for each \(k > 1\), it follows that \(M\) is \(\ad(e_\alpha)\)-stable. Similarly \(M\) is \(\ad(f_\alpha)\)-stable since \(\ad(f_\alpha)(e_\alpha) = -h_\alpha\) and \(\ad(f_\alpha)(h_\alpha) = -\ad(h_\alpha)(f_\alpha) \in \gg_{-\alpha}\) and \(\ad(f_\alpha)\) clearly preserves \(\bigoplus_{k > 0} \gg_{-k\alpha}\). But then \(\tr(\ad(h_\alpha)) = \tr([\ad(e_\alpha), \ad(f_\alpha)]) = 0\). On the other hand, \(\ad(h_\alpha)\) has the unique eigenvalue \(2k\) on \(\gg_{k\alpha}\), so that we have \[0 = \tr_M(\ad(h_\alpha)) = 2 \cdot 1 + 0 \cdot 1 - 2\sum_{k > 0} k \cdot \dim(\gg_{-k\alpha}).\]

It follows that we must have \(\dim(\gg_{-\alpha}) = 1\) and \(\dim(\gg_{-k\alpha}) = 0\) for all \(k \ge 2\). Since \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = \dim(\gg_{-\alpha})\) it follows that \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = 1\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\) and hence \(\hh\) acts diagonalisably on \(\gg\). Moreover if \(\alpha \in \Phi\) then if \(k \in \mathbb{Z}\), \(k\alpha \in \Phi\) if and only if \(k \in \{\pm 1\}\).

Finally, since \(\ad(h_\alpha)\) acts diagonalisably and \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = 1\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\) it follows immediately that \(\spl_\alpha = \operatorname{span}_k\{e_\alpha, h_\alpha, f_\alpha\}\) and that \((e_\alpha, h_\alpha, f_\alpha)\) form an \(\spl_2\)-triple, so that \(\spl_\alpha \cong \spl_2\) for each \(\alpha \in \Phi\).

□
Definition 6.1.6.

Given a root \(\alpha \in \Phi\), the element \(h_\alpha\) is known as the coroot associated to \(\alpha\). We will write \(\Phi^\vee = \{h_\alpha : \alpha \in \Phi\} \subset \hh\) for the set of coroots.

Proposition 6.1.7.

Let \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\) and set \(S_\alpha(\beta) = \{\beta + k\alpha : k \in \mathbb{Z}\} \cap \Phi_0\). Then if \(p\), respectively \(q \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}\) are maximal among positive integers \(k\) with the property that \(\beta - k\alpha \in \Phi_0\) (respectively \(\beta + k\alpha \in \Phi_0\)) then

  1. \(S_\alpha(\beta) = \{\beta + j\alpha : -p \le j \le q\}\),
  2. \[\beta(h_\alpha) = \kappa(h_\alpha, t_\beta) = \frac{2\kappa(t_\alpha, t_\beta)}{\kappa(t_\alpha, t_\alpha)} = p - q.\] In particular \(\beta - \beta(h_\alpha) \cdot \alpha \in \Phi\).
  3. If \(\beta = c \cdot \alpha\) for some \(c \in k\), then \(c = \pm 1\).
Proof.

If \(\beta \in \mathbb{Z} \cdot \alpha\) then all of the assertions of the Proposition follow from Proposition 6.1.5. Thus we may assume that \(\beta \notin \mathbb{Z} \cdot \alpha\) and hence for all \(k \in \mathbb{Z}\) the weight \(\beta + k\alpha\) is non-zero. Let \[M_\beta = \bigoplus_{\gamma \in S_\alpha(\beta)} \gg_\gamma.\] Clearly \(M_\beta\) is an \(\spl_\alpha\)-subrepresentation of \(\gg\) on which \(h_\alpha\) has eigenvalues \(\{\beta(h_\alpha) + 2k : \beta + k\alpha \in \Phi\}\), each with a 1-dimensional eigenspace. It follows that \(M_\beta\) must be an irreducible representation of \(\spl_\alpha\), and if \(q = \max\{k \in \mathbb{Z} : \beta + k\alpha \in \Phi\}\) and \(\dim(M_\beta) = l + 1\), then \((\beta + \mathbb{Z} \cdot \alpha) \cap \Phi = \{\beta + k\alpha : q - l \le k \le q\}\), and moreover we must have \(\beta(h_\alpha) + 2(q - l) = -\beta(h_\alpha) - 2q\), so that \(\beta(h_\alpha) = l - 2q\). It follows that if we set \(p = l - q \ge 0\), then \(S_\alpha(\beta) := (\beta + \mathbb{Z} \cdot \alpha) \cap \Phi = \{\beta + k\alpha : -p \le k \le q\}\) and \(\beta(h_\alpha) = p - q\). Moreover, since \(\beta(h_\alpha)\) is an eigenvalue of \(h_\alpha\) on \(M_\beta\) so is \(-\beta(h_\alpha)\) and hence \(\beta - \beta(h_\alpha) \cdot \alpha \in S_\alpha(\beta)\). We have thus established parts \(i)\) and \(ii)\).

Now suppose that \(\beta = c \cdot \alpha\) for some \(c \in k\), \(c \notin \mathbb{Z}\). Then \(\beta(h_\alpha) = p - q = 2c\) is an odd integer (since \(0\) is not a weight of \(M_\beta\) as \(\beta \notin \mathbb{Z} \cdot \alpha\)) so that \(1\) is an eigenvalue for the action of \(h_\alpha\) on \(M_\beta\) and hence \(\frac{1}{2}\alpha \in S_\alpha(\beta) \subseteq \Phi\). But since \(\alpha = 2(\frac{1}{2}\alpha)\), this contradicts part \(ii)\) of Proposition 6.1.5.

□

6.1.2 Rational form of \(\hh\) and inner product spaces

Definition 6.1.8.

Let \(\hh_\QQ = \{h \in \hh : \alpha(h) \in \QQ,\; \forall\, \alpha \in \Phi\}\). Clearly \(\hh_\QQ\) is a \(\QQ\)-vector space (a subspace of \(\hh\) viewed as a \(\QQ\)-vector space). Recall also that a symmetric bilinear form \((\cdot, \cdot)\) on a \(\QQ\)-vector space \(V\) is said to be positive definite if \((v, v) \ge 0\) with equality holding precisely when \(v = 0\). Such a form is also commonly called an inner product.

Lemma 6.1.9.

The \(\QQ\)-vector space \(\hh_\QQ\) has the following properties

  1. If \(\kappa_\QQ\) denotes the restriction of the Killing form to \(\hh_\QQ\), then \(\kappa_\QQ\) is a \(\QQ\)-valued positive definite symmetric bilinear form on \(\hh_\QQ\).
  2. \(\hh_\QQ = \langle \Phi^\vee \rangle_\QQ = \langle \{t_\alpha : \alpha \in \Phi\} \rangle_\QQ\), and \(\dim_\QQ(\hh_\QQ) = \dim_k(\hh)\). In particular, \(\theta\) identifies the dual \((\hh_\QQ)^*\) of \(\hh_\QQ\) with \(\langle \Phi \rangle_\QQ\).
Proof.

For part \(i)\), since we have shown in Proposition 6.1.5 that \(\dim(\gg_\alpha) = 1\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\), we may simplify the expression for \(\kappa|_\hh\) given in (6.1.1) to obtain \(\kappa(h_1, h_2) = \sum_{\gamma \in \Phi} \gamma(h_1)\gamma(h_2)\). It is thus immediate from the definition of \(\hh_\QQ\) that the Killing form is \(\QQ\)-valued on \(\hh_\QQ\). Moreover, if \(h \in \hh_\QQ\), since \(\gamma(h)^2 \ge 0\) for all \(\gamma \in \Phi\), we have \(\kappa_\QQ(h, h) \ge 0\), with equality if and only if \(\gamma(h) = 0\) for all \(\gamma \in \Phi\), and since \(\langle \Phi \rangle = \hh^*\), this holds only if \(h = 0\). Thus \(\kappa_\QQ\) is positive definite as claimed.

For part \(ii)\), first pick a basis \(\{\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_l\} \subseteq \Phi\) of \(\hh^*\). Then the map \(c \colon \hh \to k^l\) given by \(c(h) = (\gamma_i(h))_{i=1}^l\) is an isomorphism, and by definition it restricts to an injective \(\QQ\)-linear map \(c_\QQ \colon \hh_\QQ \to \QQ^l\), so that \(\dim_\QQ(\hh_\QQ) \le l\). On the other hand, if \(h_\alpha\) is a coroot, \(\gamma(h_\alpha) \in \ZZ\) for all \(\gamma \in \Phi\), hence \(h_\alpha \in \hh_\QQ\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi\). Since \(\Phi^\vee\) spans \(\hh\), it contains a basis for \(\hh\) which thus consists of \(l\) coroots which are linearly independent over \(k\), and hence certainly over \(\QQ\). It follows \(\dim_\QQ(\hh_\QQ) \ge l\), and hence this combines with the previous paragraph to show that \(\dim(\hh_\QQ) = l = \dim_k(\hh)\).

□
Definition 6.1.10.

Let \((-,-)\) denote the bilinear form on \(\hh^*\) which is obtained by identifying \(\hh^*\) with \(\hh\): that is \[(\lambda, \mu) = \kappa(t_\lambda, t_\mu).\] Clearly it is a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, and via the previous Lemma, for all \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\) we have \((\alpha, \beta) = \kappa(t_\alpha, t_\beta) \in \QQ\), so that it restricts to a \(\QQ\)-valued symmetric bilinear form on \(\hh^*_\QQ\) which is positive definite.

6.2 Abstract root systems and the classification theorem

In this section we study the geometry which we are led to by the configuration of roots associated to a Cartan decomposition of a semisimple Lie algebra. These configurations will turn out to have a very special, highly symmetric, form which allows them to be completely classified.

We will work with rational inner product spaces \(V\), that is, \(\QQ\)-vector spaces \(V\) equipped with a positive-definite symmetric bilinear form which we will denote by \((\cdot, \cdot)\). Such vector spaces have, in addition to a notion of length given by, for any \(v \in V\), the norm \(\|v\| = (v, v)^{1/2}\), a notion of angle: by the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality there is a unique \(\theta \in [0, \pi]\) with \[\cos(\theta) = \frac{(v_1, v_2)}{\|v_1\| \cdot \|v_2\|} \in [-1, 1].\] The group of orthogonal linear transformations of \(V\) is \[O(V) = \{g \in \GL(V) : (g(v), g(w)) = (v, w),\; \forall\, v, w \in V\}.\]

Definition 6.2.1.

A reflection is a nontrivial element of \(O(V)\) which fixes a subspace of codimension 1 (i.e. dimension \(\dim(V) - 1\)). If \(s \in O(V)\) is a reflection and \(W < V\) is the \(+1\)-eigenspace, then \(L = W^\perp\) is a line preserved by \(s\), hence the restriction \(s|_L\) of \(s\) to \(L\) is an element of \(O(L) = \{\pm 1\}\), which since \(s\) is nontrivial must be \(-1\). In particular \(s\) has order 2. If \(v\) is any nonzero element of \(L\) then it is easy to check that \(s\) is given by \[s(u) = u - \frac{2(u, v)}{(v, v)}\,v.\] Given \(v \neq 0\) we will write \(s_v\) for the reflection given by the above formula, and refer to it as the “reflection in the hyperplane perpendicular to \(v\)”.

We now give the definition which captures the geometry of the roots of a semisimple Lie algebra.

Definition 6.2.2.

A pair \((V, \Phi)\) consisting of a rational inner product space \(V\) and a finite subset \(\Phi \subset V \setminus \{0\}\) is called an (abstract) root system if it satisfies the following properties:

  1. \(\Phi\) spans \(V\);
  2. If \(\alpha \in \Phi\), \(c \in \QQ\), then \(c\alpha \in \Phi\) if and only if \(c = \pm 1\);
  3. If \(\alpha \in \Phi\) then \(s_\alpha \colon V \to V\) preserves \(\Phi\);
  4. If \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\) and we define \[\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \frac{2(\alpha, \beta)}{(\alpha, \alpha)}, \tag{6.2.1}\] then \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle \in \ZZ\). We say \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle\) is a Cartan integer.

This definition is, unsurprisingly, motivated by the following result.

Lemma 6.2.3.

Let \((\gg, \hh)\) be a Cartan pair where \(\gg\) is semisimple, and let \(\gg = \hh \oplus \bigoplus_{\alpha \in \Phi} \gg_\alpha\) be the associated Cartan decomposition and let \(\hh^*_\QQ\) be the \(\QQ\)-span of \(\Phi\) in \(\hh^*\). Then \((\hh^*_\QQ, \Phi)\) is an abstract root system.

Proof.

Let \((-,-)\) denote the symmetric bilinear form on \(\hh^*_\QQ\) induced by the restriction of the Killing form \(\kappa|_\hh\) as in §6.1.2. Lemma 6.1.9 shows that this restriction is positive definite. Property \(i)\) for an abstract root system follows immediately from the definitions, and the remaining properties follow from Proposition 6.1.7: part \(ii)\) of that Proposition establishes property \(ii)\), while part \(i)\) establishes properties \(iii)\) and \(iv)\).

□

Remarkably, the finite set of vectors given by a root system has both a rich enough structure that it captures the isomorphism type of a semisimple Lie algebra, but is also explicit enough that we can completely classify them, and hence classify semisimple Lie algebras.

Definition 6.2.4.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a root system. Then the Weyl group of the root system is the group \(W = \langle s_\alpha : \alpha \in \Phi \rangle\). Since its generators preserve the finite set \(\Phi\) and these vectors span \(V\), it follows that it is a finite subgroup of \(O(V)\).

Example 6.2.5.

Let \(\gg = \spl_n\). Then let \(\mathfrak{d}_n\) denote the diagonal matrices in \(\gl_n\) and \(\hh\) the (traceless) diagonal matrices in \(\spl_n\). As you saw in the problem sets, \(\hh\) forms a Cartan subalgebra in \(\spl_n\). Let \(\{\varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le n\}\) be the basis of \(\mathfrak{d}_n^*\) dual to the basis \(\{E_{ii} : 1 \le i \le n\}\) of \(\mathfrak{d}_n\) in \(\gl_n\). The Cartan decomposition of \(\spl_n\) is \(\hh \oplus \bigoplus_{1 \le i \neq j \le n} k \cdot E_{ij}\), where \(\ad(h)(E_{ij}) = (h_i - h_j)E_{ij}\), where \(h_k = \varepsilon_k(h)\) for \(k \in \{1, \ldots, n\}\). Thus \[\hh_\QQ = \left\{\sum_{i=1}^n h_i E_{ii} : h_i \in \QQ,\; \sum_{i=1}^n h_i = 0\right\}\] and \[\hh^*_\QQ = \left\langle \sum_{i=1}^n c_i \varepsilon_i : c_i \in \QQ \right\rangle \Big/ \QQ \cdot (\varepsilon_1 + \cdots + \varepsilon_n),\] where the roots in \(\hh^*_\QQ\) are the (images of the) vectors \(\{\varepsilon_i - \varepsilon_j : 1 \le i, j \le n,\; i \neq j\}\). Moreover, the Killing form for \(\spl_n\) is \(\kappa(x, y) = 2n \cdot \tr(xy)\), so the \(\{E_{ii} : 1 \le i \le n\}\) are an orthogonal basis of \(\hh\) with \(\kappa(E_{ii}, E_{ii}) = 2n\).

The Weyl group \(W\) in this case is the group generated by the reflections \(s_\alpha\) which, for \(\alpha = \varepsilon_i - \varepsilon_j\), interchange the basis vectors \(\varepsilon_i\) and \(\varepsilon_j\), so it is easy to see that \(W\) is just the symmetric group on \(n\) letters.

6.2.1 Root systems and bases

If \((V, \Phi)\) is an abstract root system then, since the set of roots \(\Phi\) spans \(V\), it certainly contains (many) subsets which form a basis of \(V\). The key to the classification of root systems is to show that there is a special class of such bases which capture enough of the geometry of the set of roots that the entire root system can be recovered from the bases of this form. This section is devoted to determining the “correct” notion of a special basis or “base” and establishing that this class of basis is always nonempty.

We first record some useful notation:

Definition 6.2.6.

Given a set of vectors \(X\) in a vector space \(V\), we will write \[\NN \cdot X = \left\{\sum_{s \in Y} c_s \cdot s : Y \subseteq X \text{ finite},\; c_s \in \NN\right\} \subseteq \ZZ \cdot X = \left\{\sum_{s \in Y} c_s \cdot s : Y \subseteq X \text{ finite},\; c_s \in \ZZ\right\}.\] The set \(\NN \cdot X\) is closed under vector addition and multiplication by elements of \(\NN\).

Recall that the invariance of the Killing form together with the fact that it is nondegenerate in the semisimple case implies that the set of roots is a union of pairs \(\{\pm \alpha\}\) consisting of a root and its negative. This suggests that it may be useful to have a notion of positivity for vectors in a \(\QQ\)-vector space analogous to that in \(\QQ\), namely, a set of “positive” vectors \(\mathcal{P}\) in \(V\).

Definition 6.2.7.

Let \(V\) be a \(\QQ\)-vector space. A positive system \(\mathcal{P}\) in \(V\) is a subset \(\mathcal{P} \subseteq V \setminus \{0\}\) such that

  • For each \(v \in V \setminus \{0\}\), exactly one of \(v\) or \(-v\) lies in \(\mathcal{P}\).
  • If \(v_1, v_2 \in \mathcal{P}\) and \(\lambda \in \QQ_{>0}\) then \(\lambda \cdot v_1,\; v_1 + v_2 \in \mathcal{P}\).

If \(\mathcal{P}\) is a positive system, then we define a total order \(<\) on \(V\) by \(v_1 < v_2\) if and only if \(v_2 - v_1 \in \mathcal{P}\).

Example 6.2.8.

If \(V\) is a \(\QQ\)-vector space and \(B\) is a basis for \(V\) then we will write \(\vec{B} = (e_1, \ldots, e_l)\) for an ordering of \(B\), that is, a bijection \(i \mapsto e_i\) from \(\{1, 2, \ldots, l\}\) to \(B\). The ordering equips the basis of \(V^*\) dual to \(B\) with an ordering, and we denote this ordered basis by \(\vec{B}^* = (\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_l)\) so that for any \(v \in V\) we have \(v = \sum_{i=1}^l \delta_i(v) \cdot e_i\). Let \(s_{\vec{B}} \colon V \setminus \{0\} \to \{1, \ldots, l\}\) be given by \(s_{\vec{B}}(v) = \min\{k : 1 \le k \le l,\; \delta_k(v) \neq 0\}\), and, if \(v \in V \setminus \{0\}\) has \(s_{\vec{B}}(v) = k\), let \(p_{\vec{B}}(v) = \delta_k(v)\). Then \[\mathcal{P}(\vec{B}) = \{v \in V \setminus \{0\} : p_{\vec{B}}(v) > 0\} = \left\{\sum_{i=k}^l \lambda_i e_i : k \in \{1, 2, \ldots, l\},\; \lambda_k > 0\right\}\] is a positive system.

Though we will not need the result, the following Lemma shows that all positive systems in a real inner product space arise via the real analogue of the above construction.

Lemma 6.2.9.

Let \(\mathcal{P}\) be a positive system in a real inner product space. Then there is a basis \(B\) of \(V\) such that \(\mathcal{P} = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{B}}\) for some ordering \(\vec{B}\) of \(B\).

Proof.

Since \(\mathcal{P}\) and \(-\mathcal{P}\) are convex subsets of a Euclidean vector space there is a linear functional \(\lambda \colon V \to \RR\) which separates \(\mathcal{P}\) and \(-\mathcal{P}\). Since \(V \setminus \{0\} = \mathcal{P} \sqcup (-\mathcal{P})\), by changing the sign of \(\lambda\) if necessary we may assume that \(\mathcal{P} \subseteq \{v \in V : \lambda(v) \ge 0\} = H^+\) and \(-\mathcal{P} \subseteq \{v \in V : \lambda(v) \le 0\} = H^-\). It follows that \(\operatorname{int}(\mathcal{P}) = H^+\), and if \(H = \{v \in V : \lambda(v) = 0\}\) then \(H\) is an inner product space and \(\mathcal{P}_H = \mathcal{P} \cap H\) is a positive system in \(H\), so that the theorem follows by induction on \(\dim(V)\).

□

If \((V, \Phi)\) is an abstract root system and \(\mathcal{P}\) is a positive system on \(\Phi\), then the first property of \(\mathcal{P}\) listed above shows that \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} := \Phi \cap \mathcal{P}\) must contain exactly one element of each pair \(\{\pm \alpha\} \subseteq \Phi\) and we call it the set of (\(\mathcal{P}\)-) positive roots. When there is no danger of confusion we will write \(\Phi^+\) in place of \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\). Note that if \(B \subseteq \Phi\) is a basis of \(V\), then \(\pm B \cap \mathcal{P}\) must also be a basis of \(V\), and hence any set of positive roots \(\Phi^+\) contains a basis of \(V\).

*Remark 6.2.10.

Note that \(\ZZ\Phi\) is a finitely generated abelian group which, since it lies in a \(\QQ\)-vector space, is torsion-free. It must therefore be free, and hence \(\ZZ\Phi\) has a basis \(B\), which is then certainly also a basis of \(V\). Moreover, as noted above, if \(V\) is equipped with a positive system, then we may be chosen to consist of positive vectors. It is not yet clear that such a basis must lie in \(\Phi\), but this turns out to be the case. This hopefully motivates the following definition:

Definition 6.2.11.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a root system, and let \(\Delta\) be a subset of \(\Phi\). We say that \(\Delta\) is a base (or a set of simple roots) for \(\Phi\) if

  1. \(\Delta\) is linearly independent.
  2. If \(\Phi^\pm_\Delta = \pm \NN \cdot \Delta \cap \Phi\) are the \(\Delta\)-positive/negative roots, then \(\Phi = \Phi^+_\Delta \sqcup \Phi^-_\Delta\).

Note that \(ii)\) implies that \(V = \langle \Phi \rangle_\QQ \subseteq \QQ \cdot \Delta\) so that by \(i)\) \(\Delta\) is a basis of \(V\). Indeed by \(ii)\) we have \(\ZZ \cdot \Delta = \ZZ\Phi\) so that the existence of a base implies that \(\ZZ\Phi\) is a free abelian group.

Condition \(ii)\) in the definition of a set of simple roots implies that such a set has an associated set of positive roots \(\Phi^+_\Delta\), and the key to establishing the existence of sets of simple roots is to show that one can recover \(\Delta\) from this positive set. We do this using the following definition:

Definition 6.2.12.

Given a set of positive roots \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\), we say that \(\alpha \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) is decomposable if \(\alpha = \beta + \gamma\) for some \(\beta, \gamma \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\). A root is indecomposable if it is not decomposable. Let \(\Pi_\mathcal{P}\) be the set of indecomposable roots in \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\).

Proposition 6.2.13.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be an abstract root system.

  1. Suppose that \(\mathcal{P}\) is a positive system. Then the set of indecomposable roots \(\Pi = \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) is a base of \((V, \Phi)\). In particular, every root system has a base and the map \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} \mapsto \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) assigning to a set of positive roots its indecomposable elements is bijective with inverse \(\Pi_\mathcal{P} \mapsto \NN \cdot \Pi_\mathcal{P} \cap \Phi\).
  2. Every base of \((V, \Phi)\) is of the form \(\Pi_\mathcal{P}\) for some positive system \(\mathcal{P}\), thus the map \(\Delta \to \Phi^+_\Delta\) gives a bijection between the set of all bases of \((V, \Phi)\) and the set \(\{\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} : \mathcal{P} \text{ a positive system}\}\) of all choices of a subset of positive roots. Indeed for any \(\mathcal{P}\) with \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} = \Phi^+_\Delta\) we have \(\Pi_\mathcal{P} = \Delta\).
Proof.

We establish part \(i)\) in three steps:

Claim 1: \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} \subseteq \NN \cdot \Pi\).

Proof: Note that the set \(\Phi^+\) is totally ordered by the order given by \(\mathcal{P}\), so if \(\Phi^+\) is not contained in \(\NN \cdot \Pi\), we may consider \(\alpha \in \Phi^+\), the minimal element of \(\Phi^+\) not contained in \(\NN \cdot \Pi\). Clearly \(\alpha \notin \Pi\), hence \(\alpha\) is decomposable and there are \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2 \in \Phi^+\) with \(\alpha = \gamma_1 + \gamma_2\). But then \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2 < \alpha\), so by minimality of \(\alpha\), \(\gamma_1, \gamma_2 \in \NN \cdot \Pi\). But \(\NN \cdot \Pi\) is closed under addition, so \(\alpha = \gamma_1 + \gamma_2 \in \NN \cdot \Pi\), giving a contradiction. Hence \(\Phi^+ \subseteq \NN \cdot \Pi\) as required, and \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} \mapsto \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) is bijective.

Claim 2: If \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) are distinct roots such that \((\alpha, \beta) > 0\), then at least one of them must be decomposable.

Proof: First note that \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle, \langle \beta, \alpha \rangle > 0\). Since \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi^+\) are distinct, they are linearly independent, and hence by Cauchy–Schwarz, \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle \cdot \langle \beta, \alpha \rangle \in \{1, 2, 3\}\). It follows one of \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle\) or \(\langle \beta, \alpha \rangle = 1\). By symmetry we may assume \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = 1\), and hence \(s_\alpha(\beta) = \beta - \alpha \in \Phi\). But then one of \(\alpha - \beta\) or \(\beta - \alpha\) lies in \(\Phi^+\). But as \(\alpha = (\alpha - \beta) + \beta\) and \(\beta = (\beta - \alpha) + \alpha\), it follows that one of \(\alpha\) or \(\beta\) must be decomposable as required.

Claim 3: Let \(S\) be any subset of \(\mathcal{P}\) with the property that if \(s, t \in S\) are distinct, then \((s, t) \le 0\). Then \(S\) is linearly independent.

Proof: Suppose that \(\sum_{s \in S} c_s \cdot s = 0\) is a linear dependence. Let \(S^\pm = \{s \in S : \pm c_s > 0\}\), and set \(z = \sum_{t \in S^+} c_t \cdot t = \sum_{s \in S^-} (-c_s) \cdot s\) and note that \(z \in \mathcal{P}\) unless both \(S^+\) and \(S^-\) are empty. But \[(z, z) = \left(\sum_{t \in S^+} c_t \cdot t,\; \sum_{s \in S^-} (-c_s) \cdot s\right) = \sum_{s, t} c_t(-c_s)(s, t) \le 0\] so that we must have \(z = 0\), and hence \(S^+ = S^- = \emptyset\) and hence \(c_s = 0\) for all \(s \in S\) and so \(S\) is linearly independent as required.

Claim 1 shows that \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P} \subseteq \NN\Pi\) and claim 2 shows that \(\Pi\) satisfies the hypothesis of the set \(S\) in Claim 3, hence it is linearly independent and therefore a base as required.

For part \(ii)\), given a base \(\Delta\), pick an arbitrary ordering \(\vec{\Delta} = \{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_l\}\). Then \(\vec{\Delta}\) is an ordered basis of \(V\) and hence gives a positive system \(\mathcal{P} = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{\Delta}}\) as in Example 6.2.8. Now \(\Phi^+_\Delta = \NN \cdot \Delta \cap \Phi \subseteq \mathcal{P}\) since \(\Delta \subseteq \mathcal{P}\), and hence \(\Phi^+_\Delta \subseteq \Phi \cap \mathcal{P} = \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\), and since \(\Phi^+_\Delta\) and \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) both have \(\frac{1}{2}|\Phi|\) elements, \(\Phi^+_\Delta = \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\). (Thus the set of positive roots \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) is independent of the ordering of \(\Delta\) which we chose to obtain \(\mathcal{P}\)). Now since \(\Delta\) is a basis of \(\ZZ\Phi\) we may define \(h \colon \ZZ\Phi \to \ZZ\) by setting \(h(\alpha) = 1\) for all \(\alpha \in \Delta\). Then \(h(\gamma) \ge 1\) for all \(\gamma \in \Phi^+_\Delta\) and so \(h(\alpha) \ge 2\) if \(\alpha\) is decomposable. It follows that \(\Delta \subseteq \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) and since \(|\Delta| = |\Pi_\mathcal{P}| = \dim(V)\) we must have \(\Delta = \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) as required.

□
Lemma 6.2.14.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be an abstract root system, and suppose that \(\alpha \in \Phi\).

  1. There is a positive system \(\mathcal{P}\) for which \(\alpha\) is the minimal element of \(\Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\). In particular, \(\alpha \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) is indecomposable, and so belongs to \(\Pi_\mathcal{P}\), a base of \((V, \Phi)\).
  2. If \(\Delta\) is a base and \(\alpha \in \Delta\), then if \(\beta \in \Phi^+_\Delta\) and \(\beta \neq \alpha\), then \(s_\alpha(\beta) \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\). Hence if we write \(\Phi^+(\alpha) = \Phi^+ \setminus \{\alpha\}\) then we have \(s_\alpha(\Phi^+(\alpha)) = \Phi^+(\alpha)\) whenever \(\alpha \in \Delta\).
Proof.

We use the notation of Example 6.2.8. Pick a basis \(B \subset \Phi\) of \(V\) containing \(\alpha\) and let \(\vec{B} = \{\gamma_1, \ldots, \gamma_l\}\) be an ordering of it in which \(\alpha = \gamma_l\). Let \(D = \{\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_l\}\) be the corresponding dual basis of \(V^*\) and let \(\mathcal{P} = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{B}}\). Then if \(\beta \in \Phi^+_\mathcal{P}\) has \(\beta \le \alpha\), since \(\beta \in \mathcal{P}\) and \(s_{\vec{B}}(\alpha) = l\), we must have \(s_{\vec{B}}(\beta) = l\) also, and hence \(\beta = c \cdot \alpha\) for some \(c > 0\). But since \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\) this implies \(\beta = \alpha\). It follows that \(\beta\) is minimal and hence it must be indecomposable. But then \(\beta \in \Pi_\mathcal{P}\) which we have seen is always a base.

Next if \(\Delta\) is a base containing \(\alpha\), we may take \(B\) as in part \(i)\) to be \(\Delta\), so that \(\mathcal{P} = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{\Delta}}\). If \(\vec{\Delta}_1 = s_\alpha(\vec{\Delta})\), then \(\mathcal{P}_{\vec{\Delta}_1} = s_\alpha(\mathcal{P})\). But \(s_\alpha(\gamma_i) = \gamma_i - \langle \gamma_l, \gamma_i \rangle \cdot \gamma_l\), hence the dual basis to \(\Delta_1 = s_\alpha(\Delta)\) is \(D_1 = s_\alpha^\intercal(D) = \{\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_{l-1}, -\delta_l - \sum_{i < l} \langle \gamma_l, \gamma_i \rangle \delta_i\}\). It follows that \(p_\Delta(v) = p_{\Delta_1}(v)\) for all \(v \in V \setminus \QQ \cdot \alpha\), hence \(s_\alpha(\mathcal{P}) = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{\Delta}_1}\) and \(\mathcal{P} \setminus \QQ \cdot \alpha = \mathcal{P}_{\vec{\Delta}_1} \setminus \QQ \cdot \alpha = s_\alpha(\mathcal{P}) \setminus \QQ \cdot \alpha\), so that \((\Phi \setminus \{\pm \alpha\}) \cap \mathcal{P} = (\Phi \setminus \{\pm \alpha\}) \cap \mathcal{P}_1\) as required.

□

It turns out that we can recover the entire root system provided we know a base for it. Before we can show this, we first show that any two bases of \(\Phi\) are conjugate under the action of \(W\). Let \(\Delta_0\) be a fixed choice of base for \(\Phi\) and let \(W_0 = \langle s_\alpha : \alpha \in \Delta_0 \rangle\) be the subgroup of \(W\) generated by the reflections \(s_\alpha\) for \(\alpha\) in the base \(\Delta_0\).

Proposition 6.2.15.

Suppose that \(\Delta_1\) is any base of \((V, \Phi)\) and let \(\Phi^+_1\) be the corresponding set of positive roots. Then there is some \(w \in W_0\) such that \(w(\Phi^+_1) = \Phi^+_0\), and hence \(w(\Delta_1) = \Delta_0\).

Proof.

We prove this by induction on \(d = |\Phi^+_0 \cap \Phi^-_1|\). If \(d = 0\) then \(\Phi^+_0 = \Phi^+_1\) and hence \(\Delta_0 = \Delta_1\) (hence we may take \(w = e\) the identity element of \(W_0\)). Next suppose that \(d > 0\). Let \(\mathcal{P}_1\) be a positive system such that \(\Phi^+_1 = \Phi \cap \mathcal{P}_1\). If \(\Delta_0 \subseteq \Phi^+_1\), then since any element of \(\Phi^+_0\) is a positive integer combination of \(\Delta_0\), it follows \(\Phi^+_0 \subseteq \mathcal{P}_1 \cap \Phi = \Phi^+_1\) and hence \(\Phi^+_0 \cap \Phi^-_1 = \emptyset\), which contradicts the assumption that \(d > 0\). Thus there is some \(\alpha \in \Delta_0\) such that \(\alpha \in \Phi^-_1\). But then using the notation of Lemma 6.2.14 we see that \[|\Phi^+_0 \cap s_\alpha(\Phi^-_1)| = |s_\alpha(\Phi^+_0) \cap \Phi^-_1| = |\bigl(\{-\alpha\} \cup \Phi^+_0(\alpha)\bigr) \cap \Phi^-_1| = |\Phi^+_0(\alpha) \cap \Phi^-_1| = d - 1\] where the first equality holds because \(s_\alpha^2 = 1_V\), the second equality follows from Lemma 6.2.14, and the third from the fact that \(\Phi^-_1\) contains \(\alpha\) and hence not \(-\alpha\). But then by induction there is a \(w \in W_0\) with \(ws_\alpha(\Phi_1^+) = \Phi^+_0\). Since \(ws_\alpha \in W_0\) we are done.

□
Corollary 6.2.16.

Suppose that \(\beta \in \Phi\). Then there is a \(w \in W_0\) and an \(\alpha \in \Delta_0\) such that \(w(\beta) = \alpha\). In particular, \(W\) is generated by the reflections \(\{s_\gamma : \gamma \in \Delta_0\}\), that is, \(W = W_0\).

Proof.

The first claim follows from the fact that every root lies in a base for \((V, \Phi)\), shown in part \(i)\) of Lemma 6.2.14, together with Proposition 6.2.15.

For the final claim, note that if \(\beta \in \Phi\) then we have just shown that there is a \(w \in W_0\) such that \(w(\beta) = \gamma\) for some \(\gamma \in \Delta_0\). But then clearly \(s_\beta = w^{-1} s_\gamma w \in W_0\), and so since \(W = \langle s_\beta : \beta \in \Phi \rangle\) it follows that \(W \le W_0\). Since \(W_0 \le W\) by definition, it follows \(W = W_0\) as required.

□
Remark 6.2.17.

In fact \(W\) acts simply transitively on the bases of \((V, \Phi)\), that is, the action is transitive and, if \(\Delta\) is a base and \(w \in W\) is such that \(w(\Delta) = \Delta\), then \(w = 1\). The proof (which we will not give) consists of examining the minimal length expression for \(w\) in terms of these generators \(\{s_\alpha : \alpha \in \Delta_0\}\).

6.3 Cartan matrices and isomorphisms of root systems

First let us formulate the notion of an isomorphism of root systems:

Definition 6.3.1.

If \((V, \Phi)\) and \((V', \Phi')\) are root systems, we say that a linear map \(\phi \colon V \to V'\) is an isomorphism of root systems if it is an isomorphism of vector spaces such that \(\phi(\Phi) = \Phi'\) and \[\langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(\beta) \rangle = \langle \alpha, \beta \rangle, \qquad \forall\, \alpha, \beta \in \Phi.\] Note that \(\phi\) need not be an isometry: if \(0 < c < 1\), then \((V, c \cdot \Phi)\) is a root system which is not isometric to \((V, \Phi)\), but \(\phi(x) = c \cdot x\) is an isomorphism from \((V, \Phi)\) to \((V, c \cdot \Phi)\).

Definition 6.3.2.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a root system. The Cartan matrix associated to \((V, \Phi)\) is the matrix \[C = C_\Delta = (\langle \alpha_i, \alpha_j \rangle)_{i,j=1}^l,\] where \(\{\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \ldots, \alpha_\ell\} = \Delta\) is a base of \((V, \Phi)\). Since the elements of \(W\) are isometries, and \(W\) acts transitively on the set of bases of \(\Phi\), the Cartan matrix is independent of the choice of base (though clearly determined only up to orderings of the base \(\Delta\)), \(\forall\, \alpha, \beta \in \Delta\).

Theorem 6.3.3.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a root system. Then \((V, \Phi)\) is determined up to isomorphism by the Cartan matrix associated to it.

Proof.

Given root systems \((V, \Phi)\) and \((V', \Phi')\) with the same Cartan matrix, we may certainly pick a base \(\Delta = \{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_\ell\}\) of \((V, \Phi)\) and a base \(\Delta' = \{\beta_1, \ldots, \beta_\ell\}\) of \((V', \Phi')\) such that \(\langle \alpha_i, \alpha_j \rangle = \langle \beta_i, \beta_j \rangle\) for all \(i, j\) (\(1 \le i, j \le \ell\)). We claim the map \(\phi \colon \Delta \to \Delta'\) given by \(\phi(\alpha_i) = \beta_i\) extends to an isomorphism of root systems. Since \(\Delta\) and \(\Delta'\) are bases of \(V\) and \(V'\) respectively, \(\phi\) extends uniquely to an isomorphism of vector spaces \(\phi \colon V \to V'\), so we must show that \(\phi(\Phi) = \Phi'\), and \(\langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(\beta) \rangle = \langle \alpha, \beta \rangle\) for each \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\).

Let \(s_i = s_{\alpha_i} \in O(V)\) and \(s_i' = s_{\beta_i} \in O(V')\) be the reflections in the Weyl groups \(W = W(V, \Phi)\) and \(W' = W(V', \Phi')\) respectively. Then from the formula for the action of \(s_i\) it is clear that \(\phi(s_i(\alpha_j)) = s_i'(\beta_j) = s_i'(\phi(\alpha_i))\), so since \(\Delta\) is a basis it follows \(\phi(s_i(v)) = s_i'(\phi(v))\) for all \(v \in V\). But then since the \(s_i\)s and \(s_i'\)s generate \(W\) and \(W'\) respectively, \(\phi\) induces an isomorphism \(W \to W'\), given by \(w \mapsto w' = \phi \circ w \circ \phi^{-1}\). But by Corollary 6.2.16 we have \[\phi(\Phi) = \phi(W \cdot \Delta) = (\phi W \phi^{-1})(\phi(\Delta)) = W'\phi(\Delta) = W' \cdot \Delta' = \Phi'.\]

Finally, fixing \(\alpha \in \Delta\), clearly the linear functionals given by \(v \mapsto \langle \alpha, v \rangle\) and \(v \mapsto \langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(v) \rangle\) (\(v \in V\)) agree if \(v \in \Delta\), hence by linearity they are equal. Hence \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(\beta) \rangle\) if \(\alpha \in \Delta\) and \(\beta \in \Phi\). But since \(W\) and \(W'\) act by isometries \[\langle w(\alpha), w(\beta) \rangle = \langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(\beta) \rangle = \langle w'(\phi(\alpha)), w'(\phi(\beta)) \rangle = \langle \phi(w(\alpha)), \phi(w(\beta)) \rangle,\] so that since \(W \cdot \Delta = \Phi\), it follows that \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \langle \phi(\alpha), \phi(\beta) \rangle\) for all \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\).

□

Thus to classify root systems up to isomorphism it is enough to classify Cartan matrices. It turns out that there is a more combinatorial way to encode the information given by a Cartan matrix, because the possible entries of the Cartan matrix are heavily constrained, as the next Lemma shows:

Lemma 6.3.4.

Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a root system and let \(\alpha, \beta \in \Phi\) be such that \(\alpha \neq \pm \beta\). Then the Cartan integer \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle \in \{0, \pm 1, \pm 2, \pm 3\}\). Moreover, the angle between \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\) and the ratio \(\|\alpha\|^2 / \|\beta\|^2\) are determined by the pair \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle, \langle \beta, \alpha \rangle\), as the table below shows:

\(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle\)\(\langle \beta, \alpha \rangle\)\(\theta\)\(\|\alpha\|^2 / \|\beta\|^2\)
\(0\)\(0\)\(\pi/2\)undetermined
\(1\)\(1\)\(\pi/3\)\(1\)
\(-1\)\(-1\)\(2\pi/3\)\(1\)
\(1\)\(2\)\(\pi/4\)\(2\)
\(-1\)\(-2\)\(3\pi/4\)\(2\)
\(1\)\(3\)\(\pi/6\)\(3\)
\(-1\)\(-3\)\(5\pi/6\)\(3\)
Proof.

By assumption, we know that both \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle\) and \(\langle \beta, \alpha \rangle\) are integers with the same sign. By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, if \(\theta\) denotes the angle between \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\), then: \[\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle \langle \beta, \alpha \rangle = \frac{4(\alpha, \beta)^2}{\|\alpha\|^2 \cdot \|\beta\|^2} = 4\cos(\theta)^2 < 4. \tag{6.3.1}\] Noting that \(\cos(\theta)^2\) determines the angle between the two vectors (or rather the one which is less than \(\pi\)) and (if \((\alpha, \beta) \neq 0\)) \(\langle \beta, \alpha \rangle / \langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \|\alpha\|^2 / \|\beta\|^2\) (where we write \(\|v\|^2 = (v, v)\)), it is then easy to verify the table given above by a case-by-case check.

□
Definition 6.3.5.

As the previous Lemma shows, if \(C = (c_{ij})\) is a Cartan matrix, its entries \(c_{ij}\) are highly constrained: indeed \(c_{ii} = 2\) and if \(i \neq j\), \(c_{ij} \in \{0, -1, -2, -3\}\) and \(\{c_{ij}, c_{ji}\} = \{-1, -c_{ij}c_{ji}\}\) so that \(c_{ij}\) is determined by the product \(c_{ij} \cdot c_{ji}\) and the relative lengths of the two roots (set out in the table above). As a result, the matrix can be recorded as a kind of graph: the vertex set of the graph is labelled by the base \(\{\alpha_1, \ldots, \alpha_l\}\), and one puts \(\langle \alpha_i, \alpha_j \rangle \cdot \langle \alpha_j, \alpha_i \rangle\) edges between \(\alpha_i\) and \(\alpha_j\), directing the edges so that they go from the larger root to the smaller root. Thus for example if \(\langle \alpha_i, \alpha_j \rangle = -2\) and \(\langle \alpha_j, \alpha_i \rangle = -1\) so that \(\|\alpha_j\|^2 > \|\alpha_i\|^2\), that is, \(\alpha_j\) is longer than \(\alpha_i\), we record this in the graph as: \[\alpha_i \bullet \Longleftarrow \bullet \alpha_j\] The resulting graph is called the Dynkin diagram.

Definition 6.3.6.

We say that a root system \((V, \Phi)\) is reducible if there is a partition of the roots into two non-empty subsets \(\Phi_1 \sqcup \Phi_2\) such that \((\alpha, \beta) = 0\) for all \(\alpha \in \Phi_1\), \(\beta \in \Phi_2\). Then if we set \(V_1 = \operatorname{span}(\Phi_1)\) and \(V_2 = \operatorname{span}(\Phi_2)\), clearly \(V = V_1 \oplus V_2\) and we say \((V, \Phi)\) is the sum of the root systems \((V_1, \Phi_1)\) and \((V_2, \Phi_2)\). This allows one to reduce the classification of root systems to the classification of irreducible root systems, i.e. root systems which are not reducible. It is straightforward to check that a root system is irreducible if and only if its associated Dynkin diagram is connected.

Definition 6.3.7. (Not examinable.)

The notion of a root system makes sense over the real, as well as rational, numbers. Let \((V, \Phi)\) be a real root system, and let \(\Delta = \{\alpha_1, \alpha_2, \ldots, \alpha_l\}\) be a base of \(\Phi\). If \(v_i = \alpha_i / \|\alpha_i\|\) (\(1 \le i \le l\)) are the unit vectors in \(V\) corresponding to \(\Delta\), then they satisfy the conditions:

  1. \((v_i, v_i) = 1\) for all \(i\) and \((v_i, v_j) \le 0\) if \(i \neq j\),
  2. If \(i \neq j\) then \(4(v_i, v_j)^2 \in \{0, 1, 2, 3\}\). (This is the reason we need to extend scalars to the real numbers – if you want you could just extend scalars to \(\QQ(\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{3})\), but it makes no difference to the classification problem).

Such a set of vectors is called an admissible set.

It is straightforward to see that classifying \(\QQ\)-vector spaces with a basis which forms an admissible set is equivalent to classifying Cartan matrices, and using elementary techniques it is possible to show that the following are the only possibilities (we list the Dynkin diagram, a description of the roots, and a choice of a base):

  • Type \(A_\ell\) (\(\ell \ge 1\)): Dynkin diagram: \(\bullet - \bullet - \cdots - \bullet - \bullet\) \[V = \left\{v = \sum_{i=1}^\ell c_i e_i \in \QQ^\ell : \sum c_i = 0\right\},\quad \Phi = \{\varepsilon_i - \varepsilon_j : 1 \le i \neq j \le \ell\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_{i+1} - \varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell - 1\}.\]
  • Type \(B_\ell\) (\(\ell \ge 2\)): Dynkin diagram: \(\bullet - \bullet - \cdots - \bullet \Longrightarrow \bullet\) \[V = \QQ^\ell,\quad \Phi = \{\pm \varepsilon_i \pm \varepsilon_j : 1 \le i, j \le \ell,\; i \neq j\} \cup \{\varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_1, \varepsilon_{i+1} - \varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell - 1\}.\]
  • Type \(C_\ell\) (\(\ell \ge 3\)): Dynkin diagram: \(\bullet - \bullet - \cdots - \bullet \Longleftarrow \bullet\) \[V = \QQ^\ell,\quad \Phi = \{\pm \varepsilon_i \pm \varepsilon_j : 1 \le i, j \le \ell,\; i \neq j\} \cup \{2\varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell\},\] \[\Delta = \{2\varepsilon_1, \varepsilon_{i+1} - \varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell - 1\}.\]
  • Type \(D_\ell\) (\(\ell \ge 4\)): Dynkin diagram has a fork at one end. \[V = \QQ^\ell,\quad \Phi = \{\pm \varepsilon_i \pm \varepsilon_j : 1 \le i, j \le \ell,\; i \neq j\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_1 + \varepsilon_2, \varepsilon_{i+1} - \varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le \ell - 1\}.\]
  • Type \(G_2\): Dynkin diagram: \(\bullet \Lleftarrow \bullet\) (triple bond). Let \(e = \varepsilon_1 + \varepsilon_2 + \varepsilon_3 \in \QQ^3\), then: \[V = \{v \in \QQ^3 : (v, e) = 0\},\quad \Phi = \{\varepsilon_i - \varepsilon_j : i \neq j\} \cup \{\pm(3\varepsilon_i - e) : 1 \le i \le 3\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_1 - \varepsilon_2,\; e - 3\varepsilon_1\}.\]
  • Type \(F_4\): Dynkin diagram: \(\bullet - \bullet \Longrightarrow \bullet - \bullet\) \[V = \QQ^4,\quad \Phi = \{\pm \varepsilon_i : 1 \le i \le 4\} \cup \{\pm \varepsilon_i \pm \varepsilon_j : i \neq j\} \cup \left\{\tfrac{1}{2}(\pm \varepsilon_1 \pm \varepsilon_2 \pm \varepsilon_3 \pm \varepsilon_4)\right\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_2 - \varepsilon_3,\; \varepsilon_3 - \varepsilon_4,\; \varepsilon_4,\; \tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_1 - \varepsilon_2 - \varepsilon_3 - \varepsilon_4)\}.\]
  • Type \(E_n\) (\(n = 6, 7, 8\)): These can all be constructed inside \(E_8\) by taking the span of the appropriate subset of a base, so we just give the root system for \(E_8\). \[V = \QQ^8,\quad \Phi = \{\pm \varepsilon_i \pm \varepsilon_j : i \neq j\} \cup \left\{\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^8 (-1)^{a_i}\varepsilon_i : \sum_{i=1}^8 a_i \in 2\ZZ\right\},\] \[\Delta = \{\varepsilon_1 + \varepsilon_2,\; \varepsilon_{i+1} - \varepsilon_i,\; \tfrac{1}{2}(\varepsilon_1 + \varepsilon_8 - (\varepsilon_2 + \varepsilon_3 + \cdots + \varepsilon_7)) : 1 \le i \le 6\}.\]

Note that the Weyl groups of type \(B_\ell\) and \(C_\ell\) are equal. The reason for the restriction on \(\ell\) in the types \(B\), \(C\), \(D\) is to avoid repetition, e.g. \(B_2\) and \(C_2\) are the same up to relabelling the vertices.

Remark 6.3.8.

I certainly don't expect you to remember the root systems of the exceptional types, but you should be familiar with the ones for type \(A\), \(B\), \(C\) and \(D\). The ones of rank two (i.e. \(A_2\), \(B_2\) and \(G_2\)) are also worth knowing (because for example you can draw them!)

6.3.1 The Classification of Semisimple Lie algebras

Only the statements of the theorems in this section are examinable, but it is important to know these statements!

Remarkably, the classification of semisimple Lie algebras is identical to the classification of root systems: each semisimple Lie algebra decomposes into a direct sum of simple Lie algebras, and it is not hard to show that the root system of a simple Lie algebra is irreducible. Thus to any simple Lie algebra we may attach an irreducible root system. By the conjugacy of Cartan subalgebras (see Remark 5.1.4) this gives a well-defined map from simple Lie algebras to irreducible root systems. Then the following theorem shows that its image classifies simple Lie algebras up to isomorphism.

Theorem 6.3.9.

Let \(\gg_1, \gg_2\) be semisimple Lie algebras with Cartan subalgebras \(\hh_1, \hh_2\) respectively, and suppose now \(k\) is of characteristic zero. Then if the root systems attached to \((\gg_1, \hh_1)\) and \((\gg_2, \hh_2)\) are isomorphic, there is an isomorphism \(\phi \colon \gg_1 \to \gg_2\) taking \(\hh_1\) to \(\hh_2\).

Thus to obtain a classification of simple Lie algebras, it remains to determine which irreducible root systems are associated to a simple Lie algebra. In fact all of them are!

Theorem 6.3.10.

There exists a simple Lie algebra corresponding to each irreducible root system.

Thus Theorem 6.3.9 says each irreducible root system is associated to at most one simple Lie algebra (up to isomorphism) and so is a kind of uniqueness theorem, while Theorem 6.3.10 shows each irreducible root system comes from a simple Lie algebra, so is an existence theorem. Theorem 6.3.9 is not difficult given the machinery we have developed in this course.
