The existence statement is the more substantial result, but we developed enough machinery to see the existence in most cases: the four infinite families \(A\), \(B\), \(C\), \(D\) correspond to the classical Lie algebras \(\spl_{\ell+1}\), \(\so_{2\ell}\), \(\mathfrak{sp}_{2\ell}\) and \(\so_{2\ell+1}\) – their root systems can be computed directly (as you saw in the Problem Sheets). This of course also requires checking that these Lie algebras are simple (or at least semisimple) but this is also straightforward with the theory we have developed.

It then only remains to construct the five “exceptional” simple Lie algebras. This can be done in a variety of ways – given a root system where all the roots are of the same length there is an explicit construction of the associated Lie algebra by forming a basis from the Cartan decomposition (and a choice of base of the root system) and explicitly constructing the Lie bracket by giving the structure constants with respect to this basis (which, remarkably, can be chosen for the basis vectors corresponding to the root subspaces to lie in \(\{0, \pm 1\}\)). This gives in particular a construction of the Lie algebras of type \(E_6\), \(E_7\), \(E_8\) (and also \(A_\ell\) and \(D_\ell\) though we already had a construction of these). The remaining Lie algebras can be found by a technique called “folding” which studies automorphisms of simple Lie algebras, and realises the Lie algebras \(G_2\) and \(F_4\) as fixed-points of an automorphism of \(D_4\) and \(E_6\) respectively.

There is also an alternative, more a posteriori approach to the uniqueness result which avoids showing Cartan subalgebras are all conjugate for a general Lie algebra: one can check that for a classical Lie algebra \(\gg \subset \gl_n\) as above, the Cartan subalgebras are all conjugate by an element of \(\operatorname{Aut}(\gg) \cap \GL_n(k)\). This then shows the assignment of a root system to a classical Lie algebra is unique, so it only remains to check the exceptional Lie algebras. But these all have different dimensions, and the dimension of the Lie algebra is captured by the root system, so we are done.

Appendices

Appendix I. Primary Decomposition

Throughout this section, \(k\) is an algebraically closed field unless the contrary is explicitly stated.

Definition I.1.

If \(V\) is a \(k\)-vector space and \(x \in \End_k(V)\) and \(\lambda \in k\), the generalized eigenspace for \(x\) with eigenvalue \(\lambda\) is \[V_{\lambda, x} = \{v \in V : \exists\, n \ge 0,\; (x - \lambda)^n(v) = 0\}.\] Thus \(V_{\lambda, x} \neq \{0\}\) if and only if \(x\) has an eigenvector \(v \in V \setminus \{0\}\) with eigenvalue \(\lambda\). The set of eigenvalues of \(x\) is called the spectrum of \(x\), denoted \(\sigma(x) = \{\lambda \in k : \dim(V_{\lambda, x}) > 0\}\). The subspaces \(V_{\lambda, x}\) are clearly invariant under the action of \(x\), that is \(x(V_\lambda) \subseteq V_\lambda\).

The following basic result is proved in Part A Linear Algebra. We provide a proof for the sake of completeness.

Proposition I.2.

Let \(x \colon V \to V\) be a linear map. There is a canonical direct sum decomposition \[V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in k} V_{\lambda, x}\] of \(V\) into the generalized eigenspaces of \(x\). Moreover, for each \(\lambda\), the projection \(a_\lambda \colon V \to V_{\lambda, x}\) (with kernel the remaining generalized eigenspaces of \(x\)) can be written as a polynomial in \(x\), and hence preserves any subspace preserved by \(x\).

Proof.

Let \(m_x \in k[t]\) be the minimal polynomial of \(x\). Then if \(\phi \colon k[t] \to \End(V)\) given by \(t \mapsto x\) denotes the natural map, we have \(k[t]/(m_x) \cong \operatorname{im}(\phi) \subseteq \End(V)\). If \(m_x = \prod_{i=1}^k (t - \lambda_i)^{n_i}\) where \(S(x) = \{\lambda_i : 1 \le i \le k\}\) is the spectrum of \(x\), then the Chinese Remainder Theorem and the first isomorphism theorem show that \[\operatorname{im}(\phi) \cong k[t]/(m_x) \cong \bigoplus_{i=1}^k k[t]/(t - \lambda_i)^{n_i}.\] It follows that we may write \(1 \in k[t]/(m_x)\) as \(1 = e_1 + \cdots + e_k\) according to the above decomposition. Now clearly \(e_i e_j = 0\) if \(i \neq j\) and \(e_i^2 = e_i\), so that if \(U_i = \operatorname{im}(e_i)\), then we have \(V = \bigoplus_{1 \le i \le k} U_i\). Moreover, each \(e_i\) can be written as a polynomial in \(x\) by picking any representative in \(k[t]\) of \(e_i\) (thought of as an element of \(k[t]/(m_x)\)). Note in particular this means that each \(U_i\) is invariant under \(\operatorname{im}(\phi)\).

Now the characteristic polynomial of \(x|_{V_{\lambda_i}}\) is clearly just \((t - \lambda_i)^{d_i}\) where \(d_i = \dim(V_{\lambda_i, x})\), and evidently this divides \(\chi_x(t)\) the characteristic polynomial of \(x \in \End(V)\). But since \(V = \bigoplus_{i=1}^k U_i\) we must have \(\chi_x(t) = \prod_{i=1}^k (t - \lambda_i)^{m_i}\), where \(m_i = \dim(U_i)\) and hence \(d_i \le m_i\). Since \(U_i \subseteq V_{\lambda_i, x}\) we also have \(m_i \le d_i\), and hence they must be equal, so \(V_{\lambda_i} = U_i\) as required.

□

Recall that \(\gl_1\) is the unique 1-dimensional Lie algebra (with Lie bracket identically zero). Its irreducible representations are all 1-dimensional and parameterised by \(k\), where if \(\lambda \in k\) then \((k, \lambda)\) is the \(\gl_1\)-representation given by the Lie algebra homomorphism \(m_\lambda \colon \gl_1 \to \gl_1\) given by \(m_\lambda(t) = \lambda \cdot t\). We will write \(\lambda\) for the isomorphism class of irreducible containing \((k, \lambda)\).

Corollary I.3.

Let \((V, \rho)\) be a finite-dimensional representation of \(\gl_1\). Then \(V = \bigoplus_{\lambda \in \Psi_V} V_\lambda\) where \(\Psi_V\) is the set of composition factors of \(V\) and \(V_\lambda\) denotes the \(\lambda\)-isotypic subrepresentation of \(V\), that is, the largest subrepresentation of \(V\) whose only composition factor is \(\lambda\).

Proof.

The equivalence of \(\gl_1\)-representations with the set of pairs \((V, T)\) of a vector space \(V\) together with a linear endomorphism \(T \colon V \to V\) together with Proposition I.2 applied to the linear map \(T\) yields the result.

□

The next Lemma is included for completeness – it readily implies that the coefficients of the characteristic polynomial \(\chi_a\) of an element \(a \in A\) of a subspace \(A \subseteq \gl_V\) are polynomial functions of the coordinates of \(a\) given by taking a basis of the subspace. This is used in the proof of the existence of Cartan subalgebras.

Lemma I.4.

Suppose that \(V\) and \(A\) are finite-dimensional vector spaces, \(\varphi \colon A \to \End(V)\) is a linear map, and \(\{a_1, a_2, \ldots, a_k\}\) is a basis of \(A\). Let \[\chi_a(t) = \sum_{i=0}^d c_i(a)\,t^i \in k[t]\] be the characteristic polynomial of \(\varphi(a) \in A\). Then if we write \(a = \sum_{i=1}^k x_i a_i\), the coefficients \(c_i(a)\) (\(1 \le i \le d\)) are polynomials in \(k[x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_k]\).

Proof.

Pick a basis of \(V\) so that we may identify \(\End(V)\) with \(\Mat_n(k)\) the space of \(n \times n\) matrices. Then each \(\varphi(a_i)\) is a matrix \((a_{jk}^i)_{1 \le j, k \le n}\), and if \(a = \sum_{i=1}^k x_i a_i\), we have \[\chi_a(t) = \det\left(tI_n - \sum_{i=1}^k x_i \varphi(a_i)\right),\] which from the formula for the determinant clearly expands to give a polynomial in the \(x_i\) and \(t\), which yields the result.

□

I.1 Remark on matrices

An abstract root system can be encoded in what is called a “Cartan matrix” – an array indexed by pairs of simple roots \((\alpha, \beta) \in \Delta^2\). Although we tend to order bases of a vector space when we compute with linear maps using matrices, those computations do not rely on the order we chose: If \(\alpha \colon V \to W\) is a linear map and \(B_1\) and \(B_2\) are bases of \(V\) and \(W\), then for \(b_1 \in B_1\), we have \(\alpha(b_1) = \sum_{b_2 \in B_2} a_{b_2, b_1} b_2\) for unique scalars \(a_{b_2, b_1}\). These assemble to give a function \(a \colon B_2 \times B_1 \to k\), \((b_2, b_1) \mapsto a_{b_2, b_1}\) or equivalently, a \(B_2 \times B_1\) array of scalars. This array encodes \(\alpha\) without requiring any order to be imposed on either basis: if \(v \in V\) then \(v = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} x_{b_1} b_1\), and \[\alpha(v) = \sum_{b_2 \in B_2} y_{b_2} b_2, \quad \text{where } y_{b_2} = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} a_{b_2, b_1} x_{b_1}.\] Note that such arrays can be composed: if \(\beta \colon W \to U\) and \(B_3\) is a basis of \(U\) then if \(\beta(b_2) = \sum_{b_3 \in B_3} c_{b_3, b_2} b_3\), then \(\beta \circ \alpha\) is represented by the \(B_3 \times B_1\) array \((d_{b_3, b_1})\) where \(d_{b_3, b_1} = \sum_{b_2 \in B_2} c_{b_3, b_2} a_{b_2, b_1}\). In other words, matrix algebra does not require the rows/columns of a matrix to be ordered, but rather that they should be appropriately labelled. As a \(\Delta \times \Delta\) array of integers, the “Cartan matrix” is independent of the base chosen, in that, if \(\Delta\) and \(\Delta'\) are two bases of a root system, then there is a bijection \(b \colon \Delta \to \Delta'\) such that \(\langle \alpha, \beta \rangle = \langle b(\alpha), b(\beta) \rangle\).

Appendix II. Tensor Products

II.1 Definition and construction

Tensor products were studied in Part B, Introduction to Representation Theory. We review their basic properties here.

Definition II.1.

If \(X\) and \(Y\) are sets we write \(Y^X\) for the set of all functions from \(X\) to \(Y\). If \(Y = k\) is a field, and \(V\) a \(k\)-vector space, then \(V^X\) is a \(k\)-vector space under pointwise addition and scalar multiplication.

For any \(x \in X\) let \(\delta_x \in k^X\) be the function given by \(\delta_x(y) = 1\) if \(x = y\) and \(0\) otherwise. We define \(k_X\) to be \[k_X = \operatorname{span}_k\{\delta_x : x \in X\}.\] Equivalently, \(k_X\) is the space of \(k\)-valued functions on \(X\) which vanish outside of a finite subset of \(X\). Note that the set \(\{\delta_x : x \in X\}\) is clearly linearly independent in \(k_X\) and so they form a basis of \(k_X\) and hence we have \(\dim(k_X) = |X|\) where \(|X|\) denotes the cardinality of \(X\). If \(f \in k_X\), then \(\sum_{x \in X} f(x) \cdot \delta_x\) and we will often write \(\sum_{x \in X} f(x) \cdot x\), replacing \(\delta_x\) with \(x\). As a result, \(k_X\) is usually referred to as the space of (finite) “formal linear combinations of elements of \(X\)”.

Definition II.2.

If \(V_1, V_2, \ldots, V_k\) and \(U\) are vector spaces over a field \(k\), let \[\mathcal{M}(V_1, \ldots, V_k; U) = \{\theta \colon V_1 \times \cdots \times V_k \to U : \theta \text{ is } k\text{-linear}\}\] be the vector space of all \(k\)-(multi-)linear maps on \(V_1 \times \cdots \times V_k\) taking values in a vector space \(U\). Here we say that a function \(\theta \colon V_1 \times V_2 \times \cdots \times V_k \to U\) is \(k\)-linear if it is linear in each component separately, that is, if for any \(k\)-tuples of vectors \((v_i)_{1 \le i \le k}\) and \((u_j)_{1 \le j \le k} \in V_1 \times \cdots \times V_k\) and any \(\lambda \in k\), we have for each \(i \in \{1, 2, \ldots, k\}\), \[\theta(v_1, \ldots, \lambda \cdot v_i + u_i, \ldots, v_k) = \lambda \cdot \theta(v_1, \ldots, v_i, \ldots, v_k) + \theta(v_1, \ldots, u_i, \ldots, v_k).\]

Pick a basis \(B_i\) of \(V_i\) for each \(i\) (\(1 \le i \le k\)), and let \(B_i^*\) denote the corresponding dual basis of \(V_i^*\). If \(b \in B_i\), let \(\delta_b\) denote the corresponding element of the dual basis \(B_i^*\), so that \(B_i^* = \{\delta_b : b \in B_i\}\). Let \[\mathbf{B} = B_1 \times B_2 \times \cdots \times B_k.\]

Proposition II.3.

In the notation given above, the restriction to \(\mathbf{B}\) gives an isomorphism \[r_\mathbf{B} \colon \mathcal{M}(V_1, \ldots, V_k; U) \to U^\mathbf{B}\] from the space of all \(k\)-multilinear maps taking values in \(U\) to the space of all \(U\)-valued functions on \(\mathbf{B}\). Indeed \(r_\mathbf{B}\) has inverse given explicitly by \[\mathcal{F}_\mathbf{B}(f)(v_1, \ldots, v_k) = \sum_{\mathbf{b} = (b_1, \ldots, b_k) \in \mathbf{B}} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdots \delta_{b_k}(v_k)\,f(\mathbf{b}).\] Note that since, for each \(i \in \{1, \ldots, k\}\) we have \(v_i = \sum_{b \in B_i} \delta_b(v_i) \cdot b\), \(\delta_b(v_i) \neq 0\) for only finitely many \(b \in B_i\), hence the sum on the right-hand side has only finitely many nonzero terms.

Proof.

First note that if \(\mathbf{b} = (b_1, \ldots, b_k) \in \mathbf{B}\) then the product \(\delta_\mathbf{b} = \delta_{b_1} \cdot \delta_{b_2} \cdots \delta_{b_k}\) is a \(k\)-linear map (since multiplication distributes over addition). Moreover, it is clear that \(\delta_\mathbf{b}(\mathbf{b}') = \delta_{\mathbf{b}, \mathbf{b}'}\) (that is, is zero unless \(\mathbf{b} = \mathbf{b}'\) in which case it is equal to 1), it is immediate that \(r_\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{F}_\mathbf{B}(f)) = f\), so we must show that \(\mathcal{F}_\mathbf{B}(r_\mathbf{B}(\theta)) = \theta\) for any \(\theta \in \mathcal{M}(V_1, \ldots, V_k; U)\). Explicitly, we must show that \[\theta = \sum_{\mathbf{b} \in B_1 \times \cdots \times B_k} \delta_\mathbf{b}\,\theta(\mathbf{b}). \tag{II.1}\] Indeed applying \(\theta\) to a \(k\)-tuple \(\mathbf{b} \in B_1 \times \cdots \times B_k\), we see that the coefficients on the right-hand side are uniquely determined, so it remains to show the products \(\delta_\mathbf{b}\) of dual basis vectors do indeed span.

The case \(k = 1\) is simply the standard argument that the functions \(\{\delta_{b_1}\}_{b_1 \in B_1}\) are indeed a basis of \(V_1^*\): if \(v_1 \in V_1\) then we may write \(v_1 = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \lambda_{b_1} b_1\) for unique scalars \(\lambda_{b_1} \in k\). By the definition of the functions \(\delta_{b_1}\), it then follows that \(\delta_{b_1}(v_1) = \lambda_{b_1}\), so that \(v_1 = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdot b_1\). Applying \(\theta\) gives \(\theta(v_1) = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdot \theta(b_1)\). But as this holds for all \(v_1 \in V_1\), it follows that \(\theta = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \theta(b_1) \cdot \delta_{b_1}\), as required.

The general case then follows by an easy induction: Indeed for any \(k\)-tuple of vectors \((v_i)_{1 \le i \le k}\) with \(v_i \in V_i\), using the case \(k = 1\), we may write \(v_1 = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdot b_1\). But then if \(\theta\) is \(k\)-linear we have \[\theta(v_1, \ldots, v_k) = \theta\!\left(\sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdot b_1,\; v_2, \ldots, v_k\right) = \sum_{b_1 \in B_1} \delta_{b_1}(v_1) \cdot \theta(b_1, v_2, \ldots, v_k).\] But for each \(b_1 \in B_1\), the map \((v_i)_{2 \le i \le k} \mapsto \theta(b_1, v_2, \ldots, v_k)\) is a \((k-1)\)-linear map from \(V_2 \times \cdots \times V_k\) to \(k\), hence the result follows by induction.

□
Remark II.4.

Note that, for \(k = 1\), this says that a linear map is uniquely determined by its values on a basis of \(V_1\), and the statement should be thought of as saying that a \(k\)-linear map is similarly determined “by its values on bases” where the statement of the question gives the precise meaning to the vague phrase in quotation marks.

The previous Proposition gives one way of constructing the tensor product: If \(V\) and \(W\) are \(k\)-vector spaces and we pick bases \(B_V\) and \(B_W\) of \(V\) and \(W\) respectively, then by the Proposition, if we set \(B = B_V \times B_W\), then for any vector space \(U\), we have \[\mathcal{M}(V, W; U) \cong U^B \cong \mathcal{L}_k(k_B, U), \tag{II.2}\] where \(k_B\) is, as above, the vector space with basis \(B\), that is, the space of finite formal linear combinations of elements of \(B\). The first isomorphism above is a direct consequence of the Proposition where we take \(k = 2\) and \(V_1 = V\), \(V_2 = W\), while the second is the case \(k = 1\) of the proposition with \(V_1 = k_B\). Now taking \(U = k_B\) in (II.2), the identity linear map from \(k_B\) to itself corresponds to a bilinear map \(t \colon V \times W \to k_B\).

Lemma II.5.

The bilinear map \(t \colon V \times W \to k_B\) has the universal property, so that the pair \((k_B, t)\) is the tensor product of \(V\) and \(W\).

Proof.

This is essentially established in the previous paragraph: if \(\theta \colon V \times W \to U\) is bilinear, then since \(\theta|_B \colon B \to U\) extends to a linear map \(\tilde{\theta} \colon k_B \to U\). Tracking how the isomorphism of Proposition II.3 identifies \(\theta\) with the linear map \(\tilde{\theta}\) it is easy to see that this can be expressed by means of the bilinear map \(t \colon V \times W \to k_B\) as \(\theta = \tilde{\theta} \circ t\).

□
Remark II.6.

Note that there is a natural isomorphism \(\sigma \colon V \otimes W \cong W \otimes V\) given by \(v \otimes w \mapsto w \otimes v\). If \(V \neq W\), we will normally abuse notation and identify these two spaces and thus write \(V \otimes W = W \otimes V\). If \(V = W\) however, \(\sigma \colon V \otimes V \to V \otimes V\) is an involution on \(V \otimes V\), and more generally, \(V^{\otimes n} = V \otimes \cdots \otimes V\), the tensor product of \(V\) with itself \(n\) times, has an action of \(S_n\) the symmetric group, which permutes the tensor factors: if \(\tau \in S_n\) then \(\tau(v_1 \otimes \cdots \otimes v_n) := v_{\tau(1)} \otimes \cdots \otimes v_{\tau(n)}\).

Example II.7.

If \(V = k\) and \(W\) is an arbitrary \(k\)-vector space, then if \(s \colon k \times W \to W\) is the scalar multiplication map given by \(s(\lambda, w) = \lambda \cdot w\), it is clearly bilinear and it is straightforward to check that it has the universal property so that \(k \otimes W \cong W\).

Note also that \(\mathcal{L}(k, W)\) is canonically isomorphic to \(W\), since if \(\phi \in \mathcal{L}(k, W)\) then \(\phi(1) \in W\), while if \(w \in W\), then \(s_w \colon k \to W\) given by \(s_w(t) = t \cdot w\) is an element of \(\mathcal{L}(k, W)\). Clearly \(s_w(1) = w\), so that \(\phi \mapsto \phi(1)\) and \(w \mapsto s_w\) are inverses of each other.

The construction of the tensor product allows us to replace bilinear maps with linear ones, but one can also relate bilinear maps to “linear maps to spaces of linear maps” – which is really just the process of taking a function of two variables and holding one variable fixed in order to obtain a function of one variable. Formally, we can state:

Lemma II.8.

Let \(V\), \(W\) and \(U\) be vector spaces over a field \(k\). Then we have natural isomorphisms \[\mathcal{L}(V, \mathcal{L}(W, U)) \cong \mathcal{M}(V, W; U) = \mathcal{L}(V \otimes W, U) \cong \mathcal{L}(W, \mathcal{L}(V, U)).\] In particular, if \(U = k\), this shows that \(\mathcal{L}(V, W^*) \cong \mathcal{M}(V, W; k) = (V \otimes W)^*\).

Proof.

Since there is an obvious identification between \(\mathcal{M}(V, W; U) \cong \mathcal{M}(W, V; U)\) it suffices to establish the first isomorphism. But if \(\theta \in \mathcal{L}(V, \mathcal{L}(W, U))\), then we let \(\Psi(\theta)(v, w) = \theta(v)(w)\). The fact that \(\theta\) is linear shows that \(\Psi(\theta)\) is linear in \(V\), while the fact that \(\theta(v)\) lies in \(\mathcal{L}(W, U)\) shows that \(\Psi(\theta)\) is linear in \(W\). Conversely, if \(b \in \mathcal{M}(V, W; U)\), then we may define \(\Upsilon(b)(v) = [w \mapsto b(v, w)]\). The map \(\Upsilon(b) \in \mathcal{L}(W, U)\) because \(b\) is linear in \(W\), while the map \(\Upsilon\) is linear because \(b\) is linear in \(V\). It is clear that \(\Psi\) and \(\Upsilon\) are inverse to each other, thus the first isomorphism is established.

The final claim follows immediately.

□
Definition II.9.

Let \(U, V, W\) be \(k\)-vector spaces. If \(\phi \in \mathcal{L}(U, V)\) and \(\psi \in \mathcal{L}(V, W)\), then the map \((\psi, \phi) \mapsto \psi \circ \phi\) given by composition of functions induces a linear map \[c \colon \mathcal{L}(V, W) \otimes \mathcal{L}(U, V) \to \mathcal{L}(U, W).\] Note that if we take \(V = k\), then \(\mathcal{L}(k, W)\) is canonically isomorphic to \(W\) via the map which sends \(\phi \in \mathcal{L}(k, W)\) to \(\phi(1) \in W\). Since \(\mathcal{L}(U, k)\) is just the dual space \(U^*\) of \(U\), in this case composition yields a map \(\vartheta \colon V^* \otimes W \cong W \otimes V^* \to \mathcal{L}(V, W)\). Specializing again to the case where \(U = W\) one can also compose in the opposite order to obtain a map \(\iota \colon U \otimes U^* \to k\).

Explicitly we have \(\vartheta(f \otimes w) = [u \mapsto f(u) \cdot w]\), (\(\forall\, f \in U^*\), \(w \in W\)) and \(\iota(\lambda \otimes u) = \lambda(u)\) (\(\forall\, \lambda \in U^*\), \(u \in U\)).

Proposition II.10.

Let \(V\) and \(W\) be vector spaces. The natural map \(\vartheta \colon V^* \otimes W \to \mathcal{L}(V, W)\) is injective with image the space \(\mathcal{L}_{fr}(V, W) = \{\alpha \colon V \to W : \dim(\operatorname{im}(\alpha)) < \infty\}\) of linear maps of finite rank. Moreover, when \(V\) is finite-dimensional, if \(\iota \colon V^* \otimes V \to k\) is the contraction map and \(\alpha \in \Hom_k(V, V)\), then \((\iota \circ \theta^{-1})(\alpha) = \tr(\alpha)\).

Proof.

First note that if \(f \in V^*\), \(w \in W\) then \(\theta(f \otimes w) = f \cdot w\) has image contained in \(k \cdot w\), and hence has rank at most 1. It follows that \(\theta(\sum_{k=1}^m f_i \otimes w_i)\) has rank at most \(m\), so that \(\operatorname{im}(\theta) \subseteq \mathcal{L}_{fr}(V, W)\). To see the reverse containment and that \(\theta\) is injective, let \(W_1\) be a finite-dimensional subspace of \(W\). We claim that the restriction of \(\theta\) to \(V^* \otimes W_1\) induces an isomorphism \(\theta_1 \colon V^* \otimes W_1 \to \mathcal{L}(V, W_1)\). Since any \(t \in V^* \otimes W\) lies in \(V^* \otimes W_1\) for some finite-dimensional subspace of \(W\), it follows that \(\ker(\theta) = \{0\}\), that is, \(\theta\) is injective.

Let \(\alpha \in \mathcal{L}(V, W_1)\). Pick a basis \(\{w_1, \ldots, w_k\}\) for \(W_1\), and let \(\{\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_k\}\) be the corresponding dual basis of \(W_1^*\) so that if \(w \in W_1\) then \(w = \sum_{i=1}^k \delta_i(w) \cdot w_i\). But then if \(v \in V\), \(\alpha(v) \in W_1\) so that \(\alpha(v) = \sum_{i=1}^k \delta_i(\alpha(v)) \cdot w_i = \sum_{i=1}^k \alpha^\intercal(\delta_i)(v) \otimes w_i\). Thus if we set \(\vartheta_1(\alpha) = \sum_{i=1}^k \alpha^\intercal(\delta_i) \otimes w_i\), it follows that \(\theta_1(\vartheta_1(\alpha))(v) = \alpha(v)\), for all \(v \in V\). Moreover, if \(f \otimes w \in V^* \otimes W_1\), then \(\theta(f \otimes w)^\intercal(\eta) = \eta(w) \cdot f\) and hence \[\vartheta_1 \circ \theta_1(f \otimes w) = \sum_{i=1}^k \delta_i(w)\,f \otimes w_i = f \otimes \sum_{i=1}^k \delta_i(w)\,w_i = f \otimes w.\] It follows \(\vartheta_1 = \theta_1^{-1}\), and \(\theta_1\) is an isomorphism as claimed.

Finally, consider the contraction map \(\iota \colon V^* \times V \to k\). This is again composition, but now in the opposite order, so that \(v \colon k \to V\) and \(f \colon V \to k\) compose to give \(f(v) \in k\). Applying the above to \(W_1 = V = W\), so that \(\{w_1, \ldots, w_k\}\) is now a basis of \(V\) and \(\{\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_k\}\) the corresponding dual basis of \(V^*\), we see that \[\iota \circ \theta^{-1}(\alpha) = \iota\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^k \alpha^\intercal(\delta_i) \otimes w_i\right) = \sum_{i=1}^k \delta_i(\alpha(w_i)) = \tr(\alpha).\]

□
Remark II.11.

Since we only use the cases where \(V\) and \(W\) are finite dimensional, the reader is welcome to ignore the generality the result is stated in and assume throughout that all vector spaces are finite dimensional. Here one can be a bit more concrete: if \(\{e_1, \ldots, e_n\}\) is a basis of \(V\) and \(\{f_1, \ldots, f_m\}\) is a basis of \(W\), then taking the dual basis \(\{\delta_1, \ldots, \delta_n\}\) of \(V^*\) it is easy to see that the images of \(\delta_i \otimes f_j\) under \(\vartheta\) correspond to the elementary matrices \(E_{ij}\) under the identification of \(\Hom_k(V, W)\) given by the choice of bases for \(V\) and \(W\), hence \(\vartheta\) is an isomorphism.

Remark II.12.

We will usually abuse notation somewhat and write \(\iota \colon V \otimes V^* \to k\) rather than \(\iota \circ \sigma\) where \(\sigma \colon V \otimes V^* \to V^* \otimes V\) interchanges the tensor factors.

II.1.1 Linear maps between tensor products

Let \(\alpha \colon V_1 \to V_2\) and \(\beta \colon W_1 \to W_2\) be linear maps. If \(v \in V_1\), \(w \in W_1\), the map \((v, w) \mapsto \alpha(v) \otimes \beta(w)\) from \(V_1 \times W_1 \to V_2 \otimes W_2\) is bilinear, and so induces a linear map \(\alpha \otimes \beta \in \Hom(V_1 \otimes W_1, V_2 \otimes W_2)\). In fact, the map \((\alpha, \beta) \mapsto \alpha \otimes \beta\) is itself bilinear, and so we even obtain a map \[\Hom(V_1, W_1) \otimes \Hom(V_2, W_2) \to \Hom(V_1 \otimes V_2, W_1 \otimes W_2). \tag{II.3}\] Moreover, it follows immediately from the definitions that (II.3) also respects composition. In more detail, if \(\alpha_2 \colon V_2 \to V_3\) and \(\beta_2 \colon W_2 \to W_3\) are linear maps to any vector spaces \(V_3\) and \(W_3\), then \((\alpha_2 \otimes \beta_2) \circ (\alpha_1 \otimes \beta_1) = (\alpha_2 \circ \alpha_1) \otimes (\beta_2 \circ \beta_1)\). Indeed, if \(v \in V_1\), \(w \in W_1\), then \[(\alpha_2 \otimes \beta_2) \circ (\alpha_1 \otimes \beta_1)(v \otimes w) = (\alpha_2 \otimes \beta_2)(\alpha_1(v) \otimes \beta_1(w)) = (\alpha_2 \circ \alpha_1)(v) \otimes (\beta_2 \circ \beta_1)(w) = (\alpha_2 \circ \alpha_1) \otimes (\beta_2 \circ \beta_1)(v \otimes w).\]

When all the vector spaces \(V_1, V_2, W_1, W_2\) are finite dimensional, the map (II.3) is actually an isomorphism; indeed using Proposition II.10 you can check that \[\Hom(V_1, W_1) \otimes \Hom(V_2, W_2) \cong (V_1^* \otimes W_1) \otimes (V_2^* \otimes W_2) \cong (V_1^* \otimes V_2^*) \otimes (W_1 \otimes W_2) \cong (V_1 \otimes V_2)^* \otimes (W_1 \otimes W_2) \cong \Hom(V_1 \otimes V_2, W_1 \otimes W_2),\] where the second isomorphism simply permutes the second and third tensor factors.

Example II.13.

The map \(\iota_V \colon V^* \otimes V \to k\) arises as an instance of composition, but in fact, at least in the finite-dimensional case, one can express composition of linear maps in terms of the maps \(\iota_V\): Suppose that \(U, V\) and \(W\) are \(k\)-vector spaces and that \(U\) and \(V\) are finite-dimensional. Because \(U\) and \(V\) are finite-dimensional, we may identify \(\mathcal{L}(U, V)\) and \(\mathcal{L}(V, W)\) with \(U^* \otimes V\) and \(V^* \otimes W\) respectively, and \(\mathcal{L}(U, W)\) with \(U^* \otimes W\). Composition of linear maps thus corresponds to a linear map \[c \colon (U^* \otimes V) \otimes (V^* \otimes W) \to U^* \otimes W.\] To identify it, we consider the composition of two rank-1 linear maps \(\alpha \colon U \to V\) and \(\beta \colon V \to W\), say \(\alpha = f \cdot v\), \(\beta = g \cdot w\) (\(f \in U^*\), \(g \in V^*\), \(v \in V\), \(w \in W\)). Then \(\beta \circ \alpha = g(f \cdot v)(w) = g(v) \cdot (f \cdot w)\), and hence \((f \otimes v) \otimes (g \otimes w) \mapsto g(v) \cdot (f \otimes w)\). In other words, \(c = 1_{U^*} \otimes \iota_V \otimes 1_W\).

Remark II.14.

It is sometimes useful to have the following notational convention: Given a tensor product of more than two vector spaces, such as \(U^* \otimes V \otimes V^* \otimes W\), then it can be convenient to write \(\iota_{32}\) for the map which acts via \(\iota\) on the third and second factors (that is swapping the second and third factors, applying \(\iota\) and then repeating the swap) and by the identity on the remaining tensor factors.

II.1.2 Tensor products and duality

Suppose that \(V\) and \(W\) are finite dimensional vector spaces. We wish to understand the relationship between the tensor product of the dual spaces \(V^* \otimes W^*\) and the dual space of the tensor product \((V \otimes W)^*\). But by Proposition II.10, we have an injective map \(\theta \colon V^* \otimes W^* \to \mathcal{L}(V, W^*)\). Now \(\mathcal{L}(V, \mathcal{L}(W, k)) \cong \mathcal{L}(V \otimes W, k) = (V \otimes W)^*\) by Lemma II.8, and so composing we obtain an embedding \(d_{V,W} \colon V^* \otimes W^* \to (V \otimes W)^*\). Explicitly, \[d_{V,W}(\eta \otimes \nu)(v \otimes w) = \eta(v) \cdot \nu(w), \qquad \forall\, \eta \in V^*,\; \nu \in W^*,\; v \in V,\; w \in W,\] i.e. \(d_{V,W}\) is given by multiplication of functions. Moreover, \(d_{V,W}\) is an isomorphism provided \(W\) is finite-dimensional, and hence by symmetry, \(d_{V,W}\) is an isomorphism provided one of \(V\) or \(W\) is finite-dimensional.

Next note that if \(V\) and \(W\) are finite-dimensional, we have \[\mathcal{L}(V, W) \otimes \mathcal{L}(W, V) \cong (V^* \otimes W) \otimes (W^* \otimes V) \cong (V^* \otimes W^*) \otimes (V \otimes W) \to (V \otimes W)^* \otimes (V \otimes W) \to k\] where we use Proposition II.10 in the first line, permutation of the tensor factors in the second and the contraction \(\iota_{V \otimes W}\) in the final line. If \(\alpha = f \cdot w\) and \(\beta = g \cdot v\) are rank-1 linear maps in \(\mathcal{L}(V, W)\) and \(\mathcal{L}(W, V)\) respectively, then the above map sends \((f \cdot w) \otimes (g \cdot v) \to f(v)\,g(w) = \tr_W(\alpha \circ \beta) = \tr_V(\beta \circ \alpha)\), and since rank-1 maps span \(\mathcal{L}(V, W)\) provided \(W\) is finite-dimensional, it follows that the trace pairing \(t(\alpha, \beta) := \tr(\beta \circ \alpha) = \tr(\alpha \circ \beta)\) is a nondegenerate bilinear pairing between \(\mathcal{L}(V, W)\) and \(\mathcal{L}(W, V)\).

Remark II.15.

Note that the contraction map \(\iota \colon V^* \otimes V \to k\), \(\iota(f \otimes v) = f(v)\) is an element of \((V^* \otimes V)^*\). If \(V\) is finite-dimensional, then \((V^* \otimes V)^* \cong (V^*)^* \otimes V \cong V^* \otimes V \cong V^* \otimes V\), hence we may identify \(\iota\) with an element of \(V \otimes V^*\). It is easy to check that this element corresponds to the identity linear map under the isomorphism \(\theta \colon V^* \otimes V \to \mathcal{L}(V, V)\).

Remark II.16.

The isomorphisms of Lemma II.8 come from a very basic bijection: if \(X\), \(Y\) and \(Z\) are sets and we write \(\operatorname{Map}(X, Y)\) for the set of all functions from \(X\) to \(Y\) then \[\operatorname{Map}(X \times Y, Z) = \operatorname{Map}(X, \operatorname{Map}(Y, Z))\] where the identification is as follows: if \(\theta \colon X \times Y \to Z\) then we identify \(\theta\) with the function \(\vartheta \colon X \to \operatorname{Map}(Y, Z)\), where if we let, for any \(x \in X\), the function \(i_x \colon Y \to X \times Y\) be given by \(i_x(y) = (x, y)\), then \(\vartheta(x) = \theta \circ i_x\). Equivalently, \(\vartheta(x)\) is the function on \(Y\) obtained by restricting \(\theta\) to the copy of \(Y\) indexed by \(x\) in \(X \times Y\). It is trivial to check that the inverse of this map associates to \(\vartheta\) the function \(\theta \colon X \times Y \to Z\) given by \(\theta(x, y) = \vartheta(x)(y)\).

Note that if we write \(Y^X\) for \(\operatorname{Map}(X, Y)\) (so that, for example, \(2^X\) is the set of functions from \(X\) to the 2-element set \(\{0, 1\}\), which is readily identified with the power set of \(X\)) then the above bijection gives a natural identification between \(Z^{X \times Y}\) and \((Z^Y)^X\), that is, it becomes a set-theoretic incarnation of the power law!

II.2 Bilinear forms

Definition II.17.

Let \(V\) be a \(k\)-vector space. A bilinear form on \(V\) is a bilinear map \(B \colon V \times V \to k\), that is, an element of \(\mathcal{M}(V, V; k)\). We will denote the vector space of all bilinear forms on \(V\) as \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\). From the universal property of tensor products, \(\operatorname{Bil}(V) \cong (V \otimes V)^*\).

There is an action of \(S_2 = \{e, \sigma\}\), the symmetric group on two letters, on \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) given by \(\sigma(B)(v, w) = B(w, v)\) (for any \(v, w \in V\)). A form \(B\) is said to be symmetric if \(B = \sigma(B)\), that is if \(B(v, w) = B(w, v)\) for all \(v, w \in V\).

If \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\) satisfies \(B(v, v) = 0\) we say that \(B\) is alternating. By expanding \(0 = B(v + w, v + w)\) we see that \(B(v, w) = -B(w, v)\), or \(\sigma(B) = -B\), that is, \(B\) is skew-symmetric. By our assumption that \(\operatorname{char}(k) \neq 2\), the condition that \(B\) is skew-symmetric implies that \(B\) is alternating and moreover, if we write \(S^2(V)\) for the space of symmetric bilinear forms on \(V\) and \(\Lambda^2(V)\) for the space of alternating bilinear forms on \(V\), then we have \(\operatorname{Bil}(V) = S^2(V) \oplus \Lambda^2(V)\). Indeed \(\sigma\) is an involution, hence its action on \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\) is diagonalisable and its eigenvalues are \(\pm 1\).

We may deal with the symmetric and skew-symmetric cases uniformly (to some extent) by working with a form \(B\) which has the property that \(B(v, w) = \epsilon \cdot B(w, v)\) for all \(v, w \in V\), where \(\epsilon \in \{\pm 1\}\).

Remark II.18.

The natural map of Lemma II.8 \[\Theta \colon \operatorname{Bil}(V) = \mathcal{L}(V \otimes V, k) \to \mathcal{L}(V, \mathcal{L}(V, k)) = \mathcal{L}(V, V^*),\] where if \(b \colon V \times V \to k\) is a bilinear form then its image \(\Theta_b\) under \(\Theta\) is given by \[\Theta_b(v_1)(v_2) = b(v_1, v_2), \qquad \forall\, v_1, v_2 \in V.\] Note that the action of \(\sigma \in S_2\) gives a second isomorphism \({}^1\Theta \colon \operatorname{Bil}(V) \to \mathcal{L}(V, V^*)\), where \({}^1\Theta = \Theta \circ \sigma\), that is, \({}^1\Theta_b(v_1)(v_2) = b(v_2, v_1)\). For symmetric bilinear forms the two maps agree, but for arbitrary bilinear forms they yield different isomorphisms.

Definition II.19.

Given a bilinear form \(B\), we set \[\rad(B) = \rad_L(B) = \ker(\Theta_B) = \{v \in V : B(v, w) = 0,\; \forall\, w \in V\}\] (here the subscript “\(L\)” denotes “left”). Similarly, we set \[\rad_R(B) = \ker({}^1\Theta_B) = \{v \in V : B(w, v) = 0,\; \forall\, w \in V\}.\] If \(B\) is symmetric or alternating, then \(\rad_L(B) = \rad_R(B)\), but this need not be true otherwise. We say that \(B\) is non-degenerate if \(\rad_L(B) = \{0\}\). Note that, even though in general \(\rad_L(B) \neq \rad_R(B)\), it is still the case that \(\rad_L(B) = \{0\}\) if and only if \(\rad_R(B) = \{0\}\).

From now on we will only work with symmetric and alternating bilinear forms. Fix such a form \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\) so that \(\sigma(B) = \epsilon \cdot B\) for some \(\epsilon \in \{\pm 1\}\). Then if \(U\) is a subspace of \(V\), we define \[U^\perp = \{v \in V : B(v, w) = 0,\; \forall\, w \in U\} = \{v \in V : \Theta(B)(v) \in U^0\}.\] When \(B\) is nondegenerate, so that \(\Theta_B\) is an isomorphism, this shows that \(\dim(U^\perp) = \dim(U^0) = \dim(V) - \dim(U)\). The next Lemma shows that this can be refined slightly.

Lemma II.20.

Let \(V\) be a finite-dimensional \(k\)-vector space equipped with a symmetric (or alternating) bilinear form \(B\). Then for any subspace \(U\) of \(V\) we have the following:

  1. \(\dim(U) + \dim(U^\perp) \ge \dim(V)\).
  2. The restriction of \(B\) to \(U\) is nondegenerate if and only if \(V = U \oplus U^\perp\).
Proof.

Let \(r_U \colon V^* \to U^*\) be given by \(f \mapsto f \circ \iota\) where \(\iota \colon U \to V\) is the inclusion map. (Thus \(r_U\) restricts the domain of an element of \(V^*\) from \(V\) to \(U\).) Given \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\) we set \(\phi_B = r_U \circ \Theta_B \colon V \to U^*\), that is \(\phi(v) = \Theta_B(v)|_U\). Clearly \(\ker(\phi) = U^\perp\), while \(\operatorname{im}(\phi) \le U^*\) and hence \(\dim(\operatorname{im}(\phi)) \le \dim(U)\). The inequality in \(i)\) now follows from rank-nullity.

For the second part, note that \(B\) is non-degenerate on \(U\) if and only if \(U \cap U^\perp = \{0\}\). But then the inequality in \(i)\) shows that we must have \(U \oplus U^\perp = V\) for dimension reasons.

□

II.3 Classification of symmetric bilinear forms

There is a natural linear action of \(\GL(V)\) on the space \(\operatorname{Bil}(V)\): if \(g \in \GL(V)\) and \(B \in \operatorname{Bil}(V)\) then we set \(g(B)\) to be the bilinear form given by \[g(B)(v, w) = B(g^{-1}(v), g^{-1}(w)), \qquad (v, w \in V),\] where the inverses ensure that the above equation defines a left action. It is clear the action preserves the subspace of symmetric bilinear forms.

Since we can find an invertible map taking any basis of a vector space to any other basis, the next lemma says that over an algebraically closed field there is only one nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form up to the action of \(\GL(V)\), that is, when \(k\) is algebraically closed the nondegenerate symmetric bilinear forms are a single orbit for the action of \(\GL(V)\).

Lemma II.21.

Let \(V\) be a \(k\)-vector space equipped with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form \(B\). Then if \(\operatorname{char}(k) \neq 2\), there is an orthonormal basis of \(V\), i.e. a basis \(\{v_1, \ldots, v_n\}\) of \(V\) such that \(B(v_i, v_j) = \delta_{ij}\).

Proof.

We use induction on \(\dim(V)\). The identity \[B(v, w) = \frac{1}{2}\bigl(B(v + w, v + w) - B(v, v) - B(w, w)\bigr)\] shows that if \(B \neq 0\) we may find a vector \(v \in V\) such that \(B(v, v) \neq 0\). Rescaling by a choice of square root of \(B(v, v)\) (which is possible since \(k\) is algebraically closed) we may assume that \(B(v, v) = 1\). But if \(L = k \cdot v\) then since \(B|_L\) is nondegenerate, the previous lemma shows that \(V = L \oplus L^\perp\), and if \(B\) is nondegenerate on \(V\) it must also be so on \(L^\perp\). But \(\dim(L^\perp) = \dim(V) - 1\), and so \(L^\perp\) has an orthonormal basis \(\{v_1, \ldots, v_{n-1}\}\). Setting \(v = v_n\), it then follows \(\{v_1, \ldots, v_n\}\) is an orthonormal basis of \(V\) as required.

□
Remark II.22.

Over the real numbers, for example, there is more than one orbit of nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, but the above proof can be modified to give a classification and it turns out that there are \(\dim(V) + 1\) orbits (“Sylvester's law of inertia”).

One can also classify alternating forms using essentially the same strategy, except that if \(B\) is a non-zero alternating form on a vector space \(V\), one shows that it contains a two-dimensional space \(H\) on which \(B\) is nondegenerate. Then we can choose a basis \(\{e, f\}\) of \(V\) with \(B(e, f) = 1 = -B(f, e)\), and then since \(V = H \oplus H^\perp\) one can apply induction. Moreover, in the alternating case, the classification holds over any field \(k\) where \(\operatorname{char}(k) \neq 2\).
